Citizenship may be lost as well as gained. A person convicted of a serious crime and put in prison loses his citizenship rights and in some states may not regain them without a special act of the state legislature. Most people, however, who lose citizenship

do so by voluntarily deciding to transfer their citizenship from the country in which they were born to some other country. 

Our government has always maintained that the right to transfer citizenship should be perfectly free. Expatriation means giving up allegiance to the country of one's birth; naturalization is the acquiring of citizenship in another country. Unless the two go together, there might be two countries claiming a person as a citizen. Controversies over such dual citizenship used to be fairly common, but now most countries take the same view that our own country takes in regard to transferring citizenship.¹ 

May Americans voluntarily give up their American citizenship? Is it wrong for them to do so? Why should people who intend to live permanently in a country become citizens of that country? Does a government have the right to send aliens out of the country? Young people are sometimes addressed as future citizens of the United 

States. Is such a term correct? Who might properly be called future citizens? 

5. How May an Alien Become a Citizen? Let us observe what must be done by an alien who wishes to be naturalized in the 

United States. (1) The declaration of intention or "first papers." — Any foreignborn resident of the country who is eighteen years old or more may go before a state or federal court which keeps permanent records and file a formal paper stating his desire to give up his allegiance to the government under which he was born and to become a citizen of the United States. In this "declaration of intention" he makes several statements about his family relations, the time he came to the United States, and other matters. He must declare that he is not an anarchist one who is opposed to all organized government. He may make this declaration of intention as soon as he wishes after coming to the United States. (2) The granting of citizenship or "second papers." This cannot happen until the applicant for citizenship has lived five years in the United States. At least two years, but not more than seven, must have elapsed since his declaration of intention. Then he must bring to court two citizens who will testify to his residence in this country and his fitness to be a citizen. He must show that he can read and 

1 If a person who has become naturalized in the United States goes back to his former country and stays there more than two years, or lives in some other country five years, our laws assume that he has given up his American citizenship. 

If he does not want this to happen, he must in some way make the fact known to our government.

write English and that he knows something about our government and history. If the court is satisfied with the applicant's achievement, he is allowed to take the oath of allegiance to the United States. This process gives him his certificate of naturalization and almost every privilege that a native-born citizen has except that of becoming President or Vice President. 

Women must be naturalized in the same way that men are, with one exception. A foreign-born married woman may be naturalized after living here one year, providing her husband is a citizen, and under those circumstances she will not have to declare her intention in advance. Any one who is eighteen years old or more may apply for citizenship in his own right, regardless of whether his parents have become naturalized or not; children born in this country are 

American citizens, if they remain here, even though their parents never have become naturalized. 

See Project 4, page 12.

Do you think our requirements for naturalization are strict enough? Should it be possible to withdraw naturalization from one who has already received it?

A married woman's citizenship used to depend upon that of her husband. What do you think about such a practice? Suppose a foreigner living here said to you, "I am not going to get naturalized. 

What good will it do me?" What arguments might you use in talking with him?

Is it possible for a child to be born in the United States and still not be an American citizen? Is it possible for a person to be a citizen of no country at all?

An American missionary's daughter, born in India, came back to the United States in 1923 for her college education, and then returned in 1928 to take up missionary work in India. Is she an American citizen?

A young man whose parents at the time he was born ran a Chinese laundry in Chicago accepted a business position in Shanghai as the representative of an American firm. Of what nationality was his son, born in Shanghai? 

6. How Should I Act as a Good American Citizen? One of the fundamental virtues that are usually emphasized in schools, churches, and homes, is loyalty. Surely, if I live in a country whose protection and privileges I enjoy, it is but the simplest obligation to recognize the benefits I receive by being loyal to its government. A good American citizen is loyal and patriotic.

That does not mean that I should always approve of everything that is done by public officers. Ignorant and even wicked men

sometimes get elected to high positions. True loyalty would demand that I try to get such people out of office and put good people in. If I believe that some policy of our government is not wise, my duty is, by lawful and peaceful means, to try to bring about a change in that policy. A good American citizen is courageous.

Ewing Galloway

GOOD AMERICAN CITIZENS 

These young citizens are trying to make themselves intelligent by taking advantage of their opportunities for reading and study. The truck in this picture is sent out from the county library at Greenville, South Carolina, into country districts and mill towns.

Under no circumstances do I have the right to do anything that will harm my own country. On the other hand, I do not need to think unkindly about other countries in order to be patriotic toward my own. A good American citizen is fair.

I owe to my government a true understanding of its organization, its work, and its problems. Are the Pledge of Allegiance to our flag and our patriotic songs just words or tunes, or is there some thought and spirit and feeling that makes those words and that music really mean something? A good American citizen is intelligent.

Make a list of five things you have seen or heard which indicate good citizenship. What do you think of the slogan, "My country, right or wrong"? Try writing the first and last stanzas of "America" in perfectly clear prose. 

What definite suggestions can you offer that will help to make young American citizens intelligent citizens? 

A good American citizen knows his country's history, appreciates what his government does for him, understands the organization of that government, has rights that others cannot claim, may have been born a citizen or may have become one through his own free choice, is loyal, courageous, fair, and intelligent. 

Here is William Tyler Page's statement of what he thinks an 

American citizen should believe. Do you understand it all? Do you accept it as far as you do understand it?

THE AMERICAN'S CREED 

I believe in the United States of America as a government of the people, by the people, for the people; whose just powers are derived from the consent of the governed; a democracy in a republic; a sovereign nation of many sovereign states; a perfect union, one and inseparable; established upon those principles of freedom, equality, justice, and humanity for which American patriots sacrificed their lives and fortunes.

I therefore believe it is my duty to my country to love it; to support its Constitution; to obey its laws; to respect its flag; and to defend it against all enemies. Constitution

INTERESTING PROJECTS YOU MAY LIKE TO UNDERTAKE 

1. Do you know how to find promptly information you are looking for in a book? Where in a book are you likely to find the preface, table of contents, index, and other parts of it? If you do not know, why don't you give yourself a little practice right now? 

2. A daily project. — Let some member of the class be prepared each day to summarize, in not over three minutes, the important news which he reads in the morning paper. Sometimes it may be better to have a news bulletin placed on the blackboard each day instead of having the report given orally. 

3. Dramatize the various steps by which some particular person of foreign birth becomes a citizen. You will find helpful suggestions for this in Training in Citizenship, by Hatch. 

4. Make a chart or diagram showing an imaginary family coming to the 

United States from some other country and indicate with dates what each member of the family will have to do in order to receive American citizenship. Let the family include some children. American Citizenship Charts, No. 20, will be helpful in doing this. 

5. Draw a cartoon illustrating the different relations of citizens and aliens toward our government. 

6. A term project. - Keep an original notebook. In it put outlines, diagrams, and tables, suggested in this book, or otherwise related to the work of the term; reports of committees of which you are a member; notes on matters of interest or importance that you may get from your own investigation or which are suggested by your teacher; and pictures, clippings, and the like which illustrate topics taken up in your study. BUT DON'T EVER CUT A 

GOOD BOOK TO GET PICTURES OR CLIPPINGS! Newspapers, magazines, lec tures, radio addresses, and the like may all serve as sources of material to include in your notebook. 

7. A class project. See that your class bulletin board constantly displays pictures, news items, and other material of interest to the class and related to the work of the term. If your classroom has no bulletin board, you can easily make one out of soft wood or heavy cardboard. In some classes a bulletin committee, serving for one or two weeks at a time, is made responsible for the care of the bulletin board; but every member of the class should bring in appropriate material at any time. Anything that will be useful later should be catalogued and preserved. 

8. Give floor talks on the introductory questions in the early part of the chapter or on the list of citizenship qualities on page 11.

CHAPTER II COMMUNITIES AND COMMUNITY SPIRIT 

Let me live in a house by the side of the road 

And be a friend to man. - Foss Now that we understand our place as good American citizens, let us apply the thought of citizenship to our relations with our neighbors in city, village, or country district. This will show how important it is to us that there are other people besides ourselves. We could not last very long if we refused to have anything to do with any one else or to use anything that any one else had helped to produce. Community life, from whatever viewpoint we look at it, is tremendously important to us.

Can you enumerate ways in which we depend upon others? 

In how many kinds of group life do we have a part, and what do these groups do for one another? 

Why and how have local communities been established? 

Are all communities alike? If not, what differences may we observe among local communities?

What do we mean by community spirit, and how may it be displayed? 

7. Living and Working Together. - Coöperation makes possible everything we do today. You yourself can give example after example of this fact from your school athletics. There we call it teamwork. When a girl proudly says, "I made this dress all by myself," she really is not so independent as she seems. She depended on the planter who grew the cotton, the laborers who picked and cleaned it, the factory that made the goods, the rail. road that brought the goods to the city she lives in, the man who sold it to her, the sewing machine or needles she used, her scissors, thread, pattern, and innumerable other things which aided her in the production of that dress. 

Thomas A. Edison, genius though he was, would have been almost helpless if he had had to mine or make everything that he 14

used in working out his great inventions. How, then, could we ordinary mortals hope to exist solely by ourselves and by our own efforts? 66 One man alone may be easily overcome by his enemies. 

United we stand, divided we fall." By their joint efforts men may achieve something far greater than singly they could hope to

The citizens of Portland, Oregon, are proud that they can look from their homes or offices toward such a scene of grandeur. 66 accomplish. A new spirit gets hold of them. I Non sibi sed aliis or, if you don't know Latin

Not for one's self, but for others." Perhaps you like better the slogan of the "Three 

Musketeers, ""All for one and one for all."

Did you ever know a person who lived the life of a hermit? Of how much use was he to anybody? How comfortable was his own life? Mention six occasions today when you depended upon others to do something for your comfort or advantage. Mention six occasions when other people depended upon you for some service. If you cannot mention them, why not? Show how coöperation is necessary for the success of each of the following: a party, a church, a school,

a play. Does failure to do your work in class harm anybody but yourself? Would an all-American football team without practice be likely to win from a well-trained team from some one institution? 

8. Groups and Communities to Which We Belong. We have always needed community life to protect, serve, and help one another. By a community we mean a group of people occupying a definite area and bound together by common interests and common customs or laws. In a very true sense a family, a school, a church, a factory, or a nation may be a community. There can be social communities, educational communities, religious communities, and industrial communities. Sometimes we use the word institution to mean a particular organization that has been formed for a special purpose, such as the school to provide education, or the church to promote right living and encourage the worship of God.

The neighborhood, village, or city in which we live is the kind of community we most often have in mind when we use the word. 

Such a community may include a considerable number of the other, institutions. To distinguish it from other groups, we may call it a local community. If it is organized with a definite government of its own, it becomes a political community too. 

Make a list of all the organizations with which you and the other members of your family are connected. How many of these are permanent, and how many are established for only a short time? 

May we rightly call the United States or the world a community?

For your notebook fill in the following chart, mentioning at least two ways in which each agency serves each of the others.

The New England village centered around the church or the general store. The farmers who cleared and plowed the land preferred not to go so far away that they could not hear the church bell. All over New England today are country villages, which

As people went West, the intersection of two rivers or roads was likely to become the location of a new town. Some communities grew with a rush. Remember how, in Oklahoma, when that rich territory was taken from the Indians and thrown open to settlement by white people, settlements of shacks or tents were formed over night. Some of them really did become flourishing cities. "Boom towns," which rose suddenly when oil, gold, or silver was

struck, might last only a few years if Nature's bounties in that section were easily exhausted. 

Tremendous changes take place in some of these communities after a few years. 66 The oldest house in town" may today be in one of its poorest sections. Where once the "best families" lived we may find a tenement district or even a factory. Change is the only thing that we can take for granted in a community which has actually grown. 

THE HEART OF OLD NEW ORLEANS 

This square, known as the Place d'Armes, was laid out by the French founder of the city in 1718. Within it you see the Jackson Monument, a cathedral, and some other notable buildings.

If the surrounding country, or "hinterland," of which a settlement may become the business center, is rich in resources, the place will grow much more rapidly than one that is not so favorably located. Besides, a community which strives from the beginning to have good schools, attractive streets, and safe and substantial buildings, is likely to thrive much better than one whose people think only of getting rich quickly.

Can communities grow too fast? Is it a reflection on a community if it does not grow rapidly? Why and how was your own community started? Can you find examples in your own state of communities such as are mentioned in this section?

Show on this map the rivers, mountains, and similar natural features and the location of other important communities. 

10. Kinds of Local Communities. Communities are of many sizes and of many types. The United States Bureau of the Census counts as urban communities all incorporated places with 2500

Smaller or less thickly settled places than these are called rural communities. 

Rural communities seldom have houses close to one another. 

They may, indeed, be just a collection of farms. They may be little villages with one main street and one store where you can buy almost anything from a needle to a set of furniture.

In urban communities the houses are set closer together.. 

Instead of the quiet of rural communities there may be noise and dirt and bustle. There has been a steady tendency for urban communities to increase in importance. When the first national census was taken in 1790, only 3.4% of the people were urban. No town then had as many people as Jamestown, New York, or Jackson, Mississippi, has today. In 1930, however, over 56% of the 

Pe 106th Photo Section, Air Corps, Roberts Field, Birmingham, Alabama

ALABAMA'S CHIEF CITY 

Birmingham has grown rapidly in the last half century. Its office buildings indicate its comparative newness, and the lay-out of its streets suggests planning in advance. people were living in urban communities. The census of 1810 was the first to show any city in this country with a population of 100,000. In 1880 there were nineteen such cities. In 1930 there were ninety-three, not counting two in our island territories. 

We may classify communities with reference to other char-acteristics than population. Some are distinctly residential, such as Pasadena, East Orange, and Oak Park. They have few largestores and few or no factories. City people are more and more preferring to live outside the business section of their city. Man-

hattan Island actually lost 400,000 population between 1920 and 

1930, though New York City as a whole gained 1,300,000.

Suburban communities also are often residential. They are usually on the outskirts of a city, but near enough so that their members can engage in business in the city. Their inhabitants often brag that they have all the beauty and comfort of outdoor country life, with all the convenience and luxury of the city. 

A CITY BOTH ANCIENT AND MODERN 

Here is the attractive city of Istanbul. Perhaps you know it as Constantinople. 

Its history goes back several hundred years B.C., but under the enlightened government of the new Turkish Republic it has almost every feature of a modern metropolis.

Some communities are distinctly industrial, such as Homestead and Gary. Factories are the life of the place. The inhabitants usually find employment in these factories. There are mining towns, too, such as Butte, and railroad division points, such as Altoona.

Still other communities, particularly large ones, become centers of trade or general industry - New York and Chicago, for example, or Cleveland. Sometimes a community becomes large simply because it is the business center of an extensive agricultural neighborhood. In some ways Indianapolis, El Paso, and Los 

Angeles are examples of such a situation.

Cities are not a modern institution. Think of the great centers of ancient civilization - Babylon, Tyre, Athens, Alexandria, and Rome. Even though you cannot find the ruins of some of these cities today, the shadow of their former glory looms large. 

Medieval times saw the decline of many cities and the growth of others. Constantinople (now Istanbul) was for centuries a center of light in the eastern Mediterranean world. London and Paris began to grow and have never stopped. When trade spread all over the world, other cities grew up. When factories became common and machines gave employment to millions of city workers, who in turn required the services of many other workers to supply their needs, still more cities appeared. Berlin, Buenos Aires, and

Tokyo show that great cities are not limited to any one part of the world and prove how rapidly cities have grown in recent years. 

Which name has the more definite meaning to you: Paris or 

France, London or England, Berlin or Germany, New York or the

United States? Is it good to have one city so prominent as to overshadow all the rest of a state or country? 

How would you classify your own community? What sort of communities do these words suggest to you - town, village, hamlet, crossroads? How would you classify Atlantic City? Washington, D. C.? St. Louis? What kind of community do you live in? Is it that kind of community by choice or accident? Does your family live there by choice or by accident? 

11. How People Live in Different Communities. Our cities are our centers of business and trade. Here are the great newspapers upon which we depend largely for our knowledge of what goes on in the world; here are supposed to be our best schools and our largest universities. These places cannot feed our people, but they can do almost everything else. The city has been called "the hope of America."

But almost every large city has sections which seem totally unfit for decent people to live in. Most of our large cities have at some time suffered from bad politics. One citizen may be entirely lost in the mass of thousands or even millions of people who compose its population. The large cities are usually the headquarters of the forces that work for evil in our country. If the city is the hope of America, we shall have to watch for these things.

How much do city people know about their neighbors? Is this condition good or bad? Do city people or country people waste more time in getting to and from their places of work? By what right may any one call the city "the hope of America"? In what respects is the growth of cities good for farmers? Where do we find the extremes of social differences, and where are people most nearly on the same footing socially city, small town, rural district? 

A great deal has been said and written about the rural districts. 

The so-called funny men in the newspapers and on the stage laugh at the farmer and his supposedly crude ways, though the farmer 

Courtesy Post Office Department WHAT A RURAL MAIL CARRIER MAY HAVE TO HANDLE 

This is said to be the average load on a mail route from Crescent City, California, to Brookings, Oregon. Does it suggest that the people of that district make extensive use of the mails? finds the city man's antics and mistakes just as funny. When cities have been growing fast and constantly obtaining more conveniences and luxuries, the farmer has frequently failed to get them. Since the average farmer is not rich and does not care to pay high taxes, he has been handicapped by poor roads and poor schools. To many, farming seems a hard and lonely life. Amusements may in some places be few and far between. But rural life has always had advantages too. Outdoor life and work have more than made up in many cases for the inconveniences and insanitary conditions in some farm homes. If the farmer's neighbors are far away, they at least do not disturb him or keep him awake nights. There must have been something substantial about rural life, else eighty per cent of America's financial leaders would not have come from country districts.

Today many a farm district has almost all the comforts that any city can boast. Paved highways make it easy for farmers and their Fords to go into "town" whenever they need to buy or sell something. Telephones may be used even more by the farmer and his family than by the family in the city. Rural free delivery of mail comes close to the farmer's door daily. The consolidated

school takes the farm boy and girl through a good high school course. With his radio the farmer hears the same concerts as the city man. If farm boys and girls can realize that life on the farm has freedom and opportunity that the inhabitant of the crowded city can never enjoy, there will be fewer young people seeking fame and fortune in the city- - and not finding it. 

If you were to live on a farm, what season do you imagine you would enjoy best? To what extent does the city depend upon the farm? the farm upon the city?

THE MAIN STREET IN A RURAL COUNTY SEAT 

Somerset, Pennsylvania, is not a large place, but it has many attractive features.

humdrum kind. Hardly anybody cared to be any better than he had been, or do much except work, gossip, and sleep. Some towns are that way. The people are shiftless. A dirty barber shop or pool room or the street corner is the only place for boys to spend their spare time.

Yet to think of all small towns as being like Gopher Prairie would be a slander. Some of them are outwardly lovely. Shows and amusements need not come every night in the week, but they can be of good quality when they do come. Everybody knows everybody else, and a general spirit of friendliness and common interest prevails. The schoolhouse and the church may be real social centers, ministering to everybody in the community. Life may never be so fast there as in the city; but if you want solid comfort and reasonable leisure, you may find it in the small town. 

Can you think of different kinds of small towns? Is any particular type of small town more likely to be desirable or undesirable to live in than another? 

12. Community Spirit and My Responsibility for It. "Community spirit" is that invisible something which puts life and progress into the activities of the people. They are willing to work together for their common good. They are "boosters" for their community; they think it is good and want to make it better. 

If all the good people of a community would only be interested in its welfare all the time! 

66 The people of a community should not be constantly looking back to the "good old days" of their ancestors, but it is perfectly right that a town or city that has something to celebrate should do so. Small communities cannot undertake "World Fairs," but sometimes an "old home day or old home week" brings back the memory of childhood days to those who live far away from the old-time scenes and takes them back for a visit. The whole community is stirred and aroused to new life. A town may have a community house which is open for the use of all the members of the community. Pageants, plays, or social events may draw together a large part of the people. 

Sometimes the adoption of a community slogan adds to the people's interest in their community. If people are always apolo-

gizing for their town, others will take them at their own estimate; but if they tell the world they have something worth having, perhaps somebody will believe them. 

Does your community have a community slogan? Mention slogans with which you are familiar. Do they seem to help to advertise the community which has adopted them, or otherwise help to create a better spirit?

Sometimes a forward-looking community decides to undertake a survey. This is a thorough investigation into the conditions

Far-reaching improvements have sometimes resulted from this sort of thing. 

Is there anything that your community has commemorated or could commemorate? Does it have "old home" days, pageants, a local baseball team, or any other activities that make a large number of people take an interest in the same thing? Do you think there is a proper community spirit in your town or city? Why or why not?

Perhaps you say, "I am only one of 300 people in Smithville" or 

I only one of 300,000 people in Toledo." "Will the community grow or go backward by reason of anything that I do?" You never can tell. 

Certainly, I can see to it that I do not cost the community any more than I contribute to it. I can always be among those who are interested in the progress of the community; and when some movement is started for community improvement, I can do my share. If things go wrong, it shall not be my fault.

An American community is made up of people who live and work together, includes groups and institutions which contribute to the welfare of their members, may be old or new, large or small, may differ considerably from other communities in its interests and activities, may be crowded with people and throbbing with activity, or be given over to the quieter life of the farm or village, depends for its success on the spirit and resolution of its good citizens.

Make a large map showing every place of interest, good or bad, and indicate on it everything possible with reference to the condition of your community. 

2. For each member of the class. Begin a large map of the community in which you live. At first show only the main outlines of the district, such as rivers and hills; as different topics are taken up in our study add also the main highways, railroads, schools, large churches, public buildings, parks, and other notable features of the community. 

3. A continuing class project for the semester or the year. As a practical exercise in community citizenship, let the class organize with a president, vice president, secretary, and such other officers as it desires, and draw up a constitution for itself. Class officers and committees should accept and be faithful to definite responsibilities in connection with the regular work of the class or any special activities it may undertake. Class meetings should be conducted in accordance with the recognized rules of parliamentary practice. 

Teacher and class should decide how much of the regular everyday work of the class should be done through the class organization. 

4. Let each member of the class select some town or city of special interest to him and make a study of the reasons it was founded and why it has developed to be what it is today. 

5. Make a graph showing the percentage of urban and rural population at different censuses. 

6. Draw up a community creed. 

7. A debate: Resolved, that it is better for children to grow up in a rural community than in a large city. 

8. Give floor talks on the introductory questions or on the list of community qualities on page 28.

CHAPTER III THE PEOPLE OF OUR COMMUNITIES 

This is the land where hate should die 

No feuds of faith, no spleen of race, No darkly brooding fear should try

Beneath our flag to find a place. 

MCCARTHY 

We may talk about communities all we please, but if we forget that communities are made up of men, women, and children, our discussion will have no reality. Our country was founded by people who left their homes and traveled thousands of miles to settle in this new world. It is one of the few great powers that number among their inhabitants large groups of people of different races and colors. If we are to have a real understanding of our own country, we need to have some definite facts in mind about our people. 

How are the people of the United States distributed over the country?

From what sources have people come to settle in this country, and where did they settle in the largest numbers? 

What has been our policy in regard to receiving people from other lands?

What effects upon us have been produced by the coming of these people, and how have we treated them? 

What have been the relations among people of different races and colors? Can those relations always be friendly? 

If you 

13. Where Are the People of the United States? have ever taken any long trips by automobile or railroad, you have discovered that you can ride in a car for miles in some places and see on both sides of the road a continuous line of houses, and that you can go into our western states and ride for hundreds of miles at a stretch without seeing more than a few hundred houses. 

A much larger proportion of our people live in the northeastern states than in other quarters of the country. This fact is brought 31

out by the location of what the Census Bureau calls the center of population. You may have tried to balance a loaded tray on your fingers and have noticed that the way things were distributed on the tray determined where your fingers would have to support it. 

So the surface of the country is thought of as balanced on an imaginary point, with the people distributed as they happen to be now. When the first census was taken, this point was on the Danville Lafayette York

HOW THE CENTER OF POPULATION HAS MOVED WEST 

The marked circles show from census to census how the center of population has moved from 1790 to 1930. Isn't it extraordinary how close to the 39th parallel of latitude it has kept all the time? eastern shore of Maryland. It has steadily moved west, and in recent years little south, until in 1930 it was found in Greene County, Indiana; but that point is still considerably to the east and north of the actual center of the country, which is somewhere in Kansas.

In giving statistics on population or other matters we often refer to a square mile. Does that term mean anything to you? Try to picture a region in your neighborhood a mile on each side, and get very definitely into your mind how much territory that "square mile" covers. Then perhaps when we say that the population of 

Rhode Island is 644 per square mile and that of Nevada is of a person per square mile, the contrast may mean something definite. (See Project 2 on page 42.)

To refresh your mind in regard to some of the facts that your study of geography should have taught you, work out Project 1 on page 42.

Can you give reasons why certain states are large or small either in total population or in proportion to area? In how many cases would the same states be both "large" and "small" in both respects? 

14. Sources of the Races and Nationalities among Us. We sometimes use the words "race" and "nation" or "nationality"

as if they meant the same thing. Race signifies a large group of persons who have the same general physical qualities, such as color or features. In that sense we might correctly speak of the white race, the black, the yellow, the red, and the brown. The word nation originally meant, judging by its Latin origin, people related by birth; but today we usually think of a nation as a group of people in the same territory under one government and having many interests in common. In this sense a nation may include people of several races. 

Which of the following can correctly be called a race and which a nation: Irish, Jews, Negroes, Germans, Indians (red), Mexicans, Eskimos?

You know that several European countries had a part in the opening up of North America. Spanish, French, English, Dutch, and Swedes all made settlements of their own. After colonies were actually founded, Welsh, Germans, Irish, and Scotch-Irish were also represented among those who came here to settle. 

When our forefathers issued the Declaration of Independence, however, the great majority of the people were of English ancestry. 

During the Revolution and the great series of wars in Europe a few years later, there was little opportunity for many settlers to come here. This fact gave the United States a chance to develop somewhat easily into a distinct nation by itself. 

Since 1820 the history of immigration to this country can be divided into two periods, that of the "old immigration" from 1820 to 1880, and that of the "new immigration" from 1880 to 1920. 29 1. The old immigration. The "old immigrants came mainly from northern and western Europe - Ireland, Germany, Norway and Sweden, Denmark, and Great Britain. The terrible potato famine in Ireland in 1843 and 1844 drove many thousands of Irish immigrants to our shores. Most of them settled in our eastern cities where they found jobs doing hard manual work. 

Disturbed conditions in Germany about the middle of the nineteenth century caused many Germans to leave their native land. 

They, and the Swedes, Norwegians, and Danes, went largely to the Middle West. Many of them settled in country districts and built up prosperous farms, especially in Wisconsin and Minnesota and other western states.

2. The new immigration. The "new immigrants," who have come to our shores since 1880, were more largely from southern and eastern Europe Italy, Russia, Poland, Hungary, and neighboring countries. This new immigration was much different from the old immigration. A much larger proportion of these people could not read or write, and many of them had never been 

Less than 1% 

10 to 15% 

1 to 5% 

5 to 10% 

15 to 25% 

Over 25% 900 Do

THE FOREIGN-BORN IN THE UNITED STATES 

Can you explain why certain states have so many more than others? Try to find information that will show what nationalities are most numerous in different districts. used to high standards of living. These new immigrants took their turn at the hard manual work in our mines and factories. 

They seldom went on farms, but instead settled in our large cities, where conditions were often already congested. In 1907 over 

1,200,000 people entered in one year enough to fill a city almost as big as Philadelphia was then or Los Angeles is now. 

Which immigrants do you think would be most desirable to have for occupying new lands, the old or the new? for working in mines or factories? Are immigrants likely to follow the same trade or occupation after they get here that they followed in the old country? 

Why do you suppose there are practically no Jews on farms?

About one in nine of our present population was born abroad. 

The parents of those now living here, however, were in at least

one case out of five born in a foreign land. Except for those who have come across the line from Mexico, our foreign-born population is much larger in the northeast section of our country than anywhere else. From New York City alone you could make out of its Irish-born a city as large as Richmond, Virginia; a still larger city out of its German-born or Polish-born; and two others the size of Newark, New Jersey, from its Russian-born and Italian-born.

After working out Project 3 on page 43, list the number of people of different nationalities represented in the parents, grandparents, and great-grandparents of your class. How much can you tell about a person's ancestry from his name? Make a list of some distinctive syllables in family names. Why do foreigners often change their names after they get here? 

15. How We Have Received Immigrants. At first the 

United States welcomed them all. Every one received the "glad hand," so to speak. This was the home of liberty, we boasted, and its doors were open to the oppressed of every land. We needed the immigrants in the building up of our country. At first we had no laws restricting admission, but after a while questions were raised as to whether such a policy was safe. 

1. The immigrants, especially those in the " new immigration," had customs, habits, and ideals differing widely from those of native Americans. Very often they lived by themselves in poor, thickly settled tenement districts. 

2. It was asserted that immigrants worked for low wages, crowding out the native workers, and sending back home most of what they earned. 

3. Formerly citizenship was not a necessary qualification for voting, and immigrants were used by evil politicians to enlarge the vote which they could control. They could be easily influenced by little kindnesses, or, if necessary, threats or bribes, and did not realize the importance of intelligent, honest government. 

Was it fair to find fault with the immigrant for sending home money which he received for doing work here? Do you suppose foreigners realize that they have caused serious problems here? 

Would Americans cause serious problems if they went and settled in another country in large numbers?

Step by step we began to exclude certain classes of immigrants that were considered undesirable, and to impose restrictions on individuals when we did not exclude entire groups. After the 

World War a still stricter policy was put into operation because of a fear that Europeans, finding their own countries so upset by the calamities growing out of the War, might try to come here in numbers too great for us to handle. 

The chief restrictions now in operation are as follows: (1) Chinese, Japanese, and other Mongolian laborers are not allowed to come in at all. (2) Persons unable to look out for themselves and "likely to become a public charge " are excluded. (3) A head tax of $8.00 must be paid by each immigrant. (4) An immigrant must pass a "literacy literacy" test, which shows whether he can read forty words in some language or dialect. (5) Persons suffering from contagious diseases or guilty of immoral crimes are excluded. (6) Under our present "National Origins " Act, the total number of immigrants in any one year is limited with certain exceptions to 150,000 from all parts of the world. 

Out of that 150,000, immigrants are allowed from any country up to a number that is supposed to correspond to the proportion of people of that ancestry already living here. If 10% of our people, for example, trace their ancestry to a certain country, 10% of the immigrants admitted in one year may come from that country.

Much of the examination of would-be immigrants is now done by American consuls in foreign ports. They can give health examinations and determine other qualifications just as readily as anybody on this side of the water and save the dreadful disappointment that comes to one who makes the long trip and then is refused admission. Lately there has been somewhat of a turning of the tide away from us, so that in some years now there are actually more people leaving the United States than coming here to live from other countries.

What is likely to be the attitude of native laborers in regard to our present immigration law? Do you think we should restrict our immigration still further, or relax our present laws? Discuss the arguments that may be offered for or against any of the restrictive measures mentioned in this section.

16. Making Foreigners into Americans. Although the number of aliens among us who become naturalized is much greater than it used to be, there are still a considerable number who never take out their citizenship papers.

When the 1930 census was taken there were still more than one in four of our aliens who had not even taken the first steps toward becoming citizens. 

Really to become " Americanized," an immigrant must do more than merely live here five years. Not only to become the best kind of citizen, but also for his own benefit, he must learn to speak the English language, try to understand our ideals, and learn our customs and standards. This may not be easy for an immigrant. 

Everything is so different from what he has been accustomed to the language, the customs, the clothes we wear, and the food we eat. The children of immigrants, however, are often hard to distinguish from those of native American families. Some teachers think that these children try even harder to make the most of their opportunities in school. 

Realizing the immigrants' difficulties, boards of education in many places, Y. M. C. A.'s, and similar societies have established 

Americanization schools to help foreigners prepare for citizenship and sometimes to teach them a trade. Large corporations may maintain Americanization schools for the benefit of their immigrant workers. A certificate from such a school is usually accepted by a judge in a naturalization court in place of the examination he might give. 

Is anything being done in your community for Americanization?

What are five things which might hinder the Americanization of an immigrant? Is it desirable that immigrants continue to use any of their foreign customs or dress after they come to this country? 

Show how schools help in Americanizing foreign families. Can children of foreign parents do anything toward Americanizing the rest of the family? Do you think of any ways in which you could help a recent immigrant?

We owe much to our immigrants. Some people would scornfully ask, "What do you mean 'owe'? They ought to be grateful to us." We are apt to think of them as ignorant, rude people who live in ugly homes, with "funny" customs and an unintelligible speech or broken English. And don't think for a minute

66 that the immigrant doesn't notice these things. He does not relish being called a "dago dago" or a wop." One Russian immigrant said, "If money in pocket, Americans like you; if not, don't care and swear at you." 

Another remarked, "America place like heaven for rich, but like hell for foreign worker." 

THE SINGING TOWER, LAKE WALES, FLORIDA 

The foreigner, Edward W. Bok, who gave this beautiful structure, became one of the best-loved Americans of his time. 

This tower contains seventy-one bells and is situated in beautiful surroundings on the highest point in the state of Florida. 

Immigrants provided most of the labor that built our railroads, erected great skyscrapers, paved our streets, and worked our mines. They or their children have risen to high places in the business world. Their love for the beautiful and their appreciation of art and music have influenced us more perhaps than we care to realize. How much, for instance, have Victor Herbert and Walter Damrosch done for American music!

Think of what this country would have lost if Andrew 

Carnegie had not come here from Scotland and built libraries to stand as mute reminders of him. We owe much to the inventions of such men as Michael Pupin and Charles

Steinmetz. Edward Bok not only became rich here, but did many things to make our country better. Jacob Riis was one of the finest social service workers that ever lived. Carl Schurz and 

Franklin K. Lane made fine records in our politics. The immigrant of today may be the outstanding man of tomorrow, and perhaps he will be even more valuable to America if you understand and help him! No country is so self-sufficient that it can get no benefit from other peoples.

Name other prominent men who came here from other countries. 

Do you think that we could have got along as well without them? 

How many of our moving-picture stars are foreign-born? 

Do you think that you look as queer and different to the immigrant as he appears to you? 

17. Relations among People of Different Colors. Our country has had one problem to a somewhat greater extent than most others — that of maintaining good will and proper social and business relations among people of different races and colors. White people sometimes make the mistake of thinking that they are the only capable people in the world. It is true that the greater part of world history has been wrought by white people, but that fact does not justify constantly talking about it or refusing to give a square deal to those of other colors. 

Courtesy Atchison, Topeka, & Santa Fe Railway

NAVAJO INDIANS ON THEIR RESERVATION 

These men are in front of a hogan, or dwelling house. The picture gives you a good idea of the nature of the Navajo reservation in Arizona. 

In this country the first race difficulties were between white men and red-or Indians, as they were strangely named. The red men were here first, but the white men for generations showed little consideration of any rights that the natives might have. 

The treatment of the Indians by the whites as they moved west is the worst blot on the story of our expansion across the continent. 

Today, however, a different policy prevails. Since 1924, all

Indians are considered citizens of the United States whether they live among white people or by themselves. Indians who wish to do so may continue to live in tribes in regions set apart for them and known as reservations. Schools have been established on these reservations for Indian children, and they learn just as easily as white children would under the same conditions. Men of recent 

Indian ancestry have served in Congress, and one of them, Mr. 

Charles Curtis, even became Vice President. Indians and white people frequently marry, and there does not seem to be the social difficulty arising from such marriages that occurs in the case of some other races. 

Do you think it is good to preserve the ceremonial dances, songs, and other customs of Indian tribes? Would you encourage the 

Indians to continue developing themselves as Indians or try to make them live among white people and ultimately not to be thought of as a separate race?

Your study of history has already told you the problems that were created by the bringing here of many Negroes as slaves. 

You have also learned that when these Negroes were set free, the change was so sudden that it was hard for both white men and black men to adjust themselves to the new conditions. For some time such difficulties were limited to the South. During the World 

War, when thousands of Negroes came North to work in factories and mills, some northern communities realized that perhaps they had not always understood how things were in the South. 

The southern white man believes that both whites and blacks will be better off if they live under separate conditions. So in the South today there are separate schools, separate compartments in street cars and railways, and other distinctions which do not prevail in other parts of the country. Mississippi is the only state today a majority of whose people are Negroes, but even there the margin is small. Everywhere there are signs that a better spirit is developing between white people and black. The work of such schools as that at Tuskegee, Alabama, founded by Booker T. Washington, has done much to help the Negroes to help themselves. Poets, musicians, lawyers, doctors, and skilled tradesmen have arisen among the Negroes whose work compares favorably with that of white people.

Find the names of at least two black men who have stood high among their own people or have rendered some notable public service, and learn something about their life and work. 

Another difficult question seems more serious on the Pacific Coast than it does elsewhere in the country. Here we have somewhat over 200,000 people of the yellow race. Chinese began to come at the time of the "gold rush" to California in 1849. Japanese came much later.

One cause of ill-feeling is the fact that a Chinese or Japanese laborer can live on a wage on which a white man would starve. 

Japanese are very successful in market gardening and the growing of small fruits. Chinese laborers have been kept out of the country since 1882; and when a new immigration law was passed in 

1924, the Japanese were put on the same basis as the Chinese and other Mongolian people. Students, tourists, and merchants, however, are readily admitted for visits or for longer stays. Many thought that a pleasanter way could have been found to keep out Japanese laborers than was actually employed; but perhaps if it is understood that this is our permanent policy, the Japanese will accept it as the Chinese have already done. The Japanese are a sensitive, ambitious people, who have learned bad practices as well as good ones from white nations. For our own interests, if for no other reason, we should treat them fairly.

There are Chinese and Japanese who are voters in our cities. How can that be? Have you ever met any Chinese or Japanese who did not in their personal conduct show a high degree of politeness and courtesy? 

Each one of us—white, black, red, or yellow - ought to try to think how he would feel in the other's place when any question comes up that might cause misunderstanding or lack of harmony. 

Very likely it is best that people of different races should not be forced to live where the differences between them might cause unpleasantness; but every American citizen of every color should have the opportunity to make the most of himself, and people of different races should work hand in hand for the well-being of their own race and the country as a whole. 

The people of our communities are widely but unevenly distributed over the country, come from many different races and nationalities,

have had to face some difficult problems as a result of unrestricted immigration, can best meet the problem of Americanization through earnest and sympathetic coöperation between native and foreign-born, should give credit to other colors than white for progress under difficult conditions, will find the greatest contentment among races where each strives to make the most of its own talents, but feels sincere interest in the progress of all.

CHAPTER IV THE GOOD CITIZEN IN THE HOME 

Let others delight 'mid new pleasures to roam, But give me, oh, give me, the pleasures of home! 

- PAYNE 

If the home is the first community that we know, it is likely to be first in importance also. Surely, when we spend as much time at home as most of us do, there must be habits and ideals established whose influences will reach far beyond the four walls of the house. 

Conditions that make or mar home life are certainly worthy of consideration in the study of good citizenship. 

What are the meanings of such fundamental terms as home, house, family?

How important in the life of our people are the services rendered by the home? 

What influences, good or bad, affect home life?

What conditions in the home make it a desirable or undesirable place in which to live?

How may we as individuals accept responsibilities for a worthy home life? 

18. What Is a Home? 

There are several words whose meaning we can feel much more easily than we can express. Home is one of them. Perhaps we can say that it is the life lived and the associations and sentiments formed among the members of a family. But how lifeless such a definition is! Even then we may need to define family. By this word we commonly refer to a group of people closely related by birth, marriage, or adoption who live together as one household.

What kinds of relationships are represented in families that you know? The average number of members in a family in the United 

States today is said to be 4.1. Just what does that mean? How does it compare with the size of families a hundred or more years ago? Can there be a family composed of one person? 45

Most of us come into membership in a home as soon as we come into the world, and spend some years in it before we realize that there are any other social institutions at all. There we are cared for and trained while we are still too young to gain much from 

AN OLD-TIME SOUTHERN MANSION 

Here is a Louisiana plantation mansion, built before the Civil War. We do not put up buildings like this today. other associations. There are homes, too, made up entirely of older people.

Theories have sometimes been proposed that children should be considered the property of the state, and trained in great public establishments. Apply the idea to yourself and see what you think of it. 

For old or young or middle-aged there is no idea for which it is easier to arouse deep sentiment than the thought of home.

It is a refuge when things go wrong in the world outside, a place of rest and refreshment that prepares for the world of toil and labor. Among the few songs that will never die, one will always include "Home, Sweet Home," "My Old Kentucky Home," and "Old Folks at Home." Nothing on the stage or on the screen will stir a person's wrath more readily than when the villain forecloses the mortgage or the rich landlord turns the poor washerwoman into the street. "If I were hanged on the highest hill, I know whose love would follow me still, Mother o' mine, Mother o' mine. 

If I were drowned in the deepest sea, I know whose tears would come down to me, Mother o' mine, Mother o' mine." 

What is the difference between a house and a home? 

Do the children of educated or of ignorant families as a rule remain longer

at home? Why is it specially important that very young children should receive particular care? Are there any disadvantages in being an only child? Do you think a child is better trained in a public institution, such as an orphan asylum, than in a private home, surrounded not by other orphans but by brothers and sisters and parents? 

19. What Do the Home and Family Do for Their Members? 

One hardly knows where to start in listing the many services of the home. We almost take for granted the care of physical needs and comforts as a function of the home, but that is only the beginning.

Language is probably the first important thing the child picks up in the home. Perhaps you think "baby talk" sounds the same in any language, but as soon as it begins to seem like words, the words are those spoken by members of the family. 

How does men's ability to talk give them a great advantage over animals? Is it an asset or a handicap to learn a different language than English in one's childhood days? Can one really learn to speak a language other than that which he learned in childhood? What is meant by "brogue," "accent"?

The family develops in its members habits, customs, and ideals which may last through life. In the home we get our first ideas about God and religion. It is the exception for a person who has not been brought up in a religious home to be deeply religious. 

A teacher once was discussing with a pupil the telling of untruths. "Your father would not lie to your mother, would he?" she inquired. "Sure he does!" was the boy's answer. What could one expect from a boy with that sort of home training? 

Moreover, one's tastes and opinions are frequently those of the family. If at home you have a great deal of music, you probably will acquire a liking for it. If your father is a Republican, you are likely to think you are too, long before you can give any reason for your attitude.

Do all girls like to dress in the fashion that their mothers prefer? 

Give five examples of opinions on questions of general interest that you acquired at home. Have you changed any of them since you started to school?

We get our first lessons in citizenship in the home. We are first taught respect for authority there, both when we learn to obey

our parents and when they instruct us to abide by the laws of the community or the nation. We are or should be taught to be of service to others, to make sacrifices for others' comfort, and to respect their rights. If this attitude is not developed in the home, the chances are that we will not show it elsewhere. 

THE COOLIDGE FARMHOUSE 

This is an excellent example of the plain, neat, unpretentious farmhouse one sees in many rural districts. Here President Coolidge spent many of his boyhood days, and here he took the oath of office as President, administered by his own father. 

Do children appreciate the benefits of a good home? Do children in a humble home love their parents as well as children in a rich home? 

What do we mean by a "spoiled child"? Is it possible to be so intensely devoted to one's own family as to disregard the rights of others? If a family fails to train a child in any of the respects we have just mentioned, is there any other place where he can get such training?

Discuss the difference between family life in colonial times and today with reference to the following points: independence or dependence upon others, recreation, responsibility of each member for the welfare of the family, and any other respects in which you can see a contrast.

Mention some pleasant features a modern home may have which our great-grandfathers' did not. Do you discuss current topics or school events at home? 

There is a big differ- 

20. Conditions That Affect Our Homes. ence between a house and a home. It is said that only six lan-

guages besides the English have a word for "home." And what a lack this is! A house is a place where people may live. A home is the common life of the family which occupies a particular place. 

The former is material. The latter is spiritual. A house is made of bricks, perhaps a home, of love.

When we move from one place to another our house stays behind; our home goes where we go. 

Any right-minded person wishes each member of the home to have such a genuine, whole-hearted interest in it that he will prefer to be there rather than somewhere else the greater part of the time.

He wants it a place where it is easy to do right and where no one will think of

PRESIDENT ROOSEVELT AND HIS FAMILY 

doing wrong. In the ideal home every one does his share toward making it comfortable and happy. 

Yet once in a while one finds a family in which it appears that the chief use one makes of brother and sister is to find fault with them. 

At the President's right is Mrs. Roosevelt, and at his left is his mother. Standing and sitting are the daughter and the four sons, two daughters-in-law, the son-in-law, and a grandchild. This was obviously a Christmas party. 

What can money buy for a home and what can it not buy? What effect do servants have upon home life? A husband once brought home to his wife a bouquet of carnations. When he handed them to her, her reply was, "What have you been doing now?" What do you suppose was wrong in that home? What is the effect of untidiness upon home life? Just how much authority and control should the father have in the family? Is compulsion ever justified in an ideal home? Does it indicate disrespect when a child calls his father "Dad"? Is there any event in your family history or any member of the family that may be a just cause for family pride?

Is it ever right for a child to refuse to obey his father or mother?

Can home life be ideal without the religious element in it? Mention three or four practices or habits you have seen that mark good manners in the home, and a similar number that mark bad manners. 

Unfortunately there are a good many homes that are far from ideal - homes where conditions are such as almost to chase the family out. Some people believe that there has been a general letdown of moral ideals everywhere which has lowered the tone of family life. Disgraceful family quarrels are too common. 

Every divorce means a broken home. If there are children in the family, the situation is much worse than if there are not. The number of divorces has been increasing in recent years, especially in our own country. Some states pass divorce laws that seem intended to break up the family. Some one has nicknamed Reno "the Great Divide."

There are many outside influences which tend to weaken one's interest in home. There are hundreds of places to go to, ranging from those that are free, to others where one must pay several dollars to get in and goodness only knows how many more to get out. 

Father goes to the Shriners or the Elks, mother to a bridge party, Sally to a dance, and Bill also to a dance, but not with his sister. 

Another evening they may all go out to the movies but probably not all to the same place.

Another responsible cause for loosening family ties lies in the changed conditions in business and industry. To carry on great factories and stores many thousands of people must leave their homes for the greater part of the day. In such places there has been a steadily increasing opportunity for the employment of women and girls. No doubt we should be glad that girls are willing and even anxious to support themselves rather than be a drain on the family pocketbook; but if the women and the men both have to go out to work, what is to become of the home? 

What is the effect of sermons on such subjects as "How to Be 

Happy Though Married"? Do the jokes about marriage on the stage or in the "funny" papers have a harmful effect? If you are familiar with any cases of divorce, what seemed to be the causes of them? Can the outside interests of members of the family be used to strengthen rather than weaken the family? What things once

attended to almost entirely in the home are now largely provided by organizations outside the home? 

21. Protecting the Family. - Broken homes are not always so much the fault of men and women as of the conditions under which they live. Individual men and women, for instance, are not to be blamed if their work must take them away from their homes eight hours a day in addition to the time required for going and coming. Poverty does not necessarily mean a broken home, but it means a family life with little joy in it. When the father of the family is dead, it may be next to impossible for any other member of it to support the whole group. To meet conditions of that kind some states have mothers' pension laws, which make it possible for the mother to hold her family together with the help of the contribution she gets from a Mothers' Pension Fund. On the other hand, if it is proved that parents are not treating their children properly, the children can be taken away from them and placed in an institution where they will not be abused. 

Marriage laws also serve to protect the family. True, different states have different laws some strict and some too easy yet, in every case, children under a certain age must have the permission of their parents before they can marry. In some states both parties must be twenty-one years old before they may marry without parental consent.

What the government can do to protect homes, however, is limited. Education, wise guidance, and mutual good will among the members of the family are the surest means of preventing family trouble. 

22. Where the Family Lives. - Home is life and not the place where we live. Yet it does make a difference where we live. If dozens of people are crowded into one dingy, filthy tenement, a family cannot live a real family life. A dirty, tumble-down dwelling, surrounded by a still dirtier court or street, cannot possibly create much attraction for its inmates. A clean, neat dwellingplace in a neighborhood where the air is pure and where there are a few flowers and some grass gives a sense of comfort and beauty to the home.

Even a big apartment house, no matter how richly furnished its rooms may be, can hardly seem like a real home for most people.

Who could develop much love for Apartment No. 8 on the sixth floor of 408 West 130th Street? The phrase does not sound like a home and hardly any stretch of imagination can make it so. Yet apartments and duplexes each year house more and more of the people in our large cities. Perhaps it is an easier life in a modern apartment house, but think what you lose! No longer can Bud keep a dog, or Sister have her friends to play on the front porch. 

Ewing Galloway

AN APARTMENT 

Does this look like a home? What advantages or disadvantages in regard to home life does it suggest? Contrast it with the picture on page 48. 

Instead of many airy rooms, you are cramped into three or four, at rates that are almost astounding.

It usually makes a difference, too, whether people own the place where they live. Those who own feel a personal interest in the spot. They take some pride in its appearance. It means something to them to keep the plumbing in repair and the outside painted. The renter does not think so much about these things, but depends upon the whim or generosity of his landlord. The result is that moving day is a sort of annual festival to some families.

Unfortunately, more and more people are content each year to let somebody else provide them a place to live. The census of

1930 found in this country nearly 30,000,000 families. Of these about 16,000,000 lived in rented dwellings. More than half of the people of the United States depend upon other persons to supply them their living quarters. Only about 2 per cent of the people of Manhattan Island own the places where they live. Fort Wayne, Indiana, and some cities farther west, such as Kansas City, Kansas, and Portland, Oregon, claim that from 54 to 58 per cent of their

Many farming sections do better than that, but on the other hand South Carolina has only 30 per cent of its families owning their homes, and Georgia only 28 per cent. 

Perhaps you have heard some one say, "I have sold my home."

What is your community's record in regard to house-owning? If you can work it out without advertising any family's private business, find how your own class stands on the matter and why. 

23. Our Own Responsibility for Good Citizenship at Home. 

To make the next set of homes better, we have to begin with those that are now in existence. When the boys and girls of years ago

set up homes of their own, they did not have to do many things that they had not already become accustomed to doing. Now this is changed. In the apartments and houses occupied by our wealthy families the girls do not expect to learn to cook, or to sew, or even to take care of the house. The boys have little opportunity to do anything around the house even if they are so inclined. 

A home in which a child does not help with the daily tasks is not the kind which will be of most value to him in later life. Jack can at least learn orderliness and a sense of responsibility for cleaning up his own room and putting things in their proper places. 

Mary may help her mother with the housework and perhaps do the dishes at night. In the poorer districts and sometimes even in the so-called better neighborhoods, the city does not offer the opportunity for the kind of training that one finds in a village or on the farm, but there is usually something that every member of the family can do.

GOOD ADVICE FROM DOROTHY DIX 

A girl, who plainly regards all elderly people as prehistoric fossils, asks: 66 'Why is it that old people can never understand young people?" 

Oh, but they do, my dear. It is the young who cannot understand the old, because they lack the wisdom and the knowledge that years alone bring.

It would surprise them to know that all of us older people have been down the road that they are just starting to travel. That there is not a single man or woman of us who has not been confused with the muddled thoughts of youth as they are; who has not been torn by the same temptations; who has not sent up the little painted balloons of hope, and wept the same tears of disappointment when they collapsed.

The young never complain more bitterly that the old can't understand them than when they advance the theory that they have a right to live their own lives. The older people do understand, and they know that none of us can live our own lives because our lives are so interlocked with others. They know that none of us can alone bear the consequences of our folly and our sins. Others must suffer with us and for us. Others must be branded with our shame. Others must pay the debt of our failures. Others must nurse us when we are sick, feed us when we are hungry, shoulder the burdens we have ducked. 

Oh, it isn't that the old don't understand the young, my dear. They do. Too well. Hence these tears.

Every boy and girl should understand the importance and seriousness of home responsibilities. Perhaps ignorant or careless parents who have failed to give good counsel along these lines have done much harm. Girls and boys alike should learn as much as possible about the planning and care of the various interests of home life.

Home is the earliest and most important association that we know, implants life-long habits and ideals, has changed considerably in interests and activities, should be a place of comfort and happiness but sometimes falls short of its opportunities, is to some extent protected by laws, is greatly affected by its environment, should give its younger members an all-round training for later responsibilities.
