CHAPTER V MAKING THE COMMUNITY ATTRACTIVE 

A thing of beauty is a joy forever. KEATS

In the last chapter we paid special attention to the home as the foundation of community life. Now we are going to speak of the community from the standpoint of making it attractive for the people who live in its homes. A community that looks attractive is likely to be safe and healthful. Attractiveness, safety, and health do not come by accident, but as a result of foresight and planning that may take years.

What do we mean by community planning, and what are its essential features?

With what difficulties or problems may city planners have to contend?

What are the definite practical features of intelligent community planning? 

What undesirable conditions should a community remove or avoid?

How may each one of us help to make our own community as attractive as possible? 

24. Planning a Community. If we stand at the corner of 

Main Street and Central Avenue and look in the direction of Mary's home, do we get a view of a broad highway reaching straight into the distance with beautiful maples or elms on both sides? Or can we see only a block or two down a narrow street that turns off in some direction which we cannot follow from where we stand? If Jack wants a game of tennis, does he have to walk, drive, or take a street car for two or three miles to a distant park or can he play near his own home?

The three most desirable objectives in planning a community are convenience, health, and beauty. Do you think the founders of your community had these three objectives in mind when they established it? Boston and Pittsburgh are good examples of cities that merely "happened." One can easily accept the tradition 57

that Boston's streets follow the lines of old cow-paths, when he looks at a map of the city or, better still, when he tries to get around the older part of the city before he has been there long enough to get his bearings. The older part of New York City shows the lack of planning, but in the later portions long avenues run north and south and are crossed at right angles by streets numbered in regular order. William Penn laid out Philadelphia on the plan of a checkerboard, but he was not enough of a prophet to foresee that his streets would be too narrow for the traffic of a great city. 

Courtesy National Recreation Association A SMALL COMMUNITY'S RECREATION FACILITIES 

Manchester, Illinois, provides such opportunities as this for healthful amusement. 

These boys apparently did not have to go far to enjoy their game. 

The outstanding example of a well-planned, beautiful American city is Washington, conceived by the genius of the Frenchman, Major L'Enfant. The Capitol is the center of the city's plan. 

Streets running north and south intersect at right angles with others running east and west. To break the monotony of the scheme, and to promote the beauty and convenience of the plan, a system of diagonal avenues intersects the "checkerboard." 

Where the avenues and streets come together, little parks, known as" circles," are formed. Some of the European capitals have also gained an enviable reputation for their beauty and convenience. 

City planning is not always easy. A city with an ocean or lake harbor cannot afford to separate its business district from its wharves. Yet a lake or river front can be made beautiful, and a

city's residential sections should not be deprived of the fresh air and the view. Railroads must serve factories and commercial districts conveniently, but they should be located so as not to spoil the prettier parts of town. Street railways and bus lines should be convenient for everybody, but surely we do not want them on every street. 

Courtesy Great Northern Railway

A PART OF MINNEAPOLIS 

It is easy to see on what plan much of this city must have been laid out. 

A plan suitable for level ground, like that on which Philadelphia is situated, would be utterly out of the question for the hills of San Francisco or Seattle. Yet a great city with some kind of plan was sure to rise around such harbors. Cities have sometimes put themselves to great expense to overcome natural hindrances to their growth. Three times Pittsburgh pared off the "Hump," as a steep hill that hemmed in its business section was called. Boston filled in its "Back Bay" and made it into one of its most attractive sections. Seattle washed away most of a hill that separated its business district from its water front. Los Angeles,

wanting a harbor, annexed the coast twenty miles away and spent millions of dollars to make it fit for large vessels. 

Are there any natural disadvantages which make planning a problem in your community? Is there any geographical or other reason for locating your town where it is? Is it easier to plan a small community or a large one? 

SAN FRANCISCO FROM THE BAY 

Courtesy Californians, Inc.

This city is on the point of land between San Francisco Bay and the Pacific Ocean. It is one of the hilliest of the large cities of the country. Ferryboats ply between this city and Oakland on the east side of the bay. A great bridge is planned to span the Golden Gate, which will offer another means of communication between the two cities. 

25. Laying Out a Community. - The checkerboard system of laying out streets is the simplest method and makes it easy to identify any particular spot. If the streets are named in some recognizable order, and if each block counts for 100 in numbering houses, even a stranger has little excuse for getting lost. The most serious objections to this system are the monotony of appearance and the necessity of going around too many right angles in getting anywhere. Besides, you must have fairly level territory for such a plan. The plan may often be modified by laying out diagonal

avenues, as in Washington, and by constructing boulevards to encircle the city and reach its beauty spots. 

Some people like the spider-web as a model for laying out a city's streets. Detroit partly follows this idea, with its main avenues reaching out for miles from the heart of the city, and other streets following around in a semicircle more or less regularly.

What system of naming and numbering streets seems best to you? How would your system work if applied to such a hilly place as Kansas City? 

What systems are used in any communities that you know about? Draw sketches to illustrate their main features.

Courtesy Great Northern Railway THE MAIN BUSINESS STREET OF DULUTH 

Notice how this city is set on a hill ris- A civic center is an important feature of modern city planning. It is a group of public buildings such as a courthouse, city hall, post office, library, and other institutions frequently used by the people, located where some of the most important streets intersect, so that it will be convenient of access from all sections of the city. The civic center may be both the very heart of the city and one of its chief beauty spots. ing from the western end of Lake Superior. 

What difficulties in planning might be expected here in comparison with Minneapolis, for example?

In a large city it would be undesirable, of course, to have every library, school, and museum crowded into one section. In this case several smaller centers should be created to serve different sections of the city. The much-needed parks and playgrounds should be so placed that all the people can enjoy them without great expense or waste of time. This calls for careful planning in advance. After a community's business streets are all built up,

it is like crying over spilt milk to bewail their narrowness and congestion. It is an expensive proposition to tear down business houses in order to put trees, grass, flowers, or even boulevards in their place. 

Courtesy Denver Tourist Bureau

DENVER'S CIVIC CENTER 

In this view you see the public library, the state capitol, the cathedral, and other important buildings. A comparatively new city, like Denver, can plan and lay out such a feature on a proper scale. 

Is there anything which your community can do or ought to do to improve its physical appearance or arrangement? (See Project 1 on page 76.) Should automobile tourist travel be routed through the business section of a city? Are "through" streets for automobiles desirable? — - 26. The Construction of Streets and Highways. The laying out or paving of a street is usually done by the officers of a local government, unless a state does it as a part of a state highway system. Frequently owners whose property faces the highway are required to pay for the improvements in proportion to the benefit they receive. If a house is damaged instead of benefited, the local government will pay the owner instead of asking him to contribute. 

After a street has been laid out and paved, the cost of repairing is usually taken care of by the government. 

A main street in the heart of a town or city should be 100 feet wide or more, including sidewalks, especially if there are to be street-car tracks on it. Residential streets may not need quite half

that width. In the case of such streets more attention should be given to appearance than usually can be done in the business sections of a community. Trees set in grassy parkways, and rows of flower-beds, have added much to the beauty of many a street. 

In residential districts everything possible should be done to keep down the noise of traffic. Here asphalt or concrete pavement is best, except that on hilly streets brick may be safer in bad

Canal Street, in New Orleans, is 171 feet wide. It has been declared to be one of the four best-illuminated streets in the nation. Wouldn't it be fine if all large cities could have as much room for traffic as Canal Street affords ? weather. On park and country roads the crushed stone surface that we speak of as macadamized may take the place of something more expensive, but concrete is now the most popular surface for main highways. On a business street to be traversed by heavy trucks, block stone may be needed for durability. If it is surfaced with a thin coating of concrete, it will not be so rough as plain cobblestones.

A county or state should consider the matter of planning as well as a local community. The laying out of highways, the location of parks, and attention to natural and artificial beauty are matters

that may often need to be considered with reference to large areas.

27. Beauty as a Force for Better Living. There is far more crime, ignorance, dirty politics, and sickness, and far more boys and girls run away from home, in the ugly, congested parts of our cities that we call "slums," than in the attractive districts. If a

person is always surrounded by beauty, he unconsciously learns to appreciate the better things. If his own house and lawn are well cared for, he will have respect for a public building or a public park, and may encourage others to make their home surroundings more attractive. 

A SAMPLE OF MODERN HIGHWAY CONSTRUCTION 

This is known as the "clover leaf" crossing. It is at Northboro, Massachusetts, on the fine new highway between Boston and Worcester. Notice the unique plan which makes it possible to transfer from one highway to another with the minimum danger of accident.

Is it natural for people to like beautiful things? Can there be a beautiful home in ugly surroundings? Why did the early American pioneers pay little attention to civic beauty? 

Beauty does not mean glitter or showiness. Mr. and Mrs. 

Newly Rich sometimes stuff their houses full of costly vases, pictures, and chairs. The real artist never does that. What he does have, he wants to be good, for he knows that a few good things rightly placed will make any decent room attractive. Colors in the room should harmonize. Rugs, draperies, and other furnishings should look well together. To make one's house beautiful, in the proper sense of the word, as well as to make one's community attractive, is not only a mark of good citizenship but an inspiration for still better living.

Draw a plan for a house that you would like to live in. Describe the way you would like to furnish the room in which you would receive most callers. Can you make your own house more attractive than it is?

Suggest practical ways of beautifying, inside or out, a tenement house; a single dwelling in a moderate-income district; a large place in a purely residential neighborhood. Is paint of any value except for looks?

28. "The City Beautiful." What makes a community beautiful? Very likely Carbon City and Barren Hill think their case is quite hopeless. Perhaps they have been built solely for the purpose of making steel or smelting copper. Nature does not seem to have taken so much pains with them as if it had given them a setting on the shores of an attractive lake surrounded by inspiring hills and forests. But no community has a right to refrain from trying to make itself attractive. The beautiful cities in southern

California show that courage and enterprise can make trees grow in a desert. "I think that I shall never see a poem lovely as a tree." New

England boasts of its maples, oaks, and elms; Florida and California of their palms. Parkways give the trees plenty of room and add their touch of living green. It takes years for these trees to reach their full glory, but it is worth all the time and labor that it costs. Trees make the heat of the summer more endurable

Though the most important service of parks is a means of rest and recreation, they are usually among the most attractive parts of a community. 

Can you tell one tree from another? Sketch some characteristic trees. What kinds of trees would you recommend for your community?

Just as trees add to the beauty of a town by day, so do its lights by night. Any one who has stumbled along a dark or dimly lighted

street looking for a house number realizes how important street lights are. Besides, there is a certain glamour about a "great white way "that attracts people. But still more essential is the necessity of restraining crime. Good street lights are worth dozens of policemen.

Almost every community has a skeleton in its closet which it tries to keep out of the sight of visitors. It may be a dump where tin cans, papers, old shoes, and worse-smelling things are heedlessly thrown. It may be a mosquito-breeding swamp or mudhole. 

It may be a filthy alley, unpaved, dark, strewn with bottles, garbage, and slime. Perhaps you may help arouse public sentiment against such disregard of civic beauty and health.

Billboards may do much for advertising but very little for beauty. The advertising manager thinks billboards offer the easiest way to reach the people, and the billboard man even declares that his signs improve the appearance of the street. Perhaps it would be too much to insist that there should be no public advertising. But advertising can at least be displayed in such a way as not to violate every sense of natural beauty and appropriateness. You drive along a fine highway admiring the grandeur

What do you think would happen if all decent citizens said with one voice that they would never buy anything from a firm whose advertising signs spoiled the landscape, or support a candidate for office whose name and picture was pasted or painted where it would deface public property? Wouldn't that bring them to see the error of their ways as quickly as anything? A tourist likes to make a second visit to a town that has impressed him pleasantly.

29. Keeping a Community 

Clean. One necessary feature in making a city beautiful is to keep it clean. It is not all the fault of your officers if your neighborhood does not look right. The members of a family can see to it that their own residence is kept in repair, if it belongs to them, and that it is kept clean whether it belongs to them or not. The appearance of a front lawn or a back yard is good evidence of what the family itself is. 

About the surest way to clean a city street is to have the work done by men who go over its surface with brooms and carts. But the work of these "white wings," as they have been nicknamed, is slow and therefore rather expensive. Machines with revolving brushes have been invented which sweep the dirt toward the curb,

where it can be shoveled into wagons by men following the sweeper. 

Some cities have used a kind of vacuum sweeper on the streets. A water wagon sends out streams which wash the dirt toward the gutters, where much of it runs away. Oil or calcium chloride may keep down the dust reasonably well on a dirt road. Each community must do its own experimenting and find what suits its needs best. 

Does any blame rest on you if your street is not clean? Does it make any difference whether the snow and ice are cleaned off the sidewalk in front of your house?

Some communities, especially those that have factories, find the soft coal smoke a very serious problem. It once was the fashion to argue that smoke means business, and business means prosperity. But now intelligent business men and citizens know that the smoky city is likely to be a wasteful city. 

That smoke from soft coal is itself dangerous to health has not been positively proved. But anything that makes a city dirty or shuts out sunlight cannot promote good health. To get rid of the dirt produced by

Courtesy The Elgin Corporation AN UP-TO-DATE STREET SWEEPER 

This machine flushes and sweeps streets at the same time. Machines of this kind are in use in a considerable number of our cities. unnecessary smoke causes an expense of thousands of dollars every month.

It is usually easy to induce a factory manager to do better if we can show him that he will save money by operating furnaces more efficiently. Those who will not be convinced can be brought to terms by a few prosecutions. Even a factory town can therefore largely decide for itself whether it will be grimy and ugly or attractive and clean.

How many of these problems of cleanliness, or some others, trouble your community? If you have any local problems, what is being done to remedy them? How have railroads reduced the smoke nuisance? 

30. Restrictions for the Good of the Community. - Progressive communities today do not let anybody start up any kind of business or construct any sort of building anywhere he pleases. When 

ONE PLACE FOR A ZONING ORDINANCE 

A building like this West Penn Hospital, in Pittsburgh, should be protected from unnecessary noise and disturbance from commercial or factory activities. that is allowed, one may put up an attractive dwelling house, only to have his next-door neighbor open a garage or a grocery store.

When that occurs, immediately his own place becomes much less desirable.

Is it fair to use your own property in such a way as to reduce the value of your neighbors'? 

One remedy for this sort of thing is to divide a community into zones, each for a certain purpose one for factories, one for business houses, another for single family residences, another for resi-

dence buildings of any kind, and so on. This plan helps to keep the value of property in a district fairly stable. In a large city some business zones may be permitted in different sections, but business places kept out of the purely residential districts. Other regulations may limit the height of buildings in different districts. 

Many European cities have been doing this for a long time. New 

York was the first large city in this country to adopt a thorough. zoning plan; but now many cities, large and small, have done it. 

Courtesy U. S. Post Office Department ONE WAY TO PLACE RURAL DELIVERY BOXES 

The appearance of the neighborhood is improved by having the boxes placed together in this way instead of having one box on a pole in front of each house. 

What is meant by building restrictions? What sort of people might be opposed to zoning? What argument would you use with a person who asserted that it was nobody else's affair if he made money by turning a part of his house into a store or automobile accessory shop? Can business buildings be made attractive as well as useful? Give examples to prove your answer.

Would it add to a community's attractiveness to be a noiseless town? Is noise a sign of life? Make a list of the unpleasant noises that you notice in your community. Do you have any "quiet zones"? If you do, how are they indicated? Where should quiet always be insisted upon? What would you think of a local ordinance that would forbid the playing of pianos, victrolas, or radios after 11 P.M.? (They have such rules in some German towns.)

Are church bells a form of unnecessary noise? Should newsboys and hucksters be allowed to call their wares anywhere and at all times? City people who go to the country sometimes complain that they cannot sleep. What do you suppose is the trouble? Can you offer any suggestions for reducing the noise in your community? 

Can we expect to make a town wholly noiseless? 

31. Our Own Part in Promoting Community Attractiveness. 

We that means we as individuals and not simply the other fellow must do our part in promoting the attractiveness of our 

Courtesy Atchison, Topeka, & Santa Fe Railway

PERSHING SQUARE, LOS ANGELES 

This much-frequented place is naturally beautiful. Do you suppose the people who sit on these benches do their part to keep it so? community. We can help to keep our home conditions - the house, whatever yard is around it, and as much of the neighborhood as we are really responsible for neat and clean. Perhaps we can plant some grass or some flowers. We can look out for our personal appearance. It is no disgrace to get our hands dirty doing worth-while work, but it is no credit to let them stay so all the time. 

We can take care of our clothes, so that they are not torn or damaged unnecessarily. It is our fault that parks look as they often do on Monday morning or after a holiday.

We can refrain from marring the appearance of public buildings. 

One sometimes sees grown-ups striking matches at the entrance of a library or museum, utterly ignoring the fact that the marks of the match are almost impossible to remove. A child sometimes goes along a corridor in a school or library with a pencil in his hand and a black line shows along the wall where he went. Sometimes rowdies will even deliberately deface or pull down monuments and memorial tablets set up to beautify a street or park. Every one of us not only can avoid doing things like this but may be able to prevent others from doing them. 

Who pays for such damage to property? You and I, or at least those members of our family who pay taxes directly or indirectly. 

Our own selfish interests should help us to watch that we do our part in keeping the community attractive and encourage a sense of responsibility on the part of others. 

Community attractiveness depends upon intelligent forethought, laying out streets so as to make communication convenient, constructing streets so as to handle traffic efficiently, taking advantage of opportunities for attractiveness in appearance, avoiding anything that disfigures or mars, assigning to suitable zones the various activities of the community, depending upon each good citizen to accept responsibility.

CHAPTER VI HEALTH AND RECREATION IN THE COMMUNITY 

To keep the body fit is the first essential in keeping the mind alert and operative, the conscience clear, and the soul courageous and aspiring. 

FAUNCE 

We have been talking about the community in general and the responsibility of each good citizen for coöperation with his fellow citizens for the good of all. Now let us consider the many interests in the life of a wide-awake boy or girl or man or woman of today and see what conditions are necessary for him to develop those interests. 

Particularly let us observe how the community helps us to have the priceless possession of good health and what our own responsibility may be in that connection.

Select some one whom you consider outstanding as a good citizen and make as complete a list as possible of all the activities and interests which help to make his life. 

A. AN ALL-ROUND LIFE 

32. The Many Interests of a Good Citizen. A well-rounded life surely has these four interests — physical, moral, intellectual, and material. One will fall short of the highest achievements of good citizenship if any one of them is neglected. 

1. Our physical interests. Without health everything else suffers. There are laws of health and conduct which, if obeyed, will help one to keep well. 

2. Our moral interests. Perhaps you do not consciously stop to say to yourself before you do a thing that it is right or wrong. 

Yet any act, even the smallest, is distinctly good, and therefore right, or not harmful, and therefore excusable - or else it is wrong. 

3. Our intellectual interests. - The thing above all others that distinguishes man from other living beings is that he thinks and 78

reasons. To do this successfully, he must have proper knowledge. 

He goes to school, attends lectures, reads books, or talks with intelligent people. 

4. Our material interests. 

We must eat, we must sleep, we want things to keep us comfortable. 

Every one, whether he lives in Paris or in Tompkins Corners, is interested in this material or economic side of life this matter of earning a living and having some comforts. 

Why are some people more successful in earning a 

THE GYMNASIUM, PURDUE UNIVERSITY 

No college or university thinks it is properly equipped today unless it has a modern gymnasium to look out for the physical development of its students. living than others? That is one question which we shall try to answer. 

Does your community make it possible for its citizens to develop these four interests? Have we a right to expect the government to look after this matter, or is it entirely a problem of private life? 

33. How We Attain the All-Round Life. Some years ago a committee of the National Education Association drew up a list of seven great objectives of education. They thought of education as not merely going to school, but training for the fullest development of all one's talents and interests. We may think of the seven items in their list as seven steps by which one rises to the full realization of an all-round life. 

1. Health. You know why that was put first. 

2. Command of fundamental processes. Reading, writing, and arithmetic the "three R's" of which we sometimes make fun, but which underlie all the knowledge any one acquires. 

3. Worthy home membership. - Since most of us have to spend a considerable share of our time at home, we ought to know how to spend that time most happily and comfortably. 

4. Vocational education. Most of us have to make our own living. We need to prepare for this as well as for anything else we do.

5. Citizenship training. - Citizens make a government good or bad in proportion as they understand and practice their duties toward it and toward one another. 

6. The wise use of leisure time. People as a rule do not have to work so long or so hard as they once did. They have more time for amusement and recreation than ever before. What are they going to do with this spare time? 

7. Ethical character. The community is not doing its duty if it fails to help boys and girls set up right and proper standards of conduct and measure up to these standards. 

Do you think of anything else worth including in a list of essential conditions for comfort and happiness?

How many of these features do most of us enjoy today in comparison with what the average person enjoyed a hundred years ago? 

How many of them do you think we feel the need of today to a greater extent than people did a hundred years ago? Has the person whom you had in mind as an all-round citizen been successful in attaining these seven objectives?

B. PROMOTING HEALTH 

How important is health?

What has been people's idea about disease and its prevention ? 

How do our governments help us to obtain such desirable elements as pure air, light, water, pure food? 

How may the neighborhood in which we live be kept healthful?

What is done by way of care for the sick in body or mind? 

What is our own part in maintaining conditions that build good health? 

34. People's Attitude toward Health and Disease. Sensible people today realize how important it is to protect our health. "Next to keeping good is keeping well." Perhaps from your own experience you have learned how little joy in life there is when a person is sick; and perhaps you have realized how your own illness upset the entire home, and required some one to look after you. The manager of an office or factory finds as one of his most annoying problems the absence of people for even a day or two from their regular work. You may have read of people like Robert 

Louis Stevenson who accomplished much in spite of constant weak-

ness and ill health, but how much more he might have done if he had been well!

If you have had health rules and health practices drilled into you since the early grades in school, it will not be necessary to go into them in great detail here. Yet it has not been so long that either school authorities or parents understood how important this matter is. Once people seemed to think that sickness was something that could not be helped, and did not try to keep away from it. Parents would say that Johnny would get the measles some time, and he might as well have them There is no gain in worrying just because some one in the neighborhood has a contagious disease, but it is also true that parents are almost criminals when they deliberately allow their children to get into danger. now.

More people know something about the laws of health today than ever before, and this understanding ought to mean that more people will obey those laws. If people would practice health habits at all times, and not merely when an epidemic is around, everybody would be better off. 

Changed ideas of living have brought much improvement. 

Today the average length of human life in the western world is nearly sixty years, though there was a time when thirty-three years was considered the average figure. Records of births, deaths, and marriages, which together we call vital statistics, are today kept much more carefully than ever before. Communities where people are careless about these matters are likely to show much higher death rates than elsewhere.

35. Preventing Disease. Good health is even more a result of preventing disease than of curing it. Several agencies coöperate in this effort. The most active of these, as we might expect, is the government. Governments can take measures to protect health which could seldom be as well done by individual citizens or private coöperation. To construct a sewer, for example, is usually a government's job. An individual could dig a cesspool 

Courtesy Pennsylvania Department of Health RELIEF FOR THE TUBERCULAR 

This is the Mt. Alto Sanatorium, in Pennsylvania. Notice how the cottages are constructed so that the occupants can get the full benefit of as much air and sunlight as possible. in his own back yard, but unless some qualified officer kept watch of him, that very cesspool might become a menace to the entire neighborhood.

Our national government ordinarily cannot do so much in protecting our health as in many other activities. There is a Public 

Health Service in the Department of the Treasury which from time to time issues bulletins telling facts they have discovered and making recommendations. Agents of the national government also examine would-be immigrants to see if they are suffering from any contagious diseases. Even American citizens returning from abroad would be held up by these officials if they sought to come into the country while similarly afflicted.

Sometimes an epidemic becomes so serious that the entire country is in danger, as during the several influenza epidemics, the worst of which came during the World War in the fall of 1918. Then the national Public Health Service and the various state boards of health tried to coöperate in keeping conditions under control. 

Courtesy Pennsylvania Department of Health PROTECTING SCHOOL CHILDREN 

These children are being given inoculation against diphtheria. It may be no fun right now, but they will be glad of it later on. 

Since some diseases are not merely national but world-wide, the League of Nations has its own health activities and has accomplished much good in the study of diseases. It constantly seeks to coöperate with health officials of all nations. 

Every state has a direct responsibility in this matter of health. 

It maintains a state department or board of health, which has general authority over the enforcement of state health laws. This board or its chief official may have authority to establish a quarantine over the whole state and close up theaters, schools, and other places of assemblage, if that seems advisable in order to stop the spread of an epidemic. State boards, too, are usually given a good

deal of authority in the rural districts of a state, because there are fewer local officers to attend to this important matter. They often test milk or water which is or may be used for drinking purposes and forbid the use of any that is dangerous. 

In our larger communities, however, the direct responsibility for protecting public health must rest upon the local department of health. In a city that has crowded tenement districts or slums, it must be particularly active. Both cities and states may maintain hospitals, especially for the treatment of people with contagious diseases or tuberculosis.

Our schools try to prevent disease by requiring or recommending vaccination against smallpox and inoculation against diphtheria. They have their doctors and dentists (at least in the larger cities) to watch over the health of the children. They teach hygiene and physiology and require physical exercise with the idea of building up strong bodies. 

Why do we have laws forbidding the use of roller towels and public drinking cups? Are there anti-spitting laws in your community? Are they enforced? Should the law compel every one to be vaccinated? Should schools be kept open during an epidemic? 

Are children any safer from contagious diseases on the streets than in school? 

36. Securing Pure Air. In our homes this is usually a fairly simple matter-opening the windows. opening the windows. Put screens in to keep out flies and mosquitoes, but let's have the air. The old notion. that night air is unhealthful was responsible for many foolish customs in our older families. 

In the school or public meeting-place, pure air may be provided by fans or other artificial methods. Sometimes the air is washed by passing through a series of water sprays and then drawn through driers to take out some of the moisture. No school or factory official should be satisfied with poorly ventilated rooms. 

In some cities the owners of lots are forbidden to put up buildings covering more than a certain per cent of the entire space. 

Keeping the streets clean and reducing the amount of coal smoke also help. In pure dry air tuberculosis germs do not thrive. For this reason we have in many places open-air schools for children who have weak lungs.

Courtesy Albuquerque Civic Council

ALBUQUERQUE AND ITS SURROUNDINGS 

In the dry climate of this part of the country sufferers from tuberculosis find special relief. Surroundings like those of Albuquerque make for other reasons a special appeal to many people. 

What is the proper treatment for a house or a schoolroom that has no special ventilating system? If a room is too warm or too cold, can you work efficiently in it? Does a room have to be noticeably cool in order to be well ventilated? 

How LOS ANGELES SECURED WATER IN THE DAYS WHEN IT WAS A LITTLE MEXICAN VILLAGE 

37. Furnishing Good Light. -Light and air go together so regularly that in thinking of one we are almost forced to think of the other. We all know what happens to a plant that is shut up in the dark. 

It seems reasonable to think that human beings need light just as truly as plants. 

One's eyes are too valuable to take chances with them. 

If we try to read in light that is too dim, our eyes are strained; and strained eyes may lower our general health

and permanently injure our sight. In any good schoolroom, windows are arranged so that they are at the side or back of the room, rather than the front. Why? 

Artificial light should not be too glaring, for a reflection from a shiny page to the eye is harmful. 

Is there any difficulty in your home in having a proper supply of light by day or in the evening? 

What do you think of the comparative merits of side lights, ceiling lights, or floor lamps as a source of artificial light? Are your eyes perfectly cared for? 

Does it make any difference where one buys glasses? 

38. Providing Pure Water. -Every spring, brook, or other source of water supply should be frequently tested to determine whether it contains bacteria that will cause disease. 

Whole towns have suffered from typhoid fever that came through impure water. If you are ever in doubt about the quality of the water you have 

Courtesy Los Angeles Department of Water and Power

A LONG-DISTANCE WATER SUPPLY 

This is a typical steel section of the 250mile aqueduct from the Sierra Nevada

Mountains to the city of Los Angeles. It is possible that Los Angeles may also use water from the Colorado River to supplement this particular source. to drink, the safest plan is to boil it to kill the dangerous germs.

Big cities have a very serious problem in providing drinking water for their people. If they are situated on a river, that is the easiest place to obtain it. But how can the people be sure that this river water is fit to drink?

Many of our large cities have constructed filtration plants, and they have meant life instead of death to thousands of our people every year. Pittsburgh, for instance, once had a worse death rate

from typhoid fever than any other large city in the country. It put in a fine filtration plant; now typhoid rarely occurs. 

Filtration is a fairly simple process. It means causing the water to pass through a substance, usually sand, which will draw out the impurities. Often it is also treated with chlorine or some other chemical to make the work more thorough. 

Courtesy Airmap Corporation of America A PART OF NEW YORK CITY'S WATER SUPPLY 

Kensico Reservoir is thirty miles from the southern end of Manhattan Island. 

Every day millions of gallons of water are pumped from here to the city. It is just one part of New York City's water supply. 

Some large cities have constructed exceedingly costly water sys- New York gets its water in the Catskill Mountains. Los 

Angeles brings pure water from the Owens River through an open canal and a great conduit over 200 miles into the city. Chicago and Cleveland both get their water from the lakes on which they are situated.

It would not be necessary to filter all the water used in factories or houses for other purposes than drinking; but to have two systems of water supply would be very expensive. The only practical way, in most cases, is to filter the whole supply and to distribute the water for all uses through the same mains and branch pipes.

Do you know any people who use bottled water? Why do they use it? Visit the filtration plant of your community if it has one. 

If it does not, where does your water supply come from? In either case how does it get to your school or your house? What cautions may you need to observe about water when away from home? Is clear water necessarily good to drink or muddy water harmful? 

39. Obtaining Pure Food. The national government can deal only with those food products that are carried in interstate commerce that is, from one state 

to another. In 1906 the Food and 

Drugs Act was passed, which forbade the shipment in interstate commerce of goods which were not labeled so as to make clear what all their ingredients were, or goods which contained certain harmful chemicals. The national Department of Justice enforces this act against violators, after investigations are made under the Food and 

Drug Administration of the De partment of Agriculture.

Courtesy Roswell Chamber of Commerce AN ARTESIAN WELL 

In some parts of the country which seem hopelessly dry on the surface. wells are sunk far down into the earth and a plentiful supply of water obtained. Of course it is kept under control and distributed by pipes, pumps, and other machinery. This well, near Roswell, New Mexico, is said to be the largest of its kind in the 

The Bureau of Animal Industry, in the national Department of Agriculture, has charge of the inspection of animals in the great stockyards, and of meat sold by the packing houses of Chicago, Omaha, Kansas City, and other middle-western places. In fact, some federal inspector is on duty in every important center where meat or live cattle are found which are carried in interstate commerce. 

State boards of health frequently have some responsibility in the matter of pure food. In state governments, too, we frequently find bureaus of dairy and meat inspection which try to watch the milk or meat produced or sold within that state. The governments of large cities maintain similar agencies. world.

Very little of the milk used in a large city comes from cows kept there; most of it is gathered from dairy farms many miles away. 

The lives of babies, in particular, depend upon the purity of the milk 

A FEDERAL INSPECTOR AT WORK 

This man has to inspect the meat before it can be sold to the public. which is fed them. Most milk sold in cities now is pasteurized. That is, it is subjected In to a temperature of about 145 degrees for a half-hour. this way the dangerous bacteria are disposed of.

It is easy to say that we ought to eat fresh vegetables, but city people could not raise more than a few heads of lettuce or a few hills of beans in their back yards. That is why "truck gardening" has come to be a very extensive business. 

Farmers who live near large cities sometimes take their products into the city markets themselves, but the truck gardeners of North Carolina and other southern states must ship their goods north by rail. To keep meats and vegetables fresh requires the use of refrigerator cars and other means of preserving their natural qualities. 

Another problem is getting food on the table in good condition. 

Perhaps some of you have already studied home economics in school, and have learned how many calories your potatoes and lamb chops contain and the best methods of cooking them. Some families, it is true, care nothing about the quality of the food they eat so long as they have enough of it. 

Who would be satisfied with a wife who knew nothing about cooking? What does the word calories mean, as applied to food? Do most people read the labels on the goods that they buy? If not, is the Food and Drugs Act of any value?

Can you estimate how many people have a part in furnishing your breakfast? What mistakes in the kind or amount of food used are made by people of your acquaintance? Would your own

food habits be a good model for others? Is it good that so many people get much of their food out of cans? 

40. Disposing of Wastes. Many kinds of waste material will produce conditions dangerous to our health if they are not disposed of. There is liquid waste, such as rain water and the drainage from kitchens and bathrooms; and there are the various kinds of solid waste matter ashes, garbage, and rubbish. Garbage 

PASTEURIZING TANKS IN A BIG MILK STATION 

Every precaution for cleanliness seems to have been taken here. includes the waste animal and vegetable matter that will promptly decay, and rubbish includes the other solid waste material like paper, tin cans, old furniture, and the like. 

On the farm, rain water is generally allowed to flow off naturally, with such assistance as roadside ditches and occasional drain pipes in the field may give. The disposal of sewage has been neglected too often. It used to be common for a little wooden drain to take the dishwater and washing water from the kitchen a few feet from the house, and let it run on the ground. The outdoor toilet has been altogether too conspicuous on many a farm, and is often inconvenient and usually dirty. 

This sort of thing need not continue. Almost every farm has a

water supply that will take the water into the second floor of a farmhouse, perhaps with the assistance of a pump. When this is the case, cost is the only excuse for not having as convenient a bathroom as any city home can have. The disposal of the kitchen water may call for more piping than once was thought necessary. 

All this waste matter can run into what is known as a septic tank, which will bring about purification in a very short while. 

The larger village community and the city will need to have a system of underground sewers to dispose of the rain water and other liquid waste. Where a city is situated on the bank of a river, the most convenient thing is to let the sewers empty into the stream. 

This is not always the safest method; for while the stream will purify itself after traveling some distance, one community may have to take a chance on another's sewage. 

In some communities the sewage is spread over a considerable amount of ground so that the air can get at it. Some other places have a special sewage disposal plant where air is artificially forced through the waste matter. After being thus purified the sewage can be made into fertilizer or some other product. 

The larger the community becomes, the more necessary it is for the local government to handle the ashes, garbage, or rubbish. The community can employ its own workers to collect and get rid of this material or it can make a contract with a private individual or company to do the work. The rubbish is sorted for everything worth saving, and the matter which is of no value is burned. The garbage may be put to a variety of uses. The fats can be used in making soaps and even cheap perfume. Sometimes what is left after the fats have been removed is made into fertilizer. 

In small towns and rural districts every family has to be responsible for wastes. They must see that garbage does not pile up in the back yard. It breeds flies and encourages rats and other vermin to come near or even into houses. The public dump is often about as disgusting-looking a place as one could imagine. 

If waste material is not properly disposed of by burning, it should be buried and not allowed to decay through exposure to the air.

What regulations does your community have in regard to the treatment of garbage and rubbish? What happens to the ashes in

your community? If you are on an automobile tour, what conditions should you be careful of that may affect your health?

No city has any right to call itself sensible or up-to-date if it tolerates slums.

43. Caring for the 

Handicapped. The blind, the deaf, the dumb, the crippled, and the insane and feeble-minded, are sometimes known collectively as the defective people who are lacking some normal faculty or power. Such people often need to be placed in some special institution in order to receive proper attention. 

Many blind, deaf, and crippled people can do a considerable amount of work. The blind have remarkable talent in pianotuning, furniture-weaving, wickerwork, and the like. The deaf and dumb are still less handicapped because they can engage in anything that does not require taking or giving orders by voice. 

Helen Keller, deaf, dumb, and blind from infancy, graduated from Radcliffe College and became an author. 

In special schools for the blind a course of study is usually fol-

lowed much like that of the regular schools. Textbooks and globes are used, and raised lines or dots mean just as much to the blind person as printed lines or dots do to the ordinary student. 

Deaf people can often learn to understand speech by watching a person's lips or the muscles in his throat and can carry on conversation by sign language. 

Do the blind and deaf like to have us pity them? 

Can you imagine what it would be like to lose your sight? 

Courtesy American Braille Press, Inc. 

The soldiers wounded in the 

World War have special attention from our government. 

After they came back home, those who had been injured were given training in schools where they might learn a trade. 

All the work of the government in connection with the paying of pensions or other aid to soldiers is under the direction of the Veterans' Administration. 

A GAME FOR THE BLIND 

This girl can play dominoes as well as anybody. The marks indicating their number are raised so that one can easily distinguish them. 

What do you understand by "rehabilitation work" in connection with government activities? What does your community do for crippled children? 

44. Caring for the Mentally Sick. We use the word hygiene as meaning the proper care of the body. There is such a thing as mental hygiene too, for people can be sick mentally and need a very definite sort of treatment. At one time such people were given practically no attention. If they were allowed to go free, people made fun of them, and their life was an exceedingly sad one. If they were shut up in an institution, they often received less humane care than a farmer would give his cattle. Today we do much better.

There are two kinds of mental defectives - the insane and the feeble-minded - who are distinctly different in character and require wholly different treatment. The insane person is one who once had full possession of his mental faculties but has wholly or partly lost control of them. The feeble-minded are those born without normal intelligence. 

Sometimes a person may be insane on one point and perfectly normal in every other respect. About one-third of the insane can hope to be cured. But while they are suffering from their trouble, they need to be watched in order that they harm neither themselves nor others. Usually the best place for such persons is in an institution for the insane. Some such places are private hospitals; others are state or county institutions. 

The feeble-minded are of three classes. The moron may have as much intelligence as a twelve-year-old child and is sometimes hard to distinguish from an ordinary "slow-minded" person. 

The imbecile never gets any further mentally than an eight-yearold child. The idiot is always an infant mentally, not passing the mental age of two years.

The feeble-minded are not particularly dangerous, but are often a nuisance to normal people. It is very desirable not to allow them to marry, for a great proportion of the children of such people are feeble-minded also. If the feeble-minded receive proper treatment in well-managed institutions, they can live with reasonable comfort and be of some service in supporting themselves. 

Should feeble-minded children attend public schools? Why or why not? What institutions for the mentally sick are maintained by your city, county, or state? Visit one of these if possible. Does your state government have a department of mental hygiene? 

45. Restraining Bad Habits. It is hard to understand why people eat or drink things that they know are not good for them, or become addicted to habits that weaken them physically and morally. "Dope" and intoxicating liquors almost invariably injure the health, morals, and reputation of a person. Too much smoking is bad for anybody, and for young people any smoking is too much. 

Which is better to break bad habits or to prevent them? How will following health laws prevent bad habits?

The person who becomes subject to such a habit as "dope taking" finds it almost impossible to break himself of it. He must have his opium or cocaine. We therefore have a national Drug 

Act which requires keeping strict records of all sales of dangerous habit-forming drugs, and prohibits the sale of them except under a physician's prescription. The local police officers also have the duty of enforcing city and state ordinances against the illegal distribution of such harmful drugs.

Point out the evils connected with the use of drugs and intoxicating liquor, physically and morally. 

The distress and poverty caused by liquor led several states, about the middle of the 19th century, to pass laws forbidding the manufacture or sale of intoxicating liquors as a beverage. Maine was the first to do so; it has had such a prohibition law ever since 

1846. Several other states - Kansas, for example had pro- 

hibition under state laws for fifty years or more. Other states tried to restrain the liquor business by requiring everybody who engaged in the business to take out a license, and limiting the number of licenses to be granted. 

Some states adopted local option, and allowed each town or city or county, as the case might be, to decide by popular vote whether saloon licenses should be granted. By 1920, thirty-two of the states (two-thirds of the whole number) had adopted state-wide prohibition by their own vote.

Many people felt, however, that the only way to make prohibition effective was to adopt it as a national policy. In 1917, Congress submitted to the states the Eighteenth Amendment to the national Constitution forbidding the manufacture, sale, or transportation of intoxicating liquors within the country, or their importation into the country, for beverage purposes. The Amendment went into operation January 16, 1920.

There have been many arguments as to the success of national prohibition, and numerous proposals for its abandonment or modification. Some people insist on the right to drink. Others saw a chance to make "big money" by manufacturing and selling liquor to the thirsty ones. "Bootleggers" even organized thoroughly to defy the law by smuggling liquor into the country or distributing it here.

Prohibition doubtless accomplished much for a good many people. 

Yet so much sentiment was stirred up against the Eighteenth Amendment that Congress in February, 1933, submitted to the states a proposal for a Twenty-first Amendment which would do away with the Eighteenth. If this should be accepted by the states (Section 111), the whole business of restraining the liquor traffic would be turned back to the states again, except for one restriction that no liquor should be carried into any state in violation of its own laws. 

In most of Canada the government has the oversight of the sale of liquor. It is not allowed to be drunk at the places where it is sold. 

Most of the profits from the business go into the public treasury. 

What merits or disadvantages do you see in this plan?

No intelligent person can believe that one is better off for acquiring the liquor habit. Besides, the drinker's bad habit may affect many other people, and his earnings may be spent for liquor instead of keeping his family in comfort. A drunken automobile driver may menace the lives of scores of people on one trip. Can there be any doubt that a good citizen ought to be anxious to discover and then to support whatever policy would cut to the lowest possible point the damage done by this curse of mankind?

What are the laws of your state with reference to the enforcement of prohibition or the restraint of the sale of liquor? Congress has now defined intoxicating liquor so as to permit the sale of beer and light wines. What do you think of this arrangement? Can you be sure a man is not selling stronger stuff when he has the right to sell beer and wine?

Has the habit of joking about prohibition helped to create disrespect for it? Is there any difference in principle between prohibiting the use of drugs and of liquor? Should we repeal every law which is frequently violated?

46. Our Part in Keeping Healthy. — The majority of us are lucky enough to start out with healthy constitutions, and with a little care can keep ourselves in that condition most of the time. 

A person really has no right to overwork or overplay so much as to weaken his general endurance and strength. Some people can stand more physical exercise or nervous strain than others. It is

99 asserted that some school athletes "burn themselves out before they are twenty-five years old.

It is said that the Chinese think a doctor should be paid when his patients keep well, but get no compensation when they are sick. Is this a sensible idea?

People seldom take pride today in being sick, for we feel that the normal person ought to be well. Girls no longer think it is ladylike to faint when they see a mouse. 

But now that a girl may do so many things by way of exercise and recreation that once only boys did, let us hope that she will not think it necessary or smart to adopt the bad habits of men and boys. 

If one, even after taking good care of himself, gets sick, he ought to consult a doctor rather than try to cure himself. Some who know that they are not well, instead of going to some reputable doctor, take quart after quart of some highly advertised patent

medicine. In the long run they pay more for "Doctor Smith's 

Guaranteed Purifier " than they would have to pay for the advice or prescription of a doctor who knows his business. 

We can learn something about the symptoms of common diseases and know what to do when such symptoms appear. We can try to provide ourselves with pure air, good light, pure water, and good food. No medicine has yet been discovered that will keep anybody alive forever. But we can be a much healthier nation than we are. To keep our minds free from unpleasant and low thoughts, to "think health," and to practice what we know about it, will do wonders.

47. The Importance of Recreation. 

- We have come to the 

point today where we believe it is a good thing for everybody mill worker, clerk, or business man to take a day off occasionally and not only a day, 

but a week or two. You remember that one of the seven objectives of education was said to be the wise use of leisure time. That implies that everybody does or should have leisure time "time when there 

is nothing that you have to do." If this leisure time is to benefit rather than harm, it should be used sensibly. 

Do you think every one should plan for some leisure time? Does everybody have it? Can we have too much? 

Vacation, amusement, and recreation do not always seem the same thing. What we really need is re-creation of the vigor of mind and strength of body which make it possible for us to perform our daily tasks. Any amusement or way of spending time which does not do this is a waste of hours, money, and vitality. Recreation should usually be a contrast to the individual's regular

48. Public Provision for Recreation. The community which tries to give extra pleasures and benefits to its people is most likely to have happy, healthy, and orderly citizens. Sometimes a wealthy man will play Santa Claus for the town where he was born or where he made his money. John D. Spreckels did this for San 

Diego in presenting the city with a wonderful organ for open-air concerts. Andrew Carnegie put millions of dollars into a fine museum, music hall, and library for Pittsburgh. Sometimes a small community is similarly blessed. 

Parks are the most common provision which communities make for public recreation. Here one may rest in the open air and get as near to nature as is possible in a large city. The greenhouses and conservatories which many parks possess are educational and interesting, and Grandpa as well as Billy likes to watch the monkeys and elephants at the zoo.

Should the main idea in a park be use or beauty? Should everybody be obliged to "keep off the grass"? Does your community have a public park? Describe any that you have visited in other places.

Probably nothing has done more to improve the life of the poorer districts of a community than the opening of public playgrounds.

50. Our Own Attitude toward Recreation and Amusement. 

Even though one goes to the right sort of place for recreation, it is too bad if he thinks he must depend on some one else for entertainment. A little child often seems able to amuse himself more satis-

factorily than grown-ups. The automobile has made it possible for us to get out into the open air and see places and things that otherwise we could not. If, however, it makes people so lazy that they do not know how to walk any farther than the garage, it is not so good. 

A WORLD SERIES GAME 

People crowd to see these games and often pay big prices for admission. They are described over the radio and given pages of description in our great newspapers. 

Why? Are you interested?

There are all sorts of temperaments among men, women, and children. One person finds nothing pleasanter than sitting in a boat out on the lake or by the shore and holding a fish-pole. It may make little difference to him whether he gets a bite or not; he is having a change. Even in the city, there may be room in one's back yard for a small garden. For another who wants more lively recreation there may be opportunities for golf, tennis, or quoits, so that one can hardly excuse himself for not having some recreation.

Let us be sure that we adapt our play, or leisure, or recreation

to our own needs.

Let us cultivate some kind of regular interest outside of our daily tasks - call it a hobby, an avocation, or what you will. Perhaps it is nothing but stamp-collecting; perhaps it is making something out of packing boxes in the cellar. 

Recreation, if it takes the form of games, often brings up another question. Are we good sportsmen? Do we go into a tennis match or a baseball or football game for the sake of the exercise we get out of it or merely with the idea of beating somebody? If we win, do we try to "rub it in "? If we lose, do we habitually complain about the umpire or referee? Too often athletics in our schools comes to be more like a war than a sport. Recreation should do much good to those who take part in it and to the community to which they belong but should not cause physical or moral harm to any one.

Are the people who get recreation the ones who are most likely to need it? How do you feel toward your home as a place for recreation or amusement? Why does a child so often seem to be able to amuse himself more readily than older people? Does the right spirit exist in your community and in your school toward rivals in sport? Can your school do more than it is doing to promote the right spirit in athletics? To what extent should recreation for girls be the same as or different from that of boys? Do spectators at games get any benefit themselves? Are they of any benefit to the players? Should there be scholarship requirements for members of school teams? 

An all-round citizen has physical, moral, intellectual, material interests, attains an all-round life through striving for the development of all his talents and interests.

Health is protected and encouraged by realizing its value, developing a correct attitude toward disease, community coöperation to prevent and control disease, providing the conditions needed for good health, such as pure air, good light, pure water, and pure food, arranging for sanitary disposal of wastes, providing healthful environment in which people may live, giving proper care to the sick, caring for those unable to provide fully for themselves, giving special attention to those who are sick in mind,

restraining and preventing the formation of harmful habits, practicing health rules and a healthy attitude toward life. 

Recreation promotes our physical and moral welfare through supplying the change in one's activities that renews mind and body, community coöperation to provide for all opportunities for healthful exercise and change, encouraging proper conditions in which private recreation may be carried on, maintaining for ourselves the best ideals and standards for the use of leisure time.

CHAPTER VII HOW THE COMMUNITY PROTECTS US 

Freedom is not to be secured by disobedience to law; it is not only to the advantage of others that the citizen is to obey the law, but to his own advantage. His obedience is his own protection. - COOLIDGE

Coöperation of all good citizens is tremendously important. But suppose we find some people who are not willing to coöperate who are so selfish or reckless that they do not care what happens to the lives and property of decent men and women. What are we to do in a case like that? No one person is likely to be strong enough to guard himself against the carelessness and wrongdoing of bad citizens. 

Perhaps even more than in promoting our health must we expect our governments to serve us in protecting life and property. 

A. PROTECTING LIFE AND PROPERTY 

What are some of the ways in which life and property are endangered? 

How much of this danger is chargeable to our own carelessness?

What kinds of accidents most often occur, and how may they be prevented?

What public officials are particularly responsible for promoting safety? 

How may losses from fire be guarded against or controlled? 

What can citizens do for their own safety? 

51. Menaces to Life and Property. — Recklessness and selfish ness are two great menaces to security. Mr. I. M. Careless lights a cigarette, throws down the match, and looks only too late to see whether the flame is out when it strikes the ground or floor.

Reckless Reuben drives his automobile sixty miles an hour on a crowded highway. Greedy, Dishonest, & Co. put up buildings so hastily that they will not bear the weight which they should, or will go up in smoke within a few minutes after a flame is started. 

Even civilization, you see, has brought dangers that did not exist in pioneer days. 114

The selfish person has no regard for what happens to anybody else. That is why we have to have laws to protect others from such selfishness. Sometimes, also, unpleasant results occur when people keep "within the law" but disregard entirely the finer unwritten law of good will to one's neighbors. 

Is it ever a citizen's duty to be more careful or do more than the law commands him? Is it ever to his interest to do so? 

A third menace is sometimes referred to as an "act of God," though it is hardly fair to call it that. 

The expression refers to natural calamities that are not controllable by man, such as earthquakes, tornadoes, and floods. Some floods are the result of human carelessness, but others seem to be beyond the power of man to prevent.

George Washington camped in 1754. It is as nearly like Washington's blockhouse as possible. 

Point out dangers to men's comfort and welfare in colonial times and compare them with the dangers of today. Mention other matters than health about which good citizens ought always to be on guard. Why is a sense of safety important to a community, business man, child in school, traveler, and any others whom you think of?

52. The Cost of Our Thoughtlessness. 

foolish indulgences is our recklessness with fire. year enough buildings are consumed by the flames to line a street stretching from New York to Chicago, certainly we are overdoing the matter. The property value that is destroyed in one year in this country has jumped almost to the half-billion figure. Ten thousand people lose their lives; several times as many more are thrown out of work and get no wages; they and their families suffer - and mostly because of carelessness.

We have a very extensive system of fire insurance; but at the best, property destroyed by fire is gone. If the owner gets insurance money for it, somebody has had to pay that money to the insurance companies. They do not insure houses out of love. 

They make a profit as well as protect people.

SUICIDES AUTOCIDES ONE THING THE AUTOMOBILE Does 

We talk a great deal about crime and rightly lament the number of murders in this country. 

But this diagram shows that more people are killed by autos than are murdered and commit suicide.

Automobiles have caused many accidents. 

New York City in one year had almost 100,000 accidents. Sometimes the brakes were not in good condition. In about 

12,500 cases a motorist did not have the right of way. In other instances the driver was going too fast on a crowded or slippery highway. If you drive much in the country, you will find evidences there also of case after case in which somebody's car and perhaps a driver or passenger gets badly bumped. Thirty-three thousand people killed and about one million injured in one year (1930) in this country is too heavy a price to pay for whatever thrill there may be in reckless driving. 

What are the laws of your state in regard to "the right of way"?

Draw a picture of a street intersection that will make this clear. 

Does your state require an annual inspection of automobiles? How much does it amount to? 

53. "Safety First." - People need to be constantly instructed and watched in order to prevent accidents from machinery. Machinery must be equipped with proper safety devices so that it

will not easily catch the clothing or limbs of the workers. Formerly one of the most dangerous duties of a railroad brakeman was to go between cars and put in a coupling pin to hold two cars together. Now automatic couplers make that unnecessary. 

Trainmen must not work more than sixteen hours out of twenty-four; for if they do, they become too tired to perform their duties properly.

Courtesy Safety Education 

Ships that carry passengers must have some kind of life preserver for every passenger, and enough lifeboats to accommodate the entire passenger list. Practice drills are held, when the crew and the passengers are expected to go to the place on deck where they would report if the boat were in serious danger. When airplanes become a common means of transportation, similar provisions will doubtless be made. Parachutes do no good unless they can be used far enough up so that they will open readily.

SAFEGUARDING WORKERS 

The wheels and some other parts which might catch a worker's clothes or limbs are covered so that there is very little danger.

Miners, especially in coal mines, are in danger from gas or the dreaded "fire damp." They may be overcome by the gas, or it may be ignited and explode. Using electric lights has reduced greatly the danger from this source. The Federal government and some of the states have experimental mines or other means of finding ways to make mines safer. 

Washing machines, vacuum sweepers, mangles, and many other electrical devices in our homes are simple and safe to the watchful operator. Isn't it too bad that some people are so careless in their use of them? It is usually carelessness that leads to caught fingers or burnt arms or the other deplorable accidents that occur.

All the safety laws in the world are useless unless workers accept the responsibility of obeying them. Some who do a dangerous piece of work every day become so accustomed to it that they get careless." Safety-first" campaigns are somewhat common.

Safety-first" placards may be posted throughout a mill and a record kept of the amount of work done without accident. Railroads and mining companies sometimes offer prizes to the division or department that has the fewest accidents in a certain period. 66 

Courtesy Hawaii Tourist Bureau

THE PARKING PROBLEM 

This is a scene from the city of Honolulu. Evidently that city, like most of those on the mainland, finds it difficult to accommodate its automobiles. 

The tremendous increase in the number of automobiles has made necessary many regulations in regard to their operation. Most states issue licenses only after the drivers have been tested, and reserve the right to revoke a license for drunkenness or reckless driving. The traffic problem in some places would be serious enough, even if everybody tried to be careful. Red, yellow, and green lights at important crossroads, and warning signs, such as "Sharp Curve," "Steep Grade," "Bad Crossing," and "School -Go Slow," also remove people's alibis for reckless driving.

Pedestrians as well as motorists need to observe traffic regulations. "Jay-walkers" who cross city streets outside of the regularly marked lines not only risk their own lives but cause unfair worry to motorists. When a boy rushes recklessly out into the street for a ball or catches a ride on the rear of a truck, the driver may not be at all to blame for what happens. 24.5 39 15.4 14.6 13.0 10.2 7.8 6.0 4.9 1913 1930 5.0 2.5 2.6

54. Fire Fighting. There is a good reason why the fire departments of the United States are the best in the world. They have to be!

The "bucket brigade," which passed pails from hand to hand, from a river or well to the fire, was once the best a small community could do, but often it meant not much more than sympathy. 

The pump on wheels which could be dragged to the scene of the fire was an improvement. The volunteer fire companies, which 

Los Angeles Adopts the 

Telephone as a Fire Alarm

December 28 1882 

Courtesy American Telephone and Telegraph Company

A FIRE ALARM IN THE OLD DAYS 

It used to be the custom in Los Angeles to fire five shots with a revolver as a fire alarm. You see the men responding just as they used to do. Los Angeles substituted the telephone for this method on the date given above. assembled as soon as they could after an alarm was given, and drew a fire engine to the fire by long ropes, were still better; but no large city today would care to trust itself to them. 

We must have regular companies always on duty. We have fire-alarm systems, so that fire companies may learn promptly where a fire is. We have engines, hook and ladder trucks, and chemical apparatus which, if used soon enough, can put out a fire without causing the damage that comes from hurling thousands of gallons of water. The larger cities also have water-towers and fire-boats for getting at a fire which is blazing in an awkward place. 

Gasoline power is displacing the faithful Dan and Mike which once drew the fire engines.

The number of engine-houses and firemen needed by a city depends somewhat on the ground on which the city is built.

Hilly cities, like Pittsburgh, Cincinnati, or San Francisco, need more than a level city. When we see the firemen sitting around in the engine-house playing cards or looking at the scenery, we may wonder why we pay taxes to get men to do that. But within ten minutes these same men may be risking their lives to save others, and their skill and courage may keep a whole city from destruction. 

What personal qualities are needed in a fireman? What provisions does your community have for fighting fire? What are some local ordinances intended to prevent unnecessary damage and delay in case of fire? 

55. Fire Prevention. 

Nothing can send through us a more awful thrill of alarm than a cry of "Fire" in the night. When some factory, school, or moving picture theater burns and a dozen or so people lose their lives, we become very much excited. Then we put up fire-escapes, hold fire drills, and inspect public buildings for a while.

But our memories are short and we go back to our old habits. 

A few fires cannot be prevented by any ordinary foresight. But it is estimated that not over 5 per cent of all the property destroyed by fire in an ordinary year needed to be destroyed if human beings had been careful.

After all, the proper way to fight fires is to see that they do not get started. Most of the large cities now establish "fire limits," within which they do not allow any large wooden buildings to be erected. Theaters must have asbestos curtains to shut off the auditorium from the stage and the rest of the building. Automatic sprinklers are installed in many business places. Stairways and air shafts must be of fireproof material as far as possible.

In public buildings, the aisles must always be kept open, doors must open outward, and there must be many fire exits, plainly marked. Many of these requirements must be observed in the construction of tenements. Schools must have fire drills so that every one in the building may get out in the quickest and most orderly fashion.

Fires in homes can be almost entirely prevented. The lists of "Don'ts" which are issued by fire officials from time to time are so simple that they seem almost silly. Fireplaces or gas stoves unprotected by some kind of screen are responsible for many fires

and even deaths, especially of children. The handling of explosives or matches is to blame for many more. Electric wiring, if done by some one who does not know his business, is dangerous. 

Putting hot ashes in wooden boxes or piling up wood or paper near a stove or furnace is a frequent cause of fire. 

Courtesy U. S. Bureau of Standards TESTING WHAT A FIRE WILL DO 

With this building, experiments are made in regard to the intensity and duration of fires. It can be fitted to represent any desired kind of building. 

Fires are not so common in European cities because the governments pay more attention to the inspection of private houses and business places than we do, and strictly enforce the laws. Here we have almost no inspection of private houses, for Americans think that this is inquisitive meddling into private affairs and resent it and have to take the consequences. 

Some cities have bureaus of building inspection which make factory owners and proprietors of tenement houses comply with the laws. Three-fourths of the states also have fire marshals, who may make regulations for fire prevention and may inspect any factory, store, or office building to see that the regulations are obeyed. 

They also investigate the causes of fires that have occurred. 

Can July 4 be properly celebrated without danger from fire?

What would be the effect on fire insurance if people obeyed the cautions set forth by fire marshals?

56. Farm and Forest Fires. It is not only in the city that there are serious fires. The farmer whose barn and house may be a mile or more from any other building is out of luck" if they get on fire; for, unless the fire is discovered very promptly, it is likely to get such a start that the buildings cannot be saved. The only advantage that the farmer has is that his own buildings are not likely to catch fire from any one else's. 

Courtesy U. S. Forest Service WHAT A FOREST FIRE CAN DO 

Think of the damage wrought in the Flathead National Forest, Montana, by fire. 

Can these trees be replaced?

Lightning is responsible for many rural fires. 

Much less damage is done by lightning in the cities and towns than in the country. 

Big city buildings are constructed so largely of iron and steel that when they are struck, the electricity may pass harmlessly to the ground. Lightning is also the cause of a considerable number of our forest fires. When a rain comes after a dry spell, it is sometimes accompanied by lightning which starts a blaze that not even the rain can put out promptly.

The loss from forest fires is serious. Careless campers and picnickers throw matches or cigarettes on the ground or fail to put out fires which they build. What a calamity it is when their

carelessness ruins, in a few minutes, trees that have been growing for forty, fifty, or even hundreds of years! In our national and state forests there are guards who are constantly on the watch for fires. They have "look-outs" on high places from which they can see over a wide range of country.
