57. What Each of Us Can Do. Each one of us can avoid 

the careless things that cause fires open grates, the reckless handling of cigarettes or cigars and matches, leaving materials where spontaneous combustion may start, using things that will ignite easily near fires.

If you discover a fire in your house, don't get excited. If it is a small blaze, perhaps you can put it out with some water or a fire extinguisher. If there is the least indication that the fire may RAIL

become serious, use the telephone or the nearest alarm box to notify the fire departmentor your nearest neighbors if you have no fire department. 

In an emergency of this kind remember that it is more important to save your life than your Sunday suit or your jewelry. Obey the directions that are given to you by the firemen or other responsible persons. 

If you are in school, go on with your fire drill as you have done many times in practice. When in a public place look for the nearest exit. More people are hurt in a fire than are actually burned when a panic occurs and everybody rushes for the main doorway.

We can help ourselves and the community, too, by being careful at play. Altogether too many accidents occur in school buildings and playgrounds.

Running through the halls is one inexcusable cause. Exercise in the gymnasium should improve one's health; but if a child fools around with apparatus not meant for him, the results may be disastrous. At gymnasiums, swimming pools, and other places which children or adults may use for exercise and pleasure there are always supposed to be teachers or guards on duty, but they cannot be looking in all directions at all times. 

Obedience to traffic rules would have saved a great many lives. 

We can let traffic go by and wait a half-minute before we cross the

street or the railroad track. Isn't it better to wait a half-minute than not to get there at all?

Automobile collisions would be fewer if every driver were perfectly careful in trying to avoid being hit or hitting some one else.

WHO AM I?

I have destroyed more than all the wars. 

I am more deadly than bullets, and I have wrecked more homes than 

Big Bertha.

I steal in the United States alone over $300,000,000 each year. 

I spare no one, and I find my victims among the rich and poor alike, the young and old, the strong and weak. 

I loom up to such proportions that I cast my shadow over every field of labor, from the turning of the grindstone to the moving of every railway train.

I massacre thousands upon thousands of wage-earners in a year. 

I lurk in unseen places, and do most of my work silently. You are warned against me, but you heed not. 

I am relentless.

I am everywhere—in the house, on the streets, in the factory, at railroad crossings, and on the sea. 

I bring sickness, degradation, and death, and yet few seek to avoid me. 

I destroy, crush or maim; I give nothing, but take all. 

I am your worst enemy.

I am CARELESSNESS. 

B. PROTECTING FROM WRONGDOERS 

58. Why People Do Wrong. Sensible Americans must be disgusted at the fact that there is much more crime in the United States than in most other civilized countries. It has been estimated that there is one murderer in about every 12,000 people in the United States and one murderer for about every 634,000 people in Great Britain. Almost daily the newspapers give us the distressing

impression that burglary, bootlegging, and even kidnaping are common occurrences.

An act that results in physical or moral harm to the person who commits it may be called a vice, such as dope-taking or gambling. 

An act which breaks what we believe to be a law of God is a sin, such as the violation of one of the Ten Commandments. The breaking of a law made by man we call a crime. Some misdeeds come under more than one head. Reckless profanity, for instance, would be both a vice and a sin, and in many places also a crime, though people are seldom fined for it. Murder or stealing is both a sin and a crime.

Most crimes are generally recognized as being morally wrong as well as contrary to law, but they are not necessarily so. Suppose your state has a law requiring the teaching of geography in your eighth grade. Your school board or your teachers become lawbreakers and therefore criminals if they do not teach it, though they may honestly believe that some other subject is more important just at that point.

Might a certain act or failure to act properly be a crime in one place and not in another? If a person does not know of a law and thus innocently breaks it, is he morally wrong? Can a hermit commit crime?

The more serious crimes are known as felonies; the less serious are misdemeanors. People may also commit acts that harm others without actually breaking any laws. Such cases are known as torts. A person who is thus injured has the right to bring charges against the wrongdoer in court and secure compensation for the wrong that has been done. 

Ask a lawyer to give you examples of each of the three kinds of cases just mentioned.

There are various explanations for crimes. 

1. The tendency to commit vice or crime sometimes seems to be inherited. 

2. Lack of proper training and education may lead a person to do improper acts because he knows no better. 

3. Bad habits, such as the use of liquor and drugs, sometimes turn men into beasts or take away their reason for a time.

4. Bad environment and social conditions are partly to blame for some crimes. People whose home surroundings are poor or who frequent gambling joints, speakeasies, cheap movies, and public dance halls of low types are under constant temptation to do wrong. 

5. Unemployment gets others into crime. They may be too lazy or inefficient to hold a job, or they may have been forced out by business conditions. Some steal in order to get food or clothing for themselves or their families, because they have a false pride that makes them unwilling to ask assistance of a welfare society. 

6. A low standard of public sentiment in a community also encourages crime. If the police or the people in general do not seem to care whether the laws are enforced or not, it will not be long before laws are broken openly. 

7. Some crimes, which may not do any great amount of personal harm, result from trying to get the better of others by sharp business practices. A storekeeper may give poor weight or measure for goods. A "sharper" may sell stocks or bonds based on an oil well or gold mine that existed only in some one's imagination. 

Is there likely to be such a thing as a crime wave? Do you think newspapers and movies are responsible for encouraging crime? 

Would it be better if murders and burglaries were not given prominent places in the newspapers? Is all crime committed in the slum districts or by residents of such places? How do you account for crimes committed by educated and wealthy people? 

59. Methods of Dealing with Criminals. Some of the ways and means of punishing criminals in the past seem inhuman and awful. No doubt at the time, however, they appeared just, or at least satisfactory to the majority of the people. How can one have any sympathy for some criminals! 

A very elementary idea was that of vengeance - the offender had wronged society and society would "get back at him." We are all familiar with the theory in the old religious code, "An eye for an eye, a tooth for a tooth, a life for a life." Was that unreasonable?

Somewhat higher than the motive of vengeance is the belief that an offender deserves punishment in accordance with his crime. 

For instance, a reckless, drunken auto driver deserves to have his. license taken from him.

To a certain degree the idea of deterring from crime was a further step in advance. A person might be afraid to commit crime if he thought he would have to pay a fine, or go to jail for it. The most advanced purpose, and a comparatively modern one, is the reformation of the criminal. Not all criminals are necessarily hopeless. Can we make them want to do right?

It does seem as if there are some individuals who have no respect for the rights of their fellow-men and whom it is useless to try to reform. The man who kills another and then asserts he did it while he was temporarily insane is another dangerous character. 

How can you tell when he is going to go crazy again, if he really did the first time? But a man who has done wrong and wants to begin over again and do right should receive every possible encouragement.

Do you think there is a difference between born criminals, habitual criminals, and occasional criminals? If you do, point out the distinction and tell how you would have each of these different classes treated. What do you think of the expression, "We should not care so much about fitting punishment to the crime as about fitting punishment to the criminal"? Can you tell from a person's looks whether he is likely to be a criminal? 

60. Institutions for Criminals. In the old days, when nothing was considered bad enough for a criminal, a favorite place was the dungeon built under the ground. No windows, no ventilation of any sort, a deadly dampness, rats by the score, and all kinds of filth, made a prisoner's life miserable. A prisoner who was hardy enough to keep alive had nothing to do but brood upon his fate. 

He either went mad or plotted revenge. We have had many poorly built, overcrowded, badly managed prisons, jails, and workhouses. Pounding rocks or building roads in a "chain-gang " is better than doing nothing in a filthy cell. 

Today, however, a noticeably different practice prevails in many places. Some would say we are almost in danger of swinging from one extreme to another. Sentimental women send flowers to "hard-boiled" murderers. One loafer said, "It's actually a pleasure to live in some jails."

The object of improving the jails is to develop in their occupants better ideas of living through the use of the school, the chapel, the

gymnasium, the athletic field, the radio, the library, and even the movie. Prisoners are often taught useful trades to which they can turn when they get out of prison. Prisons and workhouses may have factories for making brooms, chairs, or carpets. Their products are sold, and the workers may be allowed some pay for their service. We have the honor system in many of our prisons, and it 

Courtesy the Warden, Sing Sing Prison

A MODERN PRISON CELL BLOCK 

While it can hardly be a pleasure to be locked into a place like this, it is certainly equipped and cared for so as to protect the health of those who must stay here. often works well. Road gangs have been left alone for considerable periods with nothing but their honor, or perhaps the fear of being caught, to keep them from running away.

Sometimes a criminal is let out on probation - as long as he behaves in the right way, the punishment which he otherwise might receive will be withheld; and he can be released on parole - his promise to obey the law. Another principle frequently used is the indeterminate sentence. Its length depends upon the way the convict behaves in prison not less than one year nor more than five years, for example. 

What is capital punishment? Do you believe in it? New York

State passed an act known as the Baumes Law, which declared that

any person who was four times convicted of felonies should be given a life sentence in prison. What arguments do you suppose were used to pass such a law? Do you think it is a good one?

If a criminal really wants to start life over again properly, he should not be handicapped by having his past errors always brought up against him. Formerly when he left prison he was given a suit that fitted him like a kimono, and was turned out into 

Courtesy the Warden, Sing Sing Prison

KEEPING BUSY AT SING SING 

Mattresses of good quality are made here and used in state institutions. the world with every phase of his appearance suggesting that he had been a "jailbird." Few employers cared to hire such laborers. 

But now the best prison wardens try to keep in touch with men who have left their care, to help them get work, and to give them advice and counsel. 

61. Preventing Wrongdoing. Wouldn't it be still better to prevent crime before it happened? We hear of cases when one person is made an "example," as a suggestion to others of what they will get if they attempt to do as he did. But that does not touch "the moving why they do it."

We have already spoken of some of the reasons why people do wrong. Well, then, you say, get rid of the reason, and you get rid of the wrong. But in practice it is not so easy. Sometimes the aid of a medical man or a psychiatrist what does he do? - can 

---

help materially, as in the case of a person who has some little twist in his mental makeup that makes him do wrong things. There is something wrong with a person who steals rather than ask for help in a respectable way. But if we can find jobs for the unemployed who are willing to work, we shall at least remove the excuse for stealing.

The law tries to do something toward bettering men's environment. We may have censors to watch against movies and theaters which set up low standards of conduct. We may prohibit gambling joints and the sale of "dope" and intoxicating liquors. Yet the very people we are trying to help often do not appreciate the efforts we make to keep harm from them. 

Here again we see the need for coöperation. The efforts of a public officer to make conditions better may amount to little unless he has the sympathy of the public. Some people insist that they are being deprived of some of their liberty if we make it inconvenient for them to get drunk, or break up a baseball "pool" on which they lose money every week. Those who say they "can take it or leave it " usually do not "leave it." We need a little clearer thinking on some of these points. 

Go back to the causes or reasons for wrongdoing which we have mentioned and consider whether there can be preventative measures taken in connection with each cause or group of causes. If there can be, what measures do you suggest? 

62. Dealing with Young Offenders. The person who gets into criminal habits in his "tender years" needs a special kind of treatment. There was a time when no distinction was made between the boy who got into bad company and became a lawbreaker and those who were old hands" at the business. The result was very unfortunate. To throw the boy in with older criminals meant that he would leave the prison knowing far more about crime than when he entered. 66 Today in a well-governed community, young offenders are taken to a juvenile court, which handles all wrongdoers under twenty-one years of age. Here they can be wisely counseled and advised without much publicity. Sometimes it may be best to take them away from their homes. A profane or drunken father and a

shiftless mother are not likely to try very hard to make their children do right. 

If it is really necessary that the child be punished by sending him away for a while, he is not sent to a prison, but to a reform school. There he studies, is taught a trade, if that is what he needs, and in other ways is prepared to lead a useful life after he is released. A great many young offenders who, after talking with the judge, realize that they have done wrong and are sorry for it, are allowed to go free on probation.

Do you think that all offenders should be punished, no matter what their excuse may be? Tell something about the work of your local juvenile court, or some other such court. Make a list of the things that in your opinion might cause a young person to become a lawbreaker. Make a corresponding list of suggestions you would offer if you were giving good advice to such a person or trying to get him to change his ways. A recent writer of a book about criminals entitled it You Can't Win; what do you think he meant by it? 

63. Governmental Officials Who Protect Us. - Local, state, and national governments all furnish protection for the life, liberty, and property of our people. 

1. The local government. - Policemen not only chase criminals, but render many courtesies to people in need. To be sure, the traffic " cop on the corner may lose his temper and "bawl you out" for doing something that perhaps you were not entirely responsible for; but he may do that for the benefit of others to whom you may be setting a bad example. A policeman gives advice and directions to strangers or persons who do not know the town. He helps children and old people cross the street. He keeps order at public meetings. He walks his "beat" in fair weather and bad - always ready. Of course we are talking about policemen who are straight and vigilant themselves. A community is in a terrible state if its own officers break laws, or if they take bribes to let crooks and immoral people do as they please. 

In every well-organized police force there are some plain-clothes men, or detectives who do not wear uniforms and therefore cannot be recognized by criminals quite so easily. At the police station there are officers who keep records of persons whose history is bad. 

They take finger prints and personal measurements of those who

are arrested, so that there may always be a means of identifying criminals. Police women can handle some cases more successfully than men, particularly girls who have started on the wrong path. 

What qualities are particularly useful or necessary in a policeman? 

Would you like to be one? What should a child learn to think about a policeman? Why do policemen wear uniforms?

Frequently the police form a separate department of a city government. Sometimes they are combined with the Fire Department or some other group of officers, under the name of the Department of Public Safety. Their organization is patterned on the 

Courtesy the Warden, Sing Sing Prison

WHERE SOME MEN SPEND THEIR LAST DAYS 

This is the Death House at Sing Sing. The inmates of this house are not allowed to associate with other prisoners. They have their separate exercise yards and kitchen. plan of an army, with captains, lieutenants, and sergeants, although they are not considered military officers. Rural communities and small towns have constables who have the same sort of responsibility for guarding against criminals as city police do, but they have few other regular duties.

Policemen's salaries are often not very large. That is why policemen are sometimes tempted to take money from persons who want to break the law. In many cities there is a pension fund to which the policeman himself has to contribute and which

enables him to retire on half pay after perhaps twenty-five years' service. This is one of the ways of making up for the low salaries that the policemen get while on regular duty. 

Do you think policemen should carry guns? They are not allowed to do so in some cities. If not, would you allow them to carry any sort of weapon? Police Commissioner Whalen, of New

York City, once said, "There is a lot of law in the end of a night stick." Just what do you think he meant? In a primitive or frontier community, how are people's life and property protected? 

Is the satisfaction of rendering valuable public service partial compensation for a policeman's low salary? 

2. The state government. 

A state expects most of its laws to be enforced by the local police, sheriffs, or constables. The states themselves have a national guard or state militia which the local governments sometimes call upon.

A few states, of which New York and Pennsylvania were among the first, have a permanent police force known as the constabulary, state police, or state troopers. They have authority to enforce state laws anywhere within the limits of their state. Their services are particularly helpful in country districts which usually do not have police officers on regular duty. Special effort is made to select only the most brave and intelligent men. 

In some states private corporations that employ large numbers of men, such as coal-mining companies and great factories, are allowed to have private police forces. Do you see any danger in such a practice? If so, what safeguards would you set up? Find out the facts about the National Guard of your state and about the state police, if you have them. 

3. The national government. 

National officers are not so directly responsible for the protection of our lives and property as those of the state or local government. If a state is not able to protect itself and its people, the legislature of the state, or the governor, if the legislature is not in session, may ask the President to send United States soldiers into the state. The President also has the right to send soldiers, whether the state officials want them or not, if the laws of the United States are being disobeyed or ignored. 

For the protection of American citizens in foreign countries, the national government must accept responsibility. Sometimes

embarrassing questions arise when an American citizen goes into a foreign country to open up mines or oil wells and then gets into trouble. Some might say that 

Courtesy U. S. Bureau of Entomology WARRING AGAINST THE GYPSY MOTH 

These members of the staff of the Bureau of Entomology are studying trays of moth larvae in an effort to find parasites to use in destroying the gypsy moth. he ought to take his own chances if he goes into any such enterprise. Yet if any foreign country willfully denies to 

American citizens the rights that we grant to its citizens here, we should be rather cowardly to do nothing about the matter.

We have a consul in almost every large foreign city who gives advice and relief to our citizens in his neighborhood. 

Besides, most nations realize that their own reputations

Would suffer if tourists or business men should fail to receive courteous treatment. Americans today are as safe in London or Paris or Berlin or Istanbul as in New York or Chicago; and an Englishman or Frenchman or German is as well assured of security among us as our own citizens are. 

If you went to Russia to take charge of the building of an automobile factory there, would you expect the United States government to take a particular interest in your welfare while you were there? Why should the citizens of the United States or any other country be particularly careful to avoid getting into trouble when they are in a foreign land? 

Life and property are menaced by carelessness, selfishness, and natural calamities, are often in great danger from pure thoughtlessness, can be safeguarded in part by protective laws, are in this country, more than in some others, menaced by fire, deserve to be safeguarded by careful observance of law,

For another service of the Coast Guard, see the picture on page 324. are especially difficult to protect from fire in rural and forest regions, demand from each one of us a sense of personal responsibility. 

Protection from wrongdoers calls for understanding why people do wrong, requires worthy motives in dealing with wrongdoers, demands that wrongdoers be made good citizens if possible, would be greatly served by prevention of wrongdoing, calls for sincere and considerate treatment of young offenders. 

Officials who protect us include in the local government, policemen, detectives, constables, sheriffs, in the state government, national guard and state police, in the national government, soldiers, sailors, marines, who may be sent into the states or into foreign countries, and consuls whose services are ordinarily all that are necessary.

CHAPTER VIII GROWING IN KNOWLEDGE AND CULTURE 

He who knows not, and knows not he knows not, He is a fool - shun him; 

He who knows not, and knows he knows not, He is simple - teach him;

He who knows, and knows not he knows, He is asleep-wake him;

He who knows, and knows that he knows, He is wise follow him.

Wouldn't you think that a person needs to know a good deal in order to meet satisfactorily the opportunities and duties that come to him in his everyday community life? How is he going to obtain the knowledge and experience that will qualify him for recognizing old problems and facing new ones? This information and experience are what we often speak of as education. 

That kind of formal education which requires going to school has come to occupy such a large place in our youthful years that we must give it special attention. We must also include among those features of community life that educate us, not only the schools, but the libraries, museums, and other facilities that qualify us for intelligent living.

What qualities should a wise man possess in order to meet the problems which confront him?

From what sources may he obtain the training and experience which will develop these desirable qualities? 

What do we mean by education, and what should it do for us? 

What work does a school undertake, and what kinds of schools do we find in American communities?

How are our public schools organized and managed? 

Who attend our schools, willingly or otherwise?

What kinds of school buildings do we provide, and what should be offered in our courses of study? 

To what extent are we interested in advanced institutions of learning? 143

In what ways outside of formal schooling may we add to our knowledge and training?

What are our obligations in view of the opportunities that are provided for us so freely in this country? 

64. Some Qualities of a Wise Man. "Wisdom is the principal thing; therefore, get wisdom, and with all thy getting, get understanding." So said an ancient king who knew much better than 

A SCENE IN A PUBLIC LIBRARY 

This reading room in the Boston Public Library evidently serves a great many people who are anxious to improve themselves. he practiced. To become wise and understanding we need, for one thing, knowledge. We should know a considerable variety and quantity of facts. But knowledge is not enough. Along with it we must have intelligence. Intelligence is not so much the facts we possess as the ability to apply these facts to the conditions in which we find ourselves. "Knowledge is proud that he has learned so much; wisdom is humble that he knows no more.' What do you understand this to mean? Do you agree? What personal qualities will help one in gaining intelligence? 

The wise man will seek to acquire culture that quality which enables one to appreciate the finest and best elements in life.

It aids a person in distinguishing between what is merely show and what is really good, whether it be in ways of living or art. A cultured person, for instance, prefers that kind of music that appeals to his finest senses instead of that which expresses itself in mere noise and motion. Jazz may be his seasoning but not his regular diet. 

Is an intelligent person always cultured? What do you think of the statement, "Culture can be caught, not taught"? What opportunity do you have to improve the cultural side of your life? 

Is there a difference between culture and manners? 

The wise man is an educated man. Education does not mean simply obtaining information or going to school. It is the development of the talents and faculties of mind, body, and spirit. It is a process of training or a series of experiences. It may go on for an entire lifetime. While many of us get a great part of our education in school, none of us needs to let education come to an end when some one hands us a diploma. 

Is a person ever too old to go to school? Can there be education without a change in the person that is educated? Distinguish education from information, knowledge, intelligence. In what sense is it true that "experience is a great teacher"? 

65. How Valuable Is Education? What are the things we hope to do through education? You remember the list of the seven great objectives of education: health, command of fundamental processes, worthy home membership, vocational education, citizenship training, the wise use of leisure time, and ethical character. There is not much within the range of our interests that is not touched or covered by these seven objectives. If education can attain all these for us, it is surely worth having. 

Make a list which will include at least one example of some school activity with reference to each of the seven objectives. Put down at random the names of the first ten people that you think of outside of school. Then with each one figure out in what way education has been or ought to be helpful to him in connection with his particular interests. 

In a government like ours, which rests upon the will of the people, it is tremendously important that education should be broad and general. Since the votes of men and women decide how the

government shall be conducted, these voters should learn the things that will help them to pass judgment intelligently upon public questions.

How is an educated person likely to have an advantage over an uneducated person in earning a living, enjoying life, associating with others, practicing good citizenship?

The progressive nations of the world have seen to it that most of their people can at least read and write. Usually we define an illiterate person as one who is 10 years old or more and cannot read or write. In the countries of northern and western Europe, not more than one in a hundred of the entire population is illiterate. 

In the United States people have probably talked more about education than in any other country in the world, and yet we still have over four million illiterates. Think of it! A good many of our illiterates have come here from countries that have not done a great deal toward educating their people. But it is also a fact that those parts of our country which have the smallest percentage of foreign-born inhabitants also have some of the high figures in illiteracy. The record for the entire country showed a notable improvement from 6% of illiteracy in 1920 to 4.3% in 1930. 

Real education broadens one's range of thinking and makes him ambitious. How much the people of a community use their libraries; how much they care for music and art; how broad an interest they show in the problems of the world today - all are tests which would help one to decide the extent to which a community or nation is truly educated. 

From census reports make a list of the ten states that have the best record in illiteracy and the ten that have the worst. Show these on a map of the United States and try to determine what explanation there may be for the difference among the states. How does your own state stand in this matter? Make a similar comparison with reference to the percentage of pupils of school age who actually attend school. Do the same for the ten largest cities of the United States. 

66. Schools and Why We Have Them. In planning and guiding our education we are likely to get the best results if it is done by people who make that their specialty. A school may be defined as a place where teachers and pupils work together to attain

learning and experience that will prepare for doing well the work of life. 

Is the fact that Lincoln and Edison went to school only a few months any argument against our attending school? Make a list of ten things that you can do better than you otherwise could because you have attended school. Make a similar list of things which, as far as you can tell, the school has not helped you to do better. Which list is the more important? 

1. Public schools. -We have public schools mainly because the state feels that a large proportion of its citizens will not receive proper training unless it is offered by the state itself. Education promotes better and more intelligent citizenship. The public 

THE SENIOR HIGH SCHOOL, LITTLE ROCK, ARKANSAS 

High school buildings are often among the most imposing of any in a city. school is itself a course in democracy, training the citizens of a free country so that they may know how to protect their liberties. 

This is the chief justification for schools which are supported by public money.

Do you suppose good citizenship is the only motive for the support of schools by the state? If the school teaches a boy to be a chemist and he becomes wealthy by reason of his discoveries, is the community better off? 11 

2. Private schools. In all probability we shall never find all our children in the public schools. Some children never seem to fit in with large groups. Some pupils wish to follow a special course of study which would be difficult for public schools to provide in a short time. Sometimes it may be desirable for a pupil to leave home and be under the care of persons other than his parents or other relatives. The state, however, claims the right to insist upon a proper quality of instruction in private schools.

Some people believe that private schools encourage pupils to think they are a little better than the ordinary run of humanity. 

The principals of such schools object to such criticisms, however. 

They assert that if it were not for the work of private schools, the standards of the public schools would be much lower, and that

Catholic Central High School at Pittsburgh. 

Some cities have a complete system of schools under Catholic auspices, from elementary grades through the university. the community should be grateful to them for their services to education. 

Make a list of all the forms of private schools you can think of. What special reasons might urge a parent or pupil to make use of any of them? From your own experience do you find that any private school pupils are snobs? When, if ever, would it be wise for a pupil to attend a "business college," rather than take a regular school course? 

Is there any danger connected with such special "short-cut" schools? 

3. Parochial schools. 

These are private schools of a particular type in which special religious instruction forms a part of the regular course. The Roman Catholic Church has done the most toward the maintenance of this type of school, though some other denominations, such as the Lutheran, have in some places their own parochial schools.

Do you think it is desirable for state governments to insist that private schools be carried on in the English language except when a foreign language is the particular subject of study?

67. How Our Public Schools Are Organized. In this country the control of public schools is entirely in the hands of the state. 

Our national Constitution does not say a word about education.¹ 

In most other great countries the opposite situation prevails. 

Usually there is some national officer to supervise education for the whole country.

The national government has, however, helped the states to maintain schools. There is in our national Department of the 

Interior a division known as the Office of Education, which undertakes the gathering and publishing of information in regard to the ways our schools are or ought to be carried on. By national laws certain tracts of public land in every state except the earliest ones have been set aside to help support schools. In 1862 Congress voted to assist the states in maintaining agricultural colleges by giving them land and by other means. Under the policy which began with the so-called Smith-Hughes Act in 1917, even our local schools that maintain vocational courses may receive help from the federal government. There is a Federal Board for Vocational 

Education which helps to supervise the work of such schools. 

The state, however, is wholly responsible for setting up and maintaining public schools within its borders. Most of the actual work of administering schools is done through officers in counties, cities, townships, or special school districts. But the state tells these subdivisions what they may or must do in the matter of education. 

Once the states permitted cities or towns to set up school systems; now they command them to do so. Frequently the state helps the smaller communities out of its own treasury to support better schools than they could otherwise afford. 

How much does your state contribute annually to support public schools? Does it insist that the money be spent for specific purposes, such as teachers' salaries, for instance? 

In a number of states the county is the unit under which the schools are managed. This is particularly true in the South. In the North, each town, city, or some combination of them, is more likely to make up a school district. 

1 The national government maintains its famous schools at West Point and Annapolis, and also sees that schools are provided in the District of Columbia and the possessions. By what right does it do that?

A few states, of which New York is a notable example, have a very thorough system of state supervision of schools. There, the state publishes a syllabus of studies which every school district may use, and gives uniform examinations in certain subjects to every school in the state which is under its direction. Some states 

AN ATLANTA JUNIOR HIGH SCHOOL 

This southern city seems to have constructed a building modern in all respects. require the use of the same textbooks all over the state, but more of them allow each county or school district to select its own books. More and more states are adopting the policy of furnishing textbooks free, but this practice is by no means followed everywhere.

Which policy do you think would result in better schools, a nationally-controlled school system or such a one as we have in this country? On what basis are your local school districts organized? 

Should any distinction be made between grade school and high school pupils in the matter of free textbooks and supplies? What arguments may be offered for or against free textbooks? 

It has been proposed that there should be a Department of Education organized as a branch of the national government with a Secretary of Education at its head who would be a member of the 

President's Cabinet. Do you see any advantage in doing so?

Some people have been very much opposed to the idea. What may be their reasons?

In many states there are eight grades preceding the four years of high school. In some places, however, the twelve grades are divided into three parts. The first six are considered elementary grades, the next three the junior high school or intermediate school, and the last three the senior high school. In some southern states there are only eleven grades, seven of which precede the high school. A few communities add a two-year junior college course, from which a person can go directly into the junior year of a college or university.

What is the system of grades that prevails in your school system? 

Is there any reason why the national government should be interested in your education? Some state school systems and many cities have kindergartens. What benefit do you think is derived from them? 

68. The People Who Manage the Schools. Every state and every subdivision of a state which handles school affairs has officials to supervise the work. The chief school administrator in a state is sometimes called Commissioner of Education, sometimes Superintendent of Education, and sometimes Superintendent of Public Instruction. In about two-thirds of the states this officer is elected by popular vote; but in other states he is appointed, usually by the governor. Most states, too, have a State Council of Education or State Board of Education. In New York the persons who have this general responsibility are known as the Regents of the 

University of the State of New York.

In every state where the county has a voice in school management there is, under some name, a county Superintendent of Schools. 

Very often this county superintendent is elected by popular vote like other county officers. In Pennsylvania he is chosen by the directors of the schools over which he has charge. School districts smaller than a county may have a Superintendent of Schools or a Supervising Principal. Sometimes two or three districts may combine under one superintendent. 

Who is your superintendent, and how is he chosen?

In every school district there is a School Board or Board of Education, or Board of School Trustees. Most often they are elected by popular vote, but in some cases they are appointed by the mayor of a city or some other public official. These local school

boards have direct control over the schools of their districts. They may elect teachers and other officers, adopt policies or programs for the schools, and decide the size of the local school tax, unless the laws of the state limit their power. In most good school systems the superintendent is given extensive authority in most matters related to school management.

When a school is large enough to require several teachers, it also needs a principal. Principals and teachers are required in every state to measure up to certain standards of experience and education, but these are higher in some states than in others. Upon the teacher really rests the success of the school; for if the work in the classroom is poorly done, no amount of brains in a superintendent's office can educate the children. 

69. The People Who Support the Schools. - Private schools must be maintained by tuition fees or by endowments money that has been invested so that the income may be used to carry on the school. Religious schools may be supported by the contributions which people make directly to their churches. But most of the money to support public schools comes from taxes. Once in a great while a generous and wealthy man gives or wills to a community funds to put up a school building or help pay the salaries of its teachers. Sometimes small sources of income may help to increase the funds of a school district, such as certain kinds of licenses or selling public property.

In many states the bulk of the school money is obtained by taxes levied by the local district. In others the state government contributes generously out of its own treasury, especially if the state has within its borders some rich and populous communities. and others that are thinly settled and poor. 

In what proportion are school expenses divided in your state between the state and the local school districts? Work out Project 2 on page 169.

It is sometimes difficult to get proper support for the schools. 

James Close may complain about paying school taxes because he has no children in school. He might as well object to paving any streets except the ones he uses. The community is better in every way and his own property is safer because of what the school does for its pupils. Mr. and Mrs. Faultfinder may not realize

the variety of ways in which our public schools benefit their children. Some call our music and drawing, physical education, shop-work, domestic science, and vocational guidance, fads. 

If that is the way we feel about it, we may not like to pay the bills. 

But a great many of these activities give an immensely broader interest to our boys and girls for their life outside of school. Besides, the schools are open for more hours in the twenty-four and 

A SCHOOL WITH ITS PLAYGROUND 

This Tennessee consolidated school has been able to provide also considerable space for recreation. more days in the year than they used to be. As late as 1900 the average American had received only 1046 days or between five and six school years of average length. Surely that is not enough in these days! That is one reason we have night schools for those who cannot attend day school or would feel out of place there.

To remove misunderstandings and to keep parents and friends acquainted with what schools are doing, there have been formed in many communities Parent-Teacher Associations. Altogether too many parents do not visit schools unless the principal sends for them. Can it be that you never made clear at home just what the school is trying to do or that you failed to do what the school expected of you?

Can we who are now in school do anything to make the public think well of it? How much do you do to advertise it? What sort of advertising do you unconsciously give it by your speech and acts? 

How should we all suffer if public schools were abolished? 

How does your state compare with others in its support of public schools? 

70. The Pupils Who Make the Schools. In a very real sense the pupils make the school. If there were no pupils, there would be no schools. Practically every state insists that every pupil shall attend school from the age of seven to fifteen, or eight to sixteen, or some similar period. At least three states require school attendance until a person is eighteen unless he finishes a high school course before that time. The length of the school year varies from six to ten months. In New York it is hoped that soon every school district will have a ten-month term. There is a general tendency everywhere to lengthen the school term, though, on the other hand, in hard times schools are sometimes closed or their terms shortened because there is no money from taxes to keep them open.

A few communities have a twelve-month school. Pupils are required to attend three quarters out of four, and may in special cases be allowed to go the four quarters. Is this a good plan? Do you see any dangers in it? What is the use of school vacations?

Is there much likelihood that pupils will get through too young? 

What is the best age to finish high school? What are the laws of your state in regard to school terms and attendance? 

Parents sometimes foolishly think that their child should begin earning money at the earliest possible moment, even though by taking him out of school so early they reduce greatly his future chances of making a comfortable living. Sometimes a boy or girl gets tired of school and thinks he would enjoy holding a job. Still others just like to loaf. That is why we have to have truant officers. On the average children stay in school much longer now than formerly. In many communities it is just as natural for children to keep on from the elementary grades into the junior or senior high school as it is for them to pass from one grade to another at any point in the system. 

Is there any good reason for a pupil's dropping out of school before he has finished high school? If any of your acquaintances left

school before completing the seventh or eighth grade, why did they do it?

The high school has made wonderful progress. There are people still alive in some of our older cities who remember when the first high school in their city was opened. Now over 4,000,000 boys and girls in the United States are high school pupils. Large cities either have several "cosmopolitan" high schools which teach almost everything, or have separate classical, technical, and commercial high schools. So valuable and far-reaching is the work of the high school that it has been called "the people's college." 

Not all who go to school are children or even young people. In some countries, such as England and Denmark, perhaps even more attention is being given to adult education than here 

Courtesy Children's Bureau

A ONE-ROOM RURAL SCHOOL 

Several grades have to be accommodated in this one room, with very little in the way of equipment and supplies. Intelligent school administrators are doing away with schools of this kind as fast as possible, but many of them still exist. and we are paying a good deal of attention to it. Evening schools in our large cities are attended by people of all ages. And this kind of interest is not always limited to the cities. The so-called "moonlight schools" in the mountains of Kentucky have gained a nation-wide fame for the interest shown and the progress made by those who attend them. 

What is your opinion of the boy whose parents want to keep him in school, but who says, "I am going to get a job, and then go to night school"? 

71. School Buildings Old and New. Some people even yet have a tender spot in their hearts for the "little red schoolhouse." 

The old-time school building with a stove in the middle of its one room, and its rude desks or benches, was usually taught by one teacher who was expected to know enough about fifteen or twenty subjects to instruct pupils all the way from five to twenty years old. And it did much good! But how much better it is that

every teacher and every child shall have the benefit of comfortable desks, properly lighted schoolrooms, a reasonable amount of textbooks, reference books, supplies, maps, pictures, and the like! 

City schools, some may say, can afford all these conveniences. 

Should we be sure of that? In the city schools classes are likely to be larger and buildings more crowded. If there are reference books and maps, so many must use them that the good they can do is greatly limited. 

Courtesy Pennsylvania Department of Health

THE NEW IDEA IN RURAL SCHOOLS 

This consolidated school building is out in the country; but it is well constructed, modern in its equipment, and meets well the needs of its pupils. 

In the country school there can be a close relation between pupil and teacher. And it is possible to have first-class schools in the country. A township, instead of having half a dozen one-room schools scattered around, may put up a good consolidated school in a well-equipped central building. It can pay large enough salaries to attract capable teachers. By busses, children can be brought to school and taken home in the afternoon at a relatively low cost.

An enthusiastic neighborhood may keep its school building open evenings, holidays, and even Sundays for community meetings of all kinds. One school director who did not want to spend a cent for a school gymnasium became enthusiastic over the idea when he learned that a corn show could be held there. Some country schools have athletic fields that would be the envy of dozens of

city high schools. Particularly in some of our western cities and towns, the school building is by far the finest structure for miles around.

What features should be provided for in every school building? 

Can you judge a community's spirit by the style and condition of its public schools? In how many ways is your own school building used? Are any improvements needed in it? 

Courtesy Denver Tourist Bureau THE WEST DENVER HIGH SCHOOL 

People sometimes complain about the amount of money spent for schools. Is it not justifiable, however, to have the surroundings, as well as the buildings themselves, so planned and laid out as to appeal to one's sense of beauty? Certainly an appreciation of the beautiful is an element not to be neglected in one's education. 

72. What Shall We Teach? If the seven objectives of education are fundamental, the course of study in our schools should provide for all these interests. It should offer opportunities for the person who enjoys working with his hands as well as for the bookstudent and the thinker. It should not neglect the training of the body, for a pupil who develops his brains and neglects his body is likely to come to grief.

Those who want to go to college or do advance study by themselves should also receive all the training that they need. There was a time when too much emphasis was put on college preparatory

subjects by our high schools. Sometimes we think now that some communities may be swinging too far in the other direction. 

Guidance is another feature emphasized in the high school today. 

This first took the form of vocational guidance, in which pupils were helped to find the occupation or profession for which they thought they wanted to prepare. Practically all large high schools have

In any school we are bound to get out of it something more than "book learning." There we form friendships that are sometimes lifelong. We get convincing lessons in coöperation and teamwork - especially in athletics. We show our school spirit not only by cheering for the teams, but by participating in various activities- debating, dramatics, musical societies, and others. 

It is not strange that some people look back to their school days as the happiest period of their lives.

What is the purpose of such activities as are often called "extracurricular"? What are the values of high school athletics? May they be overdone? A NEW FEATURE OF COLLEGE LIFE 

The space under the seats of the stadium is used in part for dormitories for the college boys at the Louisiana State University. 

73. The Rush for Higher Learning. At the top of our educational structure we find the colleges and universities. To enter any of these, graduation from a four-year high school or its equivalent is required of applicants. Almost all of our colleges and universities are private institutions. Some of them have endowment funds from which they obtain incomes to carry on their work. Sometimes generous rich men contribute very liberally to found or support a college or university. 

In every state there is at least one state college or other institution which gets aid from the national government for the purpose of teaching agriculture or some other practical art. From the

Middle West to the Pacific Coast practically every state has a state university that is as much a part of the state's public school system as any primary school, and is intended to be the crown. 

Courtesy University of Pittsburgh

THE CATHEDRAL OF LEARNING 

This building, somewhat over forty stories in height, will be one of the notable educational structures of the nation. It will house a great many of the activities of the University of Pittsburgh. of the whole system. Every state has had one or more teachers' colleges or normal schools to train teachers for the schools of the state. 

For some years after the 

World War the increase in the number of people going to college was remarkable. Once hardly more than 10 per cent of the high school graduates went to college, but now some of the best high schools send over 50 per cent of their graduates to more advanced institutions. Since our best colleges frequently have more applicants for admission than they can accommodate, they can select the students who make the best records in their preparatory schools, and perhaps may be able to keep out the "coonskin coats" who have loafers, professional athletes, and disgraced some of our colleges. Some universities maintain a graduate school, where one may study for one year, two years, or more, for advanced degrees.

What are the entrance requirements and tuition charges of any college or university in which you may be interested? What are the most common college degrees, and what does each mean? 

Who should and who should not go to college? Are you planning to go? Why?

What is the record of your school in regard to the attendance of its graduates at college? What subjects are most often included in college entrance requirements? Should it make any difference to a college what particular subjects a pupil has taken in his "prep"

school? Should girls plan for college the same as boys? Name ten colleges or universities of special note. Give some striking fact about each one. Other things being equal, would a college graduate make a better President than one who is not?

Should an athlete going to college receive assistance that he would not get if he were merely a good student? Should a large city maintain its own university? 

74. Other Ways of Becoming Educated. You may know a very intelligent, cultured person who never was able to attend school very long, but has used every chance to improve himself. 

Aerial Photo by Parsifal Cobb

DUKE UNIVERSITY, DURHAM, NORTH CAROLINA 

Here is a university which has had a wonderful development in recent years. It is the wealthiest university in the South and one of the richest in the country. 

If one seeks the learning to be found in books, libraries and book shops will afford him thousands of opportunities. Newspapers and magazines, government bulletins, lecture courses, concerts, museums, art galleries, and conversation with intelligent and experienced people broaden one's knowledge and outlook. 

Some of the best industrial establishments maintain schools to help their employees become better fitted for their work. Universities offer extension courses in the " downtown " section of cities and in "centers" miles away from the university campus. Correspondence schools have helped many people, though sometimes too glowing claims are made for the accomplishments that are possible by "getting knowledge through the mail."

Those who are fortunate enough to be able to travel, even a short distance, find this a wonderful means of enlightenment. It is much more effective to see people and things with your own eyes than simply to read about them. No word picture can paint the colors of the Grand Canyon or Lake Louise, for example.

Best of all, for most of us, is that sort of intelligence that comes from actual experience. If we take part in some community

Even if we make mistakes, we learn how not to do a thing. It is a high compliment to a man to say that he never makes the same mistake twice.

What opportunities for general education or culture are offered in your community? To what extent do people take advantage of them? How much good educationally does the radio accomplish?

This is Lake Louise in the Canadian Rockies. You may get some impression of the wonderful beauty of the scene, but no picture can reproduce the coloring of the water. world. No one can tell how far-reaching is the influence created by the stories and pictures on the printed page. Pictures play a great part in the publications of today, for pictures make a stronger impression on us than mere words. A publication which is mostly pictures, however, makes an appeal to only one side of a person's intellect.

To prevent misleading accounts from appearing in the newspapers, most publishers, for their own protection and advantage,

are connected with some great news agency, such as the Associated Press, the United Press, or the International News Service. These agencies have representatives in every part of the world who send news to a central headquarters. Then this news goes out to the papers which pay the news associations for the right to receive it.

We should support those publications that tell the truth in a fair and unbiased manner in preference to the sensational newspapers and magazines whose only aim is to sell.

76. How Libraries Serve the People. - It would be a real tax on intelligent people's pocketbooks if they had to buy all the books they wanted to read. The public library has come to our aid;

in every up-to-date community there is at least one library. A wealthy citizen's gift to a town has often taken the form of a library. Andrew Carnegie, however, always insisted, when he gave a library building to a town, that the town, to show its interest, should pledge a certain amount of money to keep the library going.

Some fine libraries are connected with colleges or universities, such as the Widener Library, at Harvard, which is one of the three largest in the 

United States. In some cities, too, there are private libraries which contain many thousands of books and which render a valuable service. The greatest library in the country is the 

Library of Congress at Wash ington, but that has to be managed somewhat differently from any other. 

C.O. Buckingham

IN THE CONGRESSIONAL LIBRARY 

This stairway gives you just one of many beautiful scenes in the Library of Congress, at Washington. The total cost of the building was about $7,000,000. 

Surely you know something of the varied work of a library today its reference room, reading room, lending department, and exhibition rooms. In our large cities the libraries and museums coöperate directly in many ways with the schools, through visits, lectures, "children's hours," and the lending of materials. 99 Several states are trying to provide their rural communities with library facilities. From the state capital, the state university, or some other central place, they send out "traveling libraries which may be kept for a few weeks or months in one neighborhood and then are passed on to some other locality. Sometimes in a particular county, a central library is maintained, and wagons and trucks are sent out every week to all parts of the county. Private

individuals have earned a living by setting up libraries and lending books from them. 

What qualities should a good librarian possess? How well are you acquainted with your local library? Make a list of ten prominent writers of today in different literary fields. What are the chief provisions of our laws about copyrights? 

77. Our Obligations toward Education. Today almost every one in the United States starts out with a fair chance to acquire

your own 

gest job you have on hand right now. Don't let any good thing get away from you, and don't hinder any- body else from making his school days count for all they can. Don't abuse the privileges that are yours.

Probably Whittier's lines are never more often quoted than by those who regret the way they misused their opportunities in school: "Of all sad words of tongue or pen

The saddest are these: 'It might have been.' 999 It has been figured that a boy or girl who goes through high school has cost his community, on the average, at least $1100. In some communities the amount would be much higher. What have you done to deserve having so much money spent for you? 

Does it pay your community to give you and others like you such a chance for self-improvement at almost no cost to yourself? 

School loafers waste a community's money inexcusably. They take more of the teacher's time perhaps than the good students, because the teacher in trying to make them learn something may even neglect those who are anxious to profit from school life. 

Retarded pupils, as we call those who are more than a year behind

average age for their grade, are sometimes real nuisances. A boy or girl is not always to blame for having to take a subject over again, but if one's failure is the result of his own laziness, he has shown himself to be a poor citizen. It is about as bad to make your community spend unnecessary money for your schooling as it is to deface public property that has to be replaced and paid for. 

Our community and nation in the future will be what the boys and girls in school today make them. 

What ways of wasting time in school or at home can you avoid? 

Can you concentrate on your work when others are around? Do you plan your study time wisely? 

How can you make your school work effective in the following respects: 

1. Coöperation - how, when, with whom? 

2. Obedience why, when, to whom? 

3. Honesty and faithfulness in school relationships? 

4. Promoting high ideals for your school? 

Does your school citizens' association or your class organization do all it can to promote good school citizenship? If a person is a good citizen in school, what prediction would you be likely to make about his later citizenship? 

Education is one of the chief pursuits of a wise man, measured by a people's literacy and interest in community welfare, most extensively provided by schools - public, private, and religious. 

Public schools are controlled by the states, perhaps aided by the national government, but directly administered by local school boards and supervisory officers, organized in grades, supported mainly by taxes, kept open much longer, teaching a greater variety of subjects than formerly, available in many places for people of all ages, provided, as far as possible, with efficient buildings and useful equipment, offering many kinds of activities in addition to the study of books. 

Other means of education are found in colleges and universities, some public and a greater number private, which have in many cases a large enrollment,

libraries, museums, schools connected with industry, concerts, travel, and experience, magazines and newspapers, which are more widely read in this country than anywhere else in the world, one's own attitude toward educating himself.

INTERESTING PROJECTS YOU MAY LIKE TO UNDERTAKE 

1. Work out a plan showing in full detail the organization and management of your own school system. 

2. Make a complete list of the conditions and equipment that would characterize what you would consider an ideal schoolroom. Note such matters as the amount of floor space, the kind and number of desks, chairs, and tables, the lighting facilities, space for blackboards and bulletins, maps, bookcases, and anything that a schoolroom should have to make it complete. If your schoolroom does not have all these desirable features, can you suggest a plan by which at least some of them can be provided? 

3. For all. If you have not already done so as a part of the guidance program of your school, draw up a program for your high school course through the senior high school. Think carefully of the special use, if any, you expect to make of your high school training, and what subjects will be of particular value for college entrance or for an occupation in which you feel especially interested. 

4. A plan for continuing my education after I leave school.

5. Draw up a plan or diagram showing the organization of the school system of your state, indicating the state officials who have something to do with education, the local officials, the classification of school districts, and any other appropriate facts. 

6. Draw up a plan for the use of your own time for an entire week. Indicate the hours and length of time when you plan to be engaged in home study, school attendance, work at home or outside, recreation, sleep, church, and any other activities which you can foresee. 

7. A program including talks by pupils who have visited a famous library, museum, or university. 

8. A visit to the plant of a great newspaper. 

9. Arrange a debate on one or more of the following subjects:

Resolved, that every pupil physically able should be required to take a four-year high school course or its equivalent. 

Resolved, that an eight-hour school day, with no required home work, should be generally established.

Resolved, that schools should be open for regular sessions twelve months in the year.

Resolved, that states or local communities should furnish college instruction as freely as that of lower grades, to all who are qualified to receive it.

Resolved, that newspapers influence public opinion more than any other one force.

Resolved, that a person who limits his reading to newspapers is not likely to be a person of culture. 

Resolved, that the growth of newspaper chains is dangerous to our intellectual and social well-being.

CHAPTER IX HOW THE COMMUNITY HELPS TO BUILD CHARACTER 

If you can fill the unforgiving minute 

With sixty seconds' worth of distance run,

Yours is the Earth and everything that's in it, And which is more 

you'll be a Man, my son!

KIPLING 

There is something still more important in community life and the building of citizenship than any school building or courthouse or railroad. If a community is to be a desirable place to live in, its citizens must desire the things that are highest and best. And yet the government can directly do very little to build character. It can by proper laws encourage us to do right and can protect those agencies that work for a better community. But that is about all. By what means, then, are standards of thinking and conduct and ideals of upright living established for the citizens of a community? 

What motives lead people to act as they do?

How is public sentiment or public opinion developed in a community? 

How important are leaders in community life?

By what means may we encourage high moral standards and lofty ideals? 

How does religion influence community life?

What relation exists between religious organizations and the community as a whole? 

What is our own responsibility for character building? 

Some of our actions are 

78. Influences upon Our Conduct. easily understood we eat to satisfy our hunger and sleep in order to rest. Instinct tells us to do these things. But why do women usually wear skirts, while men usually do not? Why do we say such meaningless things as "How do you do?" Why do we take each other by the right hand rather than the left? 

Some explanations of our conduct can be grouped under the general head of environment — the surroundings in which we live 172

or work. One of the first things even a child does is to imitate people. So if "everybody's doing it," we think we must, and sometimes make ourselves believe that we want to. An actress got into her car without fastening up her galoshes, and for several months thereafter many girls let theirs flap around their ankles. 

In many matters people are guided by the law or custom of the community in which they live. There is really no special reason why we should keep to the right when we meet some one on the road. In England and some other countries they keep to the left. We simply got into the habit of keeping to the right and now we have made a law of it. 

Fear also influences our conduct physical fear of somebody stronger or fear of disapproval or ridicule. It may even make a person do right when he would rather not. In another situation a boy may learn to smoke or indulge in some other harmful practice because he is afraid to be different from a certain gang. We may give a tip because we do not want people to think that we are cheap rather than because there has been any special service. 

The expectation of benefit leads us to do many things. We want comfort in our homes; this desire may lead us to have over-stuffed furniture in the sitting room. We want to know more about a certain subject or have a little relaxation; so we go to a lecture or a show. We even say, "Honesty is the best policy."

Another common explanation for conduct is habit. Psychologists tell us that the word going out from the brain to perform a certain act from time to time wears a little channel in the nerves, as it were; and then when the same situation comes around again, we do it without any effort. The fiftieth time you write your name looks almost exactly like the first unless you deliberately try to make it look different. Try to break a habit that has a hold on you and see whether it is easy. A man who has the habit of swearing will let out an oath in places where it is not desirable to do so. 

Do people always realize why they act as they do at a given time? 

Do you find any examples in your own life and conduct of things. which you do without difficulty, but without definitely determining to do them? Why do most men think they must wear a collar when they are dressed up when women do not do so? What dangers are there in the policy, “When in Rome, do as the Romans do”?

79. Folkways and Fashions. When a habit or custom comes to be the regular practice of a large group of people, we may refer 

COLLEGE STUDENTS OF A QUARTER OF A CENTURY AGO 

Compare these costumes with those of today. Are college students today satisfied with the same kind of recreation that once appealed to them? Are present customs better or worse? done." There is no moral wrong is not considered good manners. to it as a folkway. A certain custom may be characteristic of a certain group. Folkways may even become features of a people's religion. Forms of dress are another example.

The things we put on our heads may look as funny to a cultured native of India as the yards of stuff he winds around his head do to us. 

Is there anything besides tradition that makes some churches carry on worship with rather elaborate ceremonies, while others reduce form and ceremony to a very low point? Do you, or do you not, like considerable form in church services? Why?

The natives of Hawaii gave us the word taboo, meaning some particular practice that their customs forbade. Society still has its taboos. Of a certain thing we say, "It just isn't in eating pie with a knife, but it Mention four things that custom and common practice tell us not to do in good society today. Is there sound reason back of these taboos? Can you explain the change in men's attire between the days of George Washington and present times?
