80. How Crowds and Mobs Act. 

ently in crowds than by themselves? vidual may be a peaceful law-abiding citizen, yet in a group he may act like a "roughneck." You go to a baseball game. If a player on your team gets a three-bagger with the bases full, you are on your feet yelling; yet you couldn't for the life of you remember getting out of your seat. When a person becomes one of a crowd or mob, he ceases for the time being to be a person. He is simply one unit with other units in a mass. 

There is a difference between a crowd and a mob, though they both are the outgrowth of a tendency to group together. Successful preachers and teachers, as well as political leaders, use "crowd psychology" to good advantage. "Revival" meetings have stirred many a person to resolve to do right, when ordinary appeals never touched him. Too often, however, it is the troublemaker who leads the crowd. A mob is a disorderly group possessed by one idea. Usually it is bent on destruction. It may attack a person in order to string him to a tree or "tar and feather" him.

Sometimes mobs are actually planned. "Night riders" who go out in the darkness and inflict horse-whippings do not go on the spur of the moment. Such a group may not molest decent people, but its members usually try to justify acts committed by the crowd even when they would not think of doing such things alone.

Have you ever found yourself in a crowd doing things which you had not intended to do? If you felt that a mischief-making mob was gathering, what could be done to break it up or prevent wrongdoing? Are mobs an example of cowardice?

When one gets into a crowd for even a little while, he needs to keep his wits about him. If he does not, he may be drawn into something he will regret. A strong, clear-headed person may at 

THE HOLLYWOOD BOWL 

Plays and concerts are given here in the open air by day or evening. Few places can show such a setting for a public performance. such a time, however, lead the crowd away from harm, even if he does not get them to act like human beings. A humorous remark will sometimes do the business. 

What examples have you noticed of the gathering of crowds? 

Did they act intelligently? How do people usually act at fires? Is it better to be independent and to pay no attention to the customs or standards of your associates or neighbors, or to be controlled by them? Is there any middle ground between these two positions? 

81. Public Opinion and How It Is Formed. What is "public sentiment" or "public opinion"? It cannot be everybody's opinion, for the subject upon which every one thinks the same has yet to be discovered. Perhaps we should say public opinion is the attitude of the majority of the people who think at all about a particular subject. Sometimes a small group will make a great deal of noise and deceive themselves or others into thinking that the disturbance they make represents their real importance.

There are various ways of forming public opinion. It often does not come from serious reasoning and sound judgment. Quite likely, the newspapers day by day do more in influencing public opinion, perhaps unconsciously, than any other factor. Clubs, lodges, churches, and other organizations often create a feeling among their own particular members that reaches out beyond the limits of that group. Even one man may arouse a whole community by interesting his friends in a proposition and through them their friends and their friends. 

One word which we often see in connection with this subject is propaganda information circulated with the idea of influencing people indirectly to believe certain things. In a political campaign we may be told that a certain candidate is kind to his family or fought for his country in war. Very good as far as it goes, but aren't his ideas about public questions still more important? Propaganda may be used for a good cause as well as a bad one; but, unfortunately, the word too often suggests getting support for a cause which would not command our respect on its own account.

THE HOME OF A FAMOUS SECRET ORDER 

The Masonic Temple, in Detroit, is an exceedingly attractive building. Other secret orders also frequently put up fine structures which are often used for community purposes. 

If there is difference of opinion in a community with reference to a particular topic, can there be such a thing as public sentiment in regard to it? "Vox populi, vox Dei," - "the voice of the people is the voice of God." Do you believe it? Has a newspaper the moral right to put up its news articles in such a way as to influence public opinion through the way they are written? How many people read the editorial page of their newspaper? 

Why do political parties get up parades and use red fire and hire bands? In what other ways do they try to influence public opinion?

Why are "decent citizens" so often quiet when demagogues and trouble-makers create so much noise?

82. Leaders and Their Importance. Some historians have said that history is a record of the accomplishments of great men. 

George Washington alone could not have gained American independence, but perhaps American independence would not have been gained without George Washington. 

Courtesy National Recreation Association THE WESTCHESTER COUNTY MUSICAL FESTIVAL CHORUS AND ORCHESTRA 

This county, just outside New York City, has been notable for its development of recreation facilities for the people of the county. Here is a very unusual form of community spirit. 

Not every one is qualified to be a leader. Such a person must have much initiative and the ability to persuade or command people. He must expect some people to find fault with him or with the way he proposes to do things. Wilson said, "I don't care what they say about me now; I am thinking about what they will say about me six hundred years from now." But isn't it too bad if a leader has to wait six hundred years to be appreciated?

Is it the business of a leader to impress his own ideas upon his followers, or to find out what they think and lead them in the accomplishment of their wishes? It is sometimes said, "It is better to be safe than sorry." A teacher once announced, "I will mark high for a grand mistake." Give your opinion of the thought in these two quotations.

A successful leader must understand thoroughly both his people and his policies. Almost always a cause has to be preached by individuals before the masses become enthusiastic about it. Some people found fault with Theodore Roosevelt because they said he acted as if he had discovered the Ten Commandments. Quite likely, however, many more people became interested in applying the Ten Commandments to present-day life because they admired something in "T. R."

We must have leaders or experts to direct the doing of many things. We may decide, for example, that we want to spend money for an attractive boulevard; but when it comes to working out the grades, laying the foundations, and directing the workers, we must call upon those who know how to do those things. Probably the best way to qualify ourselves to become leaders or experts is to apply ourselves faithfully day after day to the things that are at hand, at the same time observing and learning all we can. 

Then, some time, we may surprise even ourselves by putting a big job through successfully. 

Show why leaders are necessary in an army, a government, an athletic team, a factory, a school, a home, a church. Is popular vote always the best way to choose a leader? Can a person be taught to be a leader just as he may be taught algebra? What qualities are necessary in a leader? What is the difference between a leader and a demagogue? Is there any way of telling them apart? 

Illustrate by examples. 

83. Moral Standards. 

A standard is a measure or level by which we judge things. We cannot measure conduct as we measure coal or apples, but we can set up principles by which we estimate whether an act is good or bad. Morality is, we may say, the established idea of what is good.

People's judgment about things may sometimes change, and what was "right" may become "wrong." Many primitive men thought it was right to have more than one wife. Now, society says it will punish a man who has two wives at the same time. "Time makes ancient good uncouth." We no longer keep slaves, or hold lotteries to build colleges. It sometimes takes a long while, however, to uproot an evil custom, even when we know that some other way is better. On the other hand, some practices that were

once taboo are now generally accepted the idea of women working outside the home, their right to vote, and the like. 66 99 Let us not be too ready to assume, however, that old standards of conduct are mid-Victorian or that any restraint upon selfish pleasure is a " blue law" and therefore to be ignored. Let's be slow about departing from paths that experienced people consider safe and sane. Some people try to fool themselves and others by professing to be liberal and progressive when all they really want is some harmful pleasure. 

May a certain act be wrong

Is self-control a desirable virtue? for you and right for some one else? to do things because they have been is a person justified in breaking away from an established line of conduct? 

84. The Value of High Ideals. Of course, we don't mean their value in dollars and cents, though that would be pretty high. 

An ideal is a condition or state which we consider desirable for ourselves or for others. It usually means something higher or better than we already have. When a man is satisfied with himself and says, "I am through," his life for all practical purposes may be considered a tragedy. The only way individuals or communities or the whole world can hope to get ahead is by wanting to be better than they are. Even 

You know the old advice, " Hitch your wagon to a star." though we may never attain our goal, we may strive all the harder if we realize how rich and great are the things we would like to know and be and do. "A man's reach should exceed his grasp,' said Browning. Those who come after us ought to be able to accomplish more than we do; but if we lie down on the job, our successors will have to stop and do the things that we should do now, instead of going ahead with bigger and better things. It means much to a real man or woman that people will say of him, He did his best." 

Make lists of three or four worthy and unworthy ideals for each of the following: a lawyer; a business man; a teacher; a skilled tradesman. Should a person ever be satisfied with less than his best? Which is better, to set up ideals so high that we know we can never reach them, or to set up moderate goals for attainment

and, when we reach them, to set up others further ahead? People sometimes speak in rather uncomplimentary terms of some one as "an idealist." Is it just to do so? Is there any danger that we shall think too much about ideals? 

85. What Is Religion? - Religion and the church play a great part in forming people's ideals. Religion is not just the same as morality. A moral standard is a measure of conduct. Religion is our idea of the proper relation between ourselves and a higher power. For example, some may do right because they feel that 

God wishes them to do so rather than for any other reason. ligion is a matter of faith, of belief. We may look, as Napoleon is said to have done, at the stars in the heavens and ask, "Who made all this? 99 Re- 

Courtesy All-Year Club of Southern California

A FRANCISCAN MISSION 

Hardly any people or tribe ever lived that did not have some form of religion. Even primitive people realized that some invisible agencies existed which were far stronger than they, and worshiped them after their crude fashion. They either feared these forces or tried to get their good will.

They offered sacrifices, sometimes even of their own children, to make the gods feel kindly toward them. 

This building bears the date 1782. It is one of the chain of missions founded by the Franciscan Friars, who had such an important part in the early history of California.

Another phase of religious belief which we find among a great many people is the thought of a spirit world or a future life. If there is another world in which a man will be punished or rewarded for the way he lives here, there is a strong force to make him live better than he otherwise would. Perhaps this is a comparatively low motive, but it works with a great many people. 

Look up the significance of the following terms and compare their meaning with reference to their possible influence on a person's

life: Hades, Elysian Fields, Valhalla, Nirvana, Heaven; "In my father's house there are many mansions." 

It was among the ancient Hebrews that the idea of one God monotheism - instead of many gods - polytheism - really took form. Both Jews and Christians think of God as a father, and their religious teachings include the finest moral codes that the world has known. There is nothing better in this respect than the Ten Commandments, that famous verse of the prophet Micah, "What doth God require of thee except to do justice and

The Boston Avenue Methodist Church, in Tulsa, Oklahoma, is an example of a new but very attractive style of American church architecture. love mercy and walk humbly with thy God?" and the Sermon on the Mount. If a person is truly religious, he will have good morals as well. 

Can a person live a high moral life without being religious? How much is environment likely to have to do with either morality or religion? Why do you suppose in primitive religions morality had little part? Can you imagine why people would worship their ancestors, or idols of wood and stone? 

86. What Do Churches Do? -Through the churches religion does its most effective work among men. Some people may have deep and sincere religious feeling without being connected with any church, although not many do. Sensible persons do not care to live in churchless communities. 

Not only do churches try to give help, comfort, and companionship to their own members; but most of our so-called social service work began with the church. Churches have founded many schools and colleges. The doors of many a church are open not only on Sunday, but every day in the week, so that people can go

there for meditation or prayer, or simply to get away from the noise of the outside. Some churches support summer camps, Boy Scout and Girl Scout Troops, and other entertainment under favorable conditions, rather than turn young people out into the streets, to the public dance halls, pool rooms, or other harmful places. Missionaries who go to foreign lands may open schools or heal the sick before they do any preaching.

Do the people in a community who do not belong to any church get any benefits from the presence of churches there? What do you think of the person who says that he won't go to church because there are too many hypocrites who do?

On your community map locate and name the churches of the various religious bodies. The following men were or are great religious leaders. Find out something about each one and point out the qualities that have given them their influence: Cardinal 

Gibbons, Phillips Brooks, Dwight L. Moody, Harry Emerson Fosdick, Stephen S. Wise. Add other names that your parents or friends may mention. 

87. Religion and the Government. — Whatever the church may teach about the fate of one who stays outside its walls, it does not compel any one to join, in this country, at least. In that respect it differs from some other social groups. 

Such freedom of choice has not always existed. Even Christians persecuted other Christians who disagreed with them. Some countries adopted a policy of religious toleration. That is, while the government might support one religious organization, it allowed people of other beliefs to worship as they pleased. Our own national Constitution went further. It declares, "Congress shall make no law respecting an establishment of religion, or prohibiting the free exercise thereof." Since our state constitutions have statements very much like this, we therefore have complete religious freedom. This differs from toleration in that it separates "church" and "state" and makes religion entirely a personal matter.

Most of our states do not permit public money to be given to a school or hospital controlled by any one denomination. In every state, however, property owned by churches and used for religious purposes is exempt from taxation. To this extent the state shows respect for the churches.

Many of our states have laws forbidding or restricting the carrying on of business or amusements on Sunday. Some people assert that such laws violate the principle of religious freedom. But none of them command any person to do anything contrary to the teachings of his conscience or his religion. The most that can be said against them is that they forbid certain things which one may personally not consider wrong on Sunday. After all, one chief justification for Sunday laws is that any community is better off if its people observe one day in seven differently from the other days. Thus they have an opportunity for rest and recreation which will make them better fitted to do the work of the other six days of the week. 

What is meant by the "blue laws"? How did they get that name? Is it fair to allow some private businesses to be conducted for profit on Sunday and not all? What is your opinion of a person who says he can't enjoy himself on Sunday without going to the movies or the theater? 

88. Teaching Religion. Since religion means so much to the lives of people, many believe that it ought to form a part of our education. That is why Roman Catholics and a few others have parochial schools. In the public school, however, are found children from families of all faiths and of none at all. It would therefore be hardly fair to use public tax money to teach any particular creed. A few of our states even forbid the reading of the Bible in the schools on the ground that it is a sectarian book. 

Schools can be found which have the Koran in their libraries, but not the Bible. Is this consistent?

Our public schools must accept some responsibility for imparting high ideals of conduct and life to the children of modern families who do not see the inside of a church. In some communities a period is arranged when pastors may come to school to meet pupils of their faith, or when children may be excused to go to their own churches. We have Sunday schools, it is true, some of which are large and well organized. Yet it is asserted that they reach less than one-third of all the young people under the age of twentyfive, and that under such circumstances our nation is in grave danger of losing its spiritual power and its moral strength. In learn-

ing how to get rich and how to enjoy leisure, we must not lose sight of the higher values in the training of the human soul. 

In some states one can get school credit for Bible study done outside school. Do you think this is a good plan? From what you have seen of Sunday schools, do you think they do thorough work? 

How might they accomplish more? Can one get religion by study?

Many schools which do not offer religious teaching as such make it a practice to repeat the Lord's Prayer daily. Why is the Lord's 

Prayer acceptable to people of any faith? 

89. Differences among Denominations. It is not surprising that people differ about religious matters just as they do about almost every other topic. A great many people are connected with a particular denomination because it is the family church, and they have never had any special reason for departing from it. With some, attending a particular church is a matter of convenience. 

With others, it is the result of real conviction. 

Differences among denominations result not so much from teaching different ideals as from emphasizing different interpretations of the Scripture. Jewish people accept only the Old Testament as divinely given and look upon Jesus as just a great religious teacher. 

Catholics and Protestants both regard Jesus as the Son of God, but Protestants will not look upon the Pope as the head of the Church on earth. Many Protestant denominations differ so little about matters of creed and custom that in attending an ordinary service one might not discover what denomination was holding it. 

Followers of these different denominations are sincere in thinking that their church is a little nearer the truth than any of the others, but loyalty to one's own faith should not make one think harshly of members of other churches. Sometimes elections become religious fights as well as party fights. It has been well said in matters like this, " What we need is light, not heat." 

Make a diagram or outline to show the organization of the denominations to which you or your family belong. If you have attended the services of another church than your own, did you feel at home there? Why or why not?

The Catholic Bible contains a few books which are not commonly included in the Protestant versions. FOR

A CONNECTICUT COUNTRY CHURCH 

Courtesy Harry S. Myers

Here is a church built in the style of New England colonial times. It has a parsonage attached to it. People come from a considerable distance to attend services here.

Members of every faith have given their lives on the battlefield for our country and have taken active part in movements to uplift humanity in times of peace. In the World War, Catholics, Protestants, and Jews worked side by side in making life more pleasant for the soldier.

Churches often coöperate in holding services, especially in the summer. Pastors of different churches exchange pulpits. It is not unknown for a Jewish rabbi to invite a Christian minister to deliver a sermon, or vice versa. Thus better understanding comes through closer relationships. 

In the country districts, instead of having three or four small churches, no one of which amounts to much as a social force, a

federated church sometimes takes their place. The original churches may continue to exist as organizations in name, but they hold their services together and have one pastor to minister to the whole group. In missionary fields churches frequently agree that one denomination and no other may have the opportunity to work in a particular neigh- borhood.

What impression might be made upon a Chinese or a

THE Y. M. C. A. BUILDING, LITTLE ROCK, ARKANSAS 

This is one of many attractive and useful buildings put up by branches of this association in our cities and towns to aid in the social and religious life of our boys and young men. Similar work is carried on by the Knights of Columbus and Young Men's Hebrew Association. 

Besides their regular church organizations, the great religious bodies, Protestant, Catholic, and Hebrew, have their Y. M. C. A.'s and Y. W. C. A.'s, K. of C.'s, and Y. M. H. A.'s for the promotion of the higher interests of their members. Their activities are not limited to distinctly religious matters, but the religious aim always plays a part. The Salvation Army works outside of the church organizations, but is extremely active and sincere in trying to win to a better life the "down-and-outer." 

How much is your own church doing to promote the social welfare of your community? Are church suppers a good thing? Show how religion has aided the progress of other elements of culture and uplift in our lives. 

91. Our Own Moral Responsibility. If you and I are free, self-controlled people, we must not wait for the community to decide for us what we ought to do. We must set up our own high stand-

ards. We must regulate our own conduct so as to promote the good of all. We do set examples for others; surely we should set good ones rather than bad. "Am I my brother's keeper?" To what extent? Why? Did you ever see a child who had always been allowed to have his own way? What kind of individual was he? If you know that somebody intends to break a law or commit a wrong, have you any duty in the matter?

Some individuals have a higher sense of moral responsibility than others because of that elusive, intangible thing we call conscience. Ignoring its warnings repeatedly may stifle it to death, just as weeds will do to the pansies in our flower bed if we let them grow there. To keep our own conduct "on the level," to avoid leading others into error or wrong, each of us must think soberly of right or wrong when a question of moral standards into our path. 

comes Why may physical health and strength be dangerous if not accompanied by worthy moral ideals? Is there such a thing as a white lie? Should perfect truthfulness be a part of every one's moral code?

My attitude toward religion can be that of seeking the truth wherever I find it and accepting it when I become convinced that it is the truth. In this search for truth I can constantly try to be fair to those with whom I may not always agree. I can set up as my models and guides the great teachers of religion and morals and try to practice their teachings. I can keep on even when I make discouraging mistakes.

That side of our interests which concerns our mental and spiritual welfare, and which we refer to as the "higher life," is immensely important. A Frenchman a few years ago declared that we ought frequently to say to ourselves, "Every day in every way I am getting better and better." People got so that they made fun of this, but back of it there is a great truth. I can at least do my share to maintain the highest ideals that I know. 

Can a person be a good citizen and ignore matters of conduct and character? Zion City, Illinois, advertises itself as "the only place where it is easy to do right and difficult to do wrong." Should the people of every community through their government set up such an ideal for themselves?

Character and conduct are the result of many influences, show themselves in folkways, taboos, and fashions, are differently displayed in crowds and mobs than among individuals by themselves, express themselves through public opinion which is in turn formed through numerous sources, usually demand leaders to direct them, are determined by our moral standards and our ideals, are further influenced by religious beliefs and aspirations. 

Religious organizations offer the means of expressing people's religious feeling, render many kinds of services, are in this country free from governmental restraint, try by various means to train young people, differ chiefly in the interpretation of portions of Scripture, coöperate much more closely than formerly. 

It is the duty of each one of us to seek and follow the highest ideals.

PART II THE GOOD CITIZEN AND HIS GOVERNMENT CHAPTER X A TALK ABOUT GOVERNMENT IN GENERAL 

Governments, like clocks, go from the motion men give them and, as governments are made and moved by men, so by men they are ruined, too. Therefore, governments rather depend upon men than men upon governments. PENN Up to this point we have been talking chiefly about those desirable conditions and policies which make community life attractive. We depend greatly upon government to make these conditions possible. 

Government is a connecting link that binds together all our other interests and adds some new ones. It is one vast means of coöperation. Good citizens must be familiar with the machinery of government in order to accomplish the good things they desire. 

Upon what does the authority of government rest?

What kinds of government have existed in the world, and what kinds are found today?

Just what is the importance of each of the three departments of government — law-making, law-enforcing, and law-interpreting? 

Upon whom do we depend for each of these activities? 

Do we as citizens have any responsibilities in any of these lines? 

Americans believe in having constitutions; what is a constitution, and what is its place in our government? 

92. Upon What Does Government Rest?—Nobody is certain just how government began, though there must have been some form of it from the very early stages of human life. The strongest and wisest would naturally gain authority over others. Rulers used to say that they obtained their authority from God. This was the divine right idea of government. Maybe in one sense 

God did permit these rulers to have authority, but it is hard to 191

believe that everybody who ever sat upon a throne was God's direct choice for that purpose.

Our Declaration of Independence sets forth another theory. 

It says, "Governments are instituted among men, deriving their just powers from the consent of the governed." No government ever existed under which every person freely and openly consented to support all its policies. Yet people may consent to be governed by the wishes of the greater part of their number, even though they do not themselves agree with what the greater part may want. Indeed, if people put up with a bad government, they in one sense consent to it. 

Who are "the people"? Have all the people living in the United

States today definitely given their "consent" to the government under whose authority they live? If not, is it true that our government derives its just powers from the consent of the governed? 

Was the Mayflower Compact, drawn up in November, 1620, an example of government deriving its just powers from the consent of the governed?

Government carried on by one person, or in the name of one person, we call a monarchy. When that one person actually possesses complete authority, it is an absolute monarchy. If his power is limited, so that he is not free to act in all cases exactly as he pleases, we have a constitutional or limited monarchy. Ethiopia and Siam are still absolute monarchies. If a small group of people or a certain class gains control of the government, we have an oligarchy. If the ruling group is made up of wealthy or intelligent persons, or those who hold inherited titles, we usually call it an aristocracy. In the history of ancient countries, notably in some of the Greek cities, such governments were common. 

When the people as a whole determine how they shall be governed, we have a democracy. There have been few cases of a pure democracy in which the people as a whole vote directly on all the problems that come up. Usually that is not possible in a large community. We may speak of any country as a democracy in which the will of the people prevails. In this sense England, although it still has a king, is truly a democracy. It is spirit rather than form which is important. 

You may sometime get into an argument as to whether the United States is or is not a democracy. If you are talking about

the spirit of our government, by all means it is a democracy; but if you are talking about the form, the right answer may be "no."

It is a representative democracy or a republic, in which the people choose officers to carry on the work of government. Representation is a very important principle in government which the English were the first to work out in practice. 

Courtesy Foreign Service Buildings Office, Department of State THE NEW AMERICAN EMBASSY AT PARIS 

This building, which our government has just recently erected, occupies a notable site in the great French capital and is one of its attractive structures. 

But neither all republics nor all monarchies are organized just alike. In some of them almost all authority is centralized in the national government. This is the case in France, a centralized or unitary republic, and Italy, a centralized or unitary monarchy. 

The subdivisions of the country possess only such authority as the central government sees fit to allow them to exercise. On the other hand, either a monarchy or a republic might be a federation (§ 1). Germany used to be a federated monarchy, but then tried to be a republic. The United States is the most notable example of a federal republic. 

What changes in the form of governments have occurred in recent years? What kind of government would an ignorant people be likely to have or to prefer?

Look up the meaning of bureaucracy, autocracy, plutocracy. Find

from some textbook of history or book of reference examples of the different kinds of government. Why do you suppose the English people continue to have a king when his power has been reduced almost to zero? 

93. Governments in the Modern World. — From Great Britain many of the features of our own government have been derived. 

She still has her king, in whose name everything important in the government is done. His real political power, however, is small; but if, like King George V, he is likable and intelligent, he may have considerable influence in public affairs. 

Laws in Great Britain are made by the Parliament, composed of the House of Lords and the House of Commons. The members of the House of Commons are chosen by popular vote. The power of the House of Lords has very greatly declined. The Cabinet, headed by the Prime Minister, really controls the political business of the country, and is composed of members of Parliament who belong to the party which controls the House of Commons. 

Its laws are 

Who is Prime Minister now? What party does he represent?

France is a republic, but much different from ours. made by a Senate and a Chamber of Deputies. These two houses elect the President, but he has much less influence in the government than our own President. France has a Cabinet system very much like that of England, and the Premier, or Prime Minister, is the real head of the government as long as he can stay in office. 

The World War changed Germany from one of the proudest empires in the world to a republic, but one that rests on an unsteady foundation. It has (1933) a President, elected by the people, whose powers are between those of the French President and our own. Germany also has a Cabinet system, the head of which is called the Chancellor. Its chief law-making body, the 

Reichstag, is chosen by the people. 

Italy has kept her king, but he has no real power today (1933). 

A remarkable dictator named Mussolini has made himself master of the country and has done away with every sign of real democracy. 

His rule has brought Italy many benefits; but will the country be satisfied to continue always under that kind of government? 

Japan modeled its present government rather closely on what it thought England had. Much power is still in the hands of the

Emperor or Mikado, at least in theory; but all laws have to be approved by the Imperial Diet, which is composed of a House of Peers and a House of Representatives. The Cabinet is responsible to the Emperor.

Russia is now trying a system of government which is unlike any the world has seen. It calls itself a republic under the name of the Russian Socialist Federated Soviet Republic. This republic has several other sections under its control, and the combination is known as the 

Union of Socialist Soviet Re

The word 99 

66 soviet is new to the rest of the world. A soviet is an organization supposed to represent a certain class of people, as workers or soldiers. The fact is that the government is controlled by a few strong men who have been able to put and keep themselves in power by ruthless use of force. Whether a gov-

We need not be surprised if extensive changes occur in some of the governments mentioned in this section. Try to keep watch of notable events in the politics of these countries. 

94. The Progress of Democracy. - A century or so ago democracy did not mean just what it does today. When the United 

States began operating under the present Constitution, only a small part of the people were voters. Only free men could vote, and even they were often limited by religious requirements or propertyowning.

The first restrictions to be removed were those on religion. 

Gradually the requirement that a voter must own property has in most states been entirely removed, though in some states voters

The Civil War set the slaves free and gave the colored race the same right to vote as white people. 

Still half the people the women were denied the vote. 

Prominent women, such as Susan B. Anthony and Lucy Stone, protested that such an arrangement implied that women were as unfit to be voters as criminals, children, or idiots. The World War gave the ballot to women in many countries. Woman suffrage was not one of the matters about which the war was fought; but when women showed by their activity and courage that they were as good and patriotic citizens as the men, most of the opposition to their voting faded away.

Most of us take for granted that democracy is the best form of government. It may not always be the most efficient form; yet people usually prefer to take a chance on having government a little less efficient if they can be sure that they have a free government. 

Democracy works best only among an intelligent and educated people. To set up democracy suddenly among backward and inexperienced people might be the same as letting a baby play with a gun. Democracy has had its ups and downs, but the story of human government is to a great extent a story of the struggle of the people to get or keep self-government. 

Would an absolute monarchy governed by a wise and good man have any advantages over a republic composed of half-educated people without experience in self-government? Is the same kind of government suitable for all countries? Could tyranny ever exist in this country? What do you think Wilson meant by saying, "The world must be made safe for democracy"? 

95. Some Terms in Government That We Should Understand. - A word we sometimes use in discussing government is sovereignty. 

It means the supreme power in a government. In an absolute monarchy it belonged to the king or emperor or whatever else he might be called. That is why we still sometimes refer to kings as sovereigns, even when they are mere figureheads. There is some sovereign power in any government, regardless of its form. We think of it as in the hands of the people as a whole, and believe that the people exercise it by means of kings, presidents, parliaments, or congresses.

66 When we speak of a state, in the broad and general meaning of the word, we mean an independent organization of people for the purpose of government. Great Britain, the United States, or 

Sweden would be a state in that sense. We may think of a nation" without thinking of its government - not so in using the word state." We use it in this country to signify one of the parts of our Union, but that is not the original meaning of the word. 66

Majority rules," often say. In such a government as ours we have to adopt the ideas that satisfy the larger number of people -the majority-rather than those that may be held very sincerely and strongly by a smaller group — the minority. If your class wants to hold a picnic and fifteen of you want to go to Prospect Park while only ten prefer

West View, common fairness would say that the preference of the fifteen, other things being equal, should get first consideration. 

Some matters are really not suitable for decision by majority vote. 

What do you think of the following in this respect: the election of a class president; the fixing of the grade of John Smith, a member of the class; the determination of the time when class meetings shall be held (other than class recitations); the right of Mary 

Brown to propose giving a present to the teacher?

When, if ever, is it desirable to require a unanimous vote for anything? Is the majority always on the right side? Prove your answer from facts of history or matters of common knowledge. 

What is the duty of a good citizen when he feels that the desire of the majority in connection with a matter is decidedly wrong?

There is a difference, especially when we speak of counting votes, between a majority and a plurality. A majority of votes is more than half the entire number and is reckoned as being the difference between the highest vote and all the other votes put together. 

Many times in elections nobody has a majority; but unless there is a tie, somebody will have a plurality. That means a greater number of votes than some other candidate's; and it is the difference between the highest candidate's votes and the next highest, or between any other two whom we might want to compare. 

The distribution of the work of our government into three departments-legislative, executive, and judicial-we sometimes speak of as the separation of powers. Our forefathers were afraid that one department might sometime become so strong as to endanger the liberties of the people unless it should be restrained in some way. They worked out, therefore, an elaborate system of checks and balances to avert this danger, by means of which the different departments may restrain one another. 

The President may veto a bill passed by Congress, or the Supreme Court may set it aside if the justices believe its provisions are contrary to the Constitution. Congress, in turn, has the right to start impeachment proceedings against a president, a judge, or any other federal officer. If such an official is found guilty of the charges of misconduct made against him, he will be removed. 

In most state and city governments there are similar arrangements. Some think we have "checked" some officials so much that they cannot do anything thoroughly. 

Can you find legislative, executive, and judicial departments in your home, your church, your school, or a big business concern? 

Are there any "checks and balances" in these organizations? If not, why not? 

96. Law-Making and Its Importance. We cannot get along without laws; we must have some rules set down definitely in black and white, telling us what to do and, perhaps, how to do it. 

It is sometimes said, as if excusing ourselves for not trying to correct evils, "You cannot make men good by law." Maybe so, but you can make it easy for good people to do the right thing and make it more unpleasant for those who are not willing of their own accord to be good citizens. Generally speaking, we should not pass laws for the special benefit of one class or a few individuals.

We should never have laws which distinctly harm the masses for the benefit of a few. A law which is hard to understand or is capable of being interpreted in more than one way is another poor sort of law. Some firms, whose standard of honor is none too high, employ attorneys whose only business is to see how the firm can evade the law. 

What is your opinion of such laws as the following: (a) taxing the number of windows in a building; (b) regulating the size of windows in buildings; (c) exempting printers from paying taxes; (d) forbidding the use of face powder by girls; (e) forbidding the driving of pleasure cars after 10 P.M.; (f) fixing the time when county taxes should be paid?

Suppose we find or believe that a law is bad. Some people have the queer notion that they can simply ignore it and do as they please. If a person were obliged to obey only the laws which he likes, we could not expect some people to attend school or others to refrain from breaking into people's houses. We have a perfect right, however, to urge that any law which we think is unwise or wrong should be changed, provided we do it in a fair and straightforward way.

We may change (amend) a law or do away with (repeal) it by the same method by which it was passed. One legislature may set aside laws which the legislatures before it have passed. This kind of thing should not be done too often, or nobody would be sure what the laws commanded at any particular time. Even our constitutions, state and national, may be amended by striking out something as well as by adding. The method of doing it is the same in both cases.

We may be very sure that any law against which there is any real objection will be tested by some lawful and proper means. 

After all, the good citizen will not ask, "How few laws need I support?" but, "What laws will best promote the people's welfare?" 

Are laws anything more than good manners in written form? Are laws solely for the purpose of preventing people from doing what they would like to do?

Most laws are made by the representatives of the people and not by the people acting directly. Yet there may be times when it

is well to let all the voters express their opinion about certain matters. To do this a considerable number of our states have adopted the practices known as the "initiative" and the 66 referendum."

The initiative gives a certain percentage of the voters in a district or state the right to sign a petition asking that a certain law be presented to the voters for their consideration. The submitting of a law or other proposition to the people to vote on directly is called the referendum. The initiative, you can see, is of little use unless the referendum goes along with it. 

In every state but one, the adopting of a new constitution, or the making of amendments to a constitution, is done by a referendum vote. In some states, these are the only matters which the people may vote on directly, except proposals for borrowing a large amount of money. The initiative and the referendum make a gov-

Do you have the initiative and referendum in your state or local community? If so, are the people, in general, satisfied? If these practices have not been adopted, would you recommend them? 

Some people seem 

97. Law Enforcement and Its Importance. 

to think that when a law is made everything is settled. A number of good people made that mistake by expecting that when the

Eighteenth Amendment was adopted the liquor business would at once pass out. Much of the trouble with the enforcement of the amendment came from the fact that when people began to disregard it, nothing serious happened to them. 

The first requirement in enforcing laws is to get officials who are thoroughly honest, intelligent, and earnest. Not everybody can 

Courtesy Bureau of Animal Industry DISINFECTING A STOCK CAR 

One of the duties of federal inspectors in this bureau is to see that cattle cars are kept safe for the animals so that disease may not spread from them to human beings. How has this governmental activity called for, and how might it call for, legislative, executive, or judicial service on the part of the government? be a good executive official even if he tries. Sometimes officials are too stupid to recognize when laws are violated or to know how to get at the offender.

The influence of politicians is sometimes seen in very unpleasant ways. They get their friends or followers appointed to jobs for which they are not fitted. A constable or policeman may catch somebody in a crime and arrest the guilty party, only to find later that the criminal has never been brought to trial. Somebody "higher up" has " applied the fix," as they say. If this happens repeatedly, what heart has a policeman to do his own duty faithfully?

Are there any circumstances which would justify letting a person off who knowingly broke a law? Some states have laws limiting the speed of automobiles to twenty or twenty-five miles an hour, and then lay good concrete highways on which it is hard to keep a good car down to that speed. What is to be done in such a case?

Much depends upon the sentiment of the people. If by some means a law has been passed which the majority does not believe in, many evasions will occur and the most vigilant officials may have a hard time catching the offenders. Americans do not have a first-class reputation for either obeying or enforcing laws. 

It takes backbone to be a good law-enforcement officer. No one has any right to accept such a position unless he is willing to risk being unpopular for performing his duty. At the same time no one of us has any right to make it hard for a conscientious officer to do his work. To obey laws is one of the very first requirements and proofs of good citizenship.

Make a list of the qualities that a good law-enforcement officer ought to possess and give some examples of occasions when you know of such qualities being displayed. Can we establish the habit of respect for law? Mention some cases in which private citizens can properly aid in the enforcement of laws. 

98. Law Interpretation and Its Importance. Even when laws are drawn up clearly and carefully, however, a question may arise as to whether certain laws apply to a particular case. Besides, a law-making body may pass a law which others think is outside its authority. You know that in the first years under our national 

Constitution very important differences of opinion existed between the strict constructionists and loose constructionists as to the right of Congress to pass laws about certain matters. 

To explain and interpret the law is a big duty of our courts, in addition to hearing the cases of people who are accused of breaking the law. In this country, more than in any other large country, the courts have exercised the right to decide whether certain laws are in accordance with the Constitution. If the courts decide that a law is contrary to the Constitution, they will not permit it to be enforced.

Not everybody can be a good judge. A judge must be entirely impartial. He must have courage and firmness, for his decision

may determine whether wicked men will have their own way. 

A judge must know the law. How can any one interpret and apply law if he is ignorant of it himself?

Name five or six people who hold positions as judges or magistrates. What is your opinion and the opinion of the community in regard to them? Can any one other than a lawyer be a judge?

As individual citizens we may sometimes have a part in the interpreting and applying of laws. We may be called upon to act as witnesses in court. It is our duty to tell clearly and courageously just what we know about the case in question. Sometimes, too, we may be required to serve as jurors and hear the testimony in a case that comes before the court. The juror has to be like a judge in that he must also be impartial and courageous. We cannot get away from responsibility for good government to some extent ourselves, whether it is in obeying laws or coöperating with officials in enforcing them.

What temptations would a witness need to guard against if he were called upon to testify in a case in court? Who do you think would make the best kind of juror, one who knew nothing at all about a case, or one who had heard about it but was still openminded enough to be influenced by the evidence given in court? Is it possible these days for an intelligent person to know nothing whatever of important cases that may come up in court? 

99. Constitutions and Their Importance. - Americans are great believers in constitutions. A constitution may be defined as a law or a group of laws which sets forth the form of government and the powers which it may exercise. We think of a constitution as a fundamental thing, and only fundamental matters should go into it. 

Since people may change their minds about the form and powers of their government, it is necessary to provide in the constitution the way to change it. It is usually well to make it more difficult to change a constitution than to repeal or pass an ordinary law. 

Which of the following matters would probably go into a state constitution, and which would be dealt with by ordinary laws: the length of term or the powers of the governor; the main divisions of the state courts; the salary of members of law-making bodies; the length of term in the school districts of the state; the area of state forests; general laws in regard to the making of new counties; provisions for amending the constitution; regulations as to the width of highways?

Most constitutions are considerably alike in form. They usually begin with a statement of the reasons for drawing up the constitution which is known as the preamble. The main body of the constitution is usually divided into articles and the articles are further subdivided into sections. All the state constitutions except one have a Bill of Rights, which sets forth the rights and privileges of the people with which the government must not interfere. In the United States Constitution this was added as a series of amendments after the original document had been adopted and had gone into effect.

Find a copy of your state constitution and see what the different "Articles" deal with. Does your city or other local community have a constitution, or something like one? Do clubs and societies which you know about have constitutions? If so, why? What do they include?

Most civilized countries feel the way we do about the importance of a written constitution. Great Britain, however, has no one document of this kind. The important laws, charters, and customs which have been handed down from the past serve as a sort of constitution. We often speak of Great Britain as having an unwritten constitution.

Do you think of any reason why the people of the United States should prefer to have a definite written constitution? Which kind is better for France? for China? for Germany? for Brazil?

Even in our own country, certain practices are observed with almost as much respect as if they were in the Constitution itself. 

They might be called our own "unwritten constitution." In our national Constitution, for instance, the word "cabinet" does not appear, yet we have a very real President's Cabinet. In electing the President, the states are required to choose presidential electors, but by custom they always vote for the candidate who has already been named by the party that has chosen them. We cannot be sure that these practices will never be changed, but it is plain that we have something like an "unwritten constitution." 

It has been our custom not to elect a person to the office of President for more than two terms. Do you think it is best to let that custom continue to be merely a custom or should it be written in the Constitution?

100. Our Responsibility in Government. Shall we say that a good government is one under which the people are happy and progressive? If so, who makes it that way? Surely it is not entirely a matter of form, for no two governments are exactly alike; and under any form of government there have been good times and bad times. 

THE GATES OF ST. AUGUSTINE OP. A. Wolfe 

In this Florida city one may find many relics of the varied experiences through which this oldest town in the United States has passed. What other protection besides a wall and cannons does St. Augustine need? 

It must get down to this. If the people themselves are lawabiding, anxious to improve themselves, desirous of treating one another and their neighbors in other nations fairly, they will insist that their officials serve honestly, faithfully, and to the best of their ability. If our country is to have a good government, then it rests with our own people. Read again' the quotation from 

William Penn with which this chapter began. Isn't it true? Government as we see it, rests upon the consent of the governed, has taken various forms,

among most modern nations changes its leaders to agree with public sentiment, has in our own country, and many others, steadily become more democratic, is characterized by such significant terms as sovereignty, state, representation, and, among us, separation of powers, rests in the first place upon the making of good laws, depends for its effectiveness upon the enforcement of laws, requires often that laws shall be interpreted and applied, has in most countries its fundamental features expressed in a constitution.

CHAPTER XI POLITICAL PARTIES AND ELECTIONS 

All who possess political rights should have the opportunity to acquire the knowledge which will make their share in the government a blessing and not a danger. GRANT In every country where the people have a distinct voice in their government there are political parties. Sometimes we wish we could get along without them, but we have not learned how to do so. 

If we must have them, how can they be used for good rather than harm? 

A citizen needs to know about them and about the laws governing elections, if he is going to play an intelligent part in our government. 

Why do political parties exist, and how are they managed? 

Is the effect of parties in our political life good or harmful? 

How are candidates for office brought before the people?

What are our laws and customs in regard to the holding of elections?

What qualifications are required of voters and of office-holders? 

What reforms or changes in the methods of voting have been proposed with the idea of bringing about better politics? 

101. The Work of Political Parties. If you wanted to be elected to office, or were particularly anxious to have some other person elected, how would you go about it? Unless you live in a small community, it would be hard for you to see more than a small part of the voters. You might make speeches on street corners or anywhere else that people meet, or over the radio, but you could not compel voters to listen to you. If you undertook to do these things all by yourself, you would find that it takes more time and money than most of us have to spend. 

So a group of people who hold similar views on public questions may form a political party. When they work together, they are much more likely to put into power officials who will carry out their ideas than if each voter acts by himself. It is the old story again, "In union there is strength." 209

There is nothing in the Constitution of the United States to show that its makers expected there would be political parties. 

Parties became prominent soon after it went into effect, however; for arguments arose over the interpretation of the Constitution, the powers that the government ought to have, and numerous other questions.

JOHN REHMS WILLIAM HUGHEY AN OHIO BALLOT 

This is a sample of the "party column ballot. As on the Pennsylvania ballot, one may vote a straight ticket by making one cross. Only parties that get a certain percentage of all the votes may have a special symbol and circle. 

The original political parties have long since disappeared. 

The two leading parties of today, the Republicans and the Democrats, have been rivals three-quarters of a century. (Be sure you do not confuse this Republican party with the party that used that name in the time of Washington and Jefferson.) But if you can tell just what is the difference between these parties today, you will do better than millions of citizens who vote under those party names. As the years go on, different questions come up; either the old parties take a stand on these new questions, or the old parties disappear and new ones are formed. Shortlived parties may be organized for some local election or for a special occasion.

Parties that expect to take part in elections time after time need to have organizations to carry on campaigns, to plan for holding conventions and other meetings, and to raise money to pay the bills. Our big parties have a very thorough organization. It may begin as far down as the voting districts or precincts. One or more persons from each voting district may be members of a ward committee. Members of this ward committee may also belong to or send representatives to the city or county committee. State committees are also made up of persons chosen by the party from different districts in the state. There is also a national committee consisting of one man and one woman from each state in the Union. This committee is most active in years when a president is to be chosen.

What form of local organizations do the parties have in your community, county, and state? Is there any advantage in permanent organization of a party for any district smaller than the state? 

Ask five people from different families to tell you what they think is the difference between the Republican and the Democratic parties today. See whether their answers agree or disagree. 

102. The Effects of Parties. Parties must render some service or else they would not exist. Parties help voters to center their support on certain candidates instead of distributing it among so many that nobody will receive any large vote. Parties stir up interest and rivalry in political campaigns which is good as long as it is fair and honorable. They help to get voters out to the polls on election day. If there is more than one strong party, the party which is out of power will be ready to point out any mistakes or any misconduct for which the party in power is responsible.

There is another side to the story, too. Sometimes parties are concerned not with the success of ideas but with the profit that will come through graft or some other form of dishonesty. Sometimes a few men get control of a party, and this group, or party "machine," will try to win elections time after time simply to be sure that its own gang will stay in office. Sometimes even controls a party in a city or state and rules it in his own interest. Parties may stir up disagreement and trouble when there is no occasion for it. one man 

99 a political" boss 99

Do you think bosses are more likely to appear in large cities or in country districts? How can you tell the difference between a party organization which tries simply to keep the party in good shape for the next election and that undesirable situation when the organization has become a self-seeking "machine"? Why do people ever put up with dishonest officials or party leaders? 

103. The Good Citizen's Relations with Parties. What should be a good citizen's attitude toward political parties? 

Should he connect himself definitely with one party or consider himself independent of all of them?

Ohio elects its judges without reference to any party names.

Should other states follow that example? 

A voter who wants to do the right thing does not always know just what to believe about candidates for office.

In political campaigns all sorts of unfounded charges are made regarding parties or candidates. Newspapers fail to help the voter much, for they themselves are often partisan and unwilling to present both sides of a disputed question. This is where one's civic education ought to help him to think for himself and to pass some intelligent judgment on the claims put forth by different candidates for office. 

Do honest people ever hold incorrect opinions about candidates and parties? What are the party beliefs or prejudices of the newspapers which you most frequently read? Why do you think the members of your family vote as they usually do at elections?

A person who calls himself a member of a particular party is not under any obligation to support every candidate who runs under that party name. If there are six or eight offices to be filled at an election and James Brown, who is running for county treasurer on the Republican ticket, has a bad name, Re-

publican voters would be perfectly justified in not voting a "straight ticket." If the Democratic candidate is a good clean man, they could "split" the ticket and vote for him for treasurer, while supporting the Republican candidates for other offices. 

It might be an advantage not to use national party names in contests for local offices. What difference does it make whether a man calls himself a Republican or a Democrat, if all that is required of him is intelligence, good judgment, and honesty! 

Is independent voting better encouraged by trying to abolish the use of party names, or by cultivating a practice of intelligent selection on the part of voters? 

104. Choosing Candidates for Office. When we use party names, there must be some way of finding out who are to represent the different parties for mayor, governor, or some other office. 

Even if party names were not used, voters might be able to choose more intelligently from two or three candidates than from six or eight. To make these selections of candidates, we hold primary elections, so called because they are the first or primary step in the process of filling offices. The picking out of candidates to be voted for in a regular election we refer to as nomination. (Be sure you understand the difference between nomination and election.) 

It used to be the custom to make nominations by a caucus or convention. A caucus is a meeting held by the members of a party in some limited district, as a city ward, or by the members of a party in a state legislature or Congress. The word convention usually refers to a large body of delegates chosen to represent their party. Party bosses so often controlled caucuses and conventions that most states now use the direct primary to take the place of the earlier methods of nominating candidates.

The direct primary is an election carried on like any other election, at which all the members of a party within a district or state may indicate whom they would like to have chosen as that party's candidates for various offices. Politicians sometimes control direct primaries as well as conventions; but if they do control them, it is the fault of the voters themselves. 

What is the law of your state with reference to the holding of primary elections? In some states, particularly in the South, a person must have a majority of all the votes cast at a primary in order to

Compare the four styles of state ballots shown in this chapter and see which you like best. be his party's candidate. If no candidate gets a majority, there will be a "run-off" primary between the two highest in the first election. Do you think that would be a good practice to adopt generally?

Why do you suppose that in many states voters do not turn out for primary elections in as large numbers as at the general election? 

In some other states, particularly in the South, voters participate in the primaries even more fully than in November elections. Can you explain that fact? Why is it not likely that the direct primary will be entirely satisfactory for nominating candidates for the presidency?

Voters who are not satisfied with the candidates of the regular parties may sign a petition asking to have a person's name printed on the ballot at the regular election as a candidate for some office. 

Candidates nominated in this way may not have as good a chance for election as they would if presented by a regular party; but they

sometimes do win. To wait until election day and then add a candidate's name to the ballot in pencil or with " stickers " is a way still more difficult, although even such a method has succeeded. 

Weeks or months usually elapse between the nomination and the election. Just as bitter campaigns are sometimes carried on for mayor and other local officers as for the presidency itself, with even more temptation to cheat. Our politics are not on the level that they should be unless every election is a fair and open contest between honest people who would prefer to lose than to win by dishonorable means. 

Make a list of what you consider honorable ways by which you would try to get people to support your party candidate. What do you think are right and what do you think are wrong uses of money in elections? Why do the laws of many states require candidates and campaign committees to report the amount of money they spend and the purpose for which it is spent? 

105. How Elections Are Conducted. The Tuesday after the first Monday in November has been adopted as the day on which general elections take place. In some states, elections for local officers occur on other days; and Maine still chooses its governor and congressmen in September. In still other states elections for local officers are held in the odd-numbered years and elections for state and national officers in the even-numbered years, so as to make it easier for voters to distinguish between the questions at stake in national elections and those in local elections. Primary elections are held on whatever dates a state sees fit to hold them. 

What are the laws of your state with reference to the holding of primary and local elections? Is any attempt made to separate local and national elections? If so, how is that done? 66 Elections today are conducted much better than formerly. 

Once votes were announced out loud viva voce," as the expression is. The first step in advance came when written or printed ballots were permitted. But it was easy to stuff the ballot box and too difficult to split the ticket.

A MASSACHUSETTS BALLOT 

Notice that the names of candidates in connection with each office are arranged in alphabetical order. There is no party square, so one must vote for each office separately. Massachusetts, like Pennsylvania and a number of other states, no longer prints on the ballot the names of all the candidates for presidential electors put up by the different parties. 

York and Massachusetts ballots on which persons are compelled to vote separately for each office. Which of these ballots do you think is most likely to encourage independent voting and which voting a straight party ticket?

Be sure you understand the form of ballot used in your own state in comparison with the forms in this book. What arrangement do you think is fairest for all concerned? How long has your state had the Australian ballot system? Prove that the Australian ballot encourages independent voting and reduces the chances of bribing voters.

A means of casting votes which is year by year becoming more common is the voting machine a machine set up in such a way that the front looks much like an enlarged paper ballot. There are buttons or levers which a voter turns to mark the candidates

for whom he wishes to vote. In states that permit straight ticket voting, one lever will vote for all the candidates of a certain party. 

Since the machine records each vote as it is cast, all that has to be done when the election is over is to open the machine and call off 

THE PRESIDENT AT A VOTING MACHINE 

Franklin D. Roosevelt, like many other

New York voters, has been working one of these machines on election day for many years instead of marking a ballot. the figures that are recorded for each office. New York 

State has used voting machines more extensively than any other. 

Does your state or any part of it use the voting machine? If it does not, has it ever been seriously considered? Do you see any disadvantages in the use of this machine? 

Too often we have to fight against dishonesty in elections. 

The first chance for fraud often comes in the making of the list of persons qualified to vote.

For this reason, many states require voters, at least in their large cities, to appear before a registration board some days or weeks before an election and have their residence, age, and other details recorded, so that only the proper persons may vote and no one may vote on another's name.

An immense amount of responsibility rests upon the local election board in a voting district or precinct. If the election board is dishonest or without backbone, all kinds of things may happen. 

Honest and intelligent people have sometimes been unwilling to give their time to act as officials on election day, even though they were paid for doing so. Voters ought never to let such boards be controlled by ignorant or dishonest political workers. 

Do you know the boundaries of the election district in which the members of your family vote? Do the members of your family vote regularly? If not, why not? Can a person properly consider himself a good citizen if he fails to take part in elections?

106. The Qualifications of Voters. The national government has very little to say concerning the qualifications of voters. In the Fifteenth Amendment to the Constitution, the states are forbidden to deny the vote "on account of race, color, or previous condition of servitude"; the Nineteenth Amendment forbade 

CASTING A BALLOT 

The election board are making entries in the voting lists as voters go through the process of marking their ballots and putting them in the ballot boxes. 

In many cases today there would be one or more women in a picture of an election board. states to deny the right to vote on account of sex. But as long as these two requirements are met, the federal government has nothing to say about the qualifications which shall be set up. 

In every state now at least three qualifications are necessary: 

1. The voter must be 21 years old. 

2. He must be a citizen of the United States. 

3. He must be a resident of the state where he wants to vote. 

The length of time a person must live in a state before gaining the right to vote depends upon the state. Almost everywhere, too, there is a requirement in regard to residence in the county, city, or voting district for a certain length of time. How is it in your state?

Read this and then write the answers. Read it as many times as you need to.

February and the summer games in August. Both men and women took part in these games. There were representatives from fifteen nations in the various contests at Lake Placid and forty-six at Los Angeles. In ancient times the 

Olympic Games were held in the city of Olympia, Greece. The first Olympic
