A NEW YORK STATE LITERACY TEST FOR Voters 

This is an exact copy of one of the tests given in 1932 for those who wanted to qualify as voters in New York State and could not present a school diploma. What do you think of it as a basis for determining a voter's intelligence? Would you justify any one who could not pass this test in thinking that he ought to vote? ences.

Outside of these uniform requirements, there are many differ- Some states still require tax-paying, but the tax is so low that few would be kept from voting on that account. In other states a person may have to show that he can read, write, or understand the state constitution. Perhaps New York has gone the farthest of any state in setting up educational requirements. 

A person, to become a voter in that state, must show a diploma or certificate from a school official testifying that he graduated from eighth grade or high school, or take an examination to prove that he is able to understand a simple paragraph of English. 

Do you believe a literacy test will prove that a person is going to be a good citizen? If not, is there any use in giving it? What do

you suppose the arguments may have been to support laws requiring voters to be tax-payers? Why is the voting requirement that one must understand the state constitution open to abuse? 

There is one queer thing about this business of voting. If a state legislature should pass a law saying that 100,000 people who had been voters should no longer vote, wouldn't it start something! Yet in state after state many more than that number of voters never go near the polls on election day. Our country won't be a representative democracy unless the people turn out in large enough numbers to show us how they do feel about candidates or political issues. Let us hope that every one of you will resolve to do your full duty as a voting citizen as soon as the state lets you have that privilege. 

In Russia people are allowed to vote as soon as they are eighteen. 

Is the United States wiser in putting twenty-one as the minimum age?

If you know people who never vote, or vote so rarely that it does not matter, have you ever heard them give any reasons for their neglect? What do you suppose those reasons could be? It used to be said that the only excuse for failing to keep a dinner engagement was "battle, murder, or sudden death." 

Should any less serious excuse be accepted for failing to vote? 

107. Selecting Officeholders. It is a little difficult to state in exact terms all the qualities desirable in officeholders. By law or constitution we can set up requirements of age and residence; and we ought to include United States citizenship, although even that is not always insisted upon. The nature of the office frequently suggests certain conditions. In the case of a judge, it is often required that he be "learned in the law." A member of a 

Public Service Commission ought not to be a stockholder in any railroad that might have cases coming before that commission. 

Too often party politics gets as much consideration as actual fitness. Officials are chosen by one of three methods. 

Members of law-making bodies and the highest officials in the executive branches of a government are almost invariably chosen by popular vote. This practice rests upon the idea that the people themselves should choose those officers who are going to determine the policies of their government. One disadvantage is that voters may not always know the candidates well enough to tell who out of three or four is really best for the place.

A considerable number of offices must always be filled by direct appointment by the President, governor, or corresponding officer. 

If we are going to make a person responsible for the administration of a country or state, we must be willing to let him have congenial advisers and hope that he will choose people fitted to do their duty. 

For the great mass of executive offices we can set up civil service examinations to find out whether applicants have suitable education and intelligence and, in many cases, whether they have special preparation for particular kinds of service. No one should be appointed to a responsible place in the Foreign Service, for instance, who knows nothing of international law. To work in the 

Bureau of Chemistry, one may need to have a college training in that field. These are things which can be tested more or less successfully by examination. Along with them we need some evidence as to character, dependability, and practical experience. 

When judges are chosen, the best selections are likely to be made by appointment by an officer who can look carefully into a man's learning, experience, and fair-mindedness. But in most of our states the feeling that the judges are the servants of the people and should represent the people is strong enough to cause judgeships to be filled by popular vote. 

To what extent do the three methods mentioned above prevail in your state or local government? Why do you suppose we have so few appointments for life or "good behavior" in state governments? What qualities could you not test very well by a written examination? 

108. How to Remove Unworthy Officials. If only every person elected or appointed to an office proved worthy of the honor he had received! But sometimes officials do not try to give the office the time and attention required; sometimes they are actually dishonest; sometimes they try hard enough, but do not have the qualities necessary to make good in the position. So we must have some means of getting rid of incapable or unworthy officeholders.

One way to remove officers is by the same authority that made the selection in the first place. If the responsibility of selecting officials has been given to a certain officer, he also should have the right to remove those officers who do not measure up to the

desirable standards. The President, therefore, may remove members of his Cabinet and many other national officers; governors and mayors often may remove heads of departments and lower officials in state and city governments. 

There are some exceptions, however, to this general power of removal. Where persons have shown their fitness by civil service examinations, they ought not to have to worry about being removed whenever a different party gains control of the government. 

Definite charges must be brought against an official accused of wrongdoing, and he may not be dismissed without a hearing and verification of the charges. Judges, too, ought to feel a sense of independence while they are in office, so they may perform their duties "without fear or favor." In the federal government, therefore, judges may be removed only by impeachment. 

Impeachment is the bringing of formal charges against an executive or judicial officer by the law-making body. In the national government, impeachment charges are brought by the House of Representatives through the passing of a resolution which formally accuses an officer of violating some particular law or being guilty of undesirable conduct. A person may be impeached without being convicted. If a national officer who has been impeached does not resign from office, he is tried before the Senate somewhat as he would be tried in any court. If two-thirds of the senators vote that he is guilty, he will be removed from office and may be disqualified from holding any other official position in the United States. In the state governments there are somewhat similar arrangements for impeachment. 

What would you say was probably the most famous impeachment case in our national history? Look in your state constitution and find just how impeachment cases are handled in your state. Why do you suppose a two-thirds vote of the United States Senate is required to convict a person rather than just a majority? 

None of the above methods of removing officers applies to members of law-making bodies. They are usually subject to removal only by the body to which they belong. By a two-thirds vote the 

Senate or the House of Representatives may expel from its membership one whom they consider unworthy. Similar arrangements usually exist in state governments.

Still another method, known as the recall, is used in some states which have gone very far in giving the people a direct voice in their government. Under this method a certain percentage of the Cincinnati

Regular City Election NOVEMBER 3, 1931 DIRECTIONS TO VOTERS 

Put the figure 1 in the square opposite the name of your first choice. Express your second, third and other choices by putting the figure 2 opposite the name of your second choice, the figure 8 opposite the name of your third choice, and so on. You may express thus as many choices as you please, without any regard to the number being elected. 

Your ballot will be counted for your first choice if it can be used to help elect him. If it can not help elect him, it will be transferred to the highest of your other choices whom it can help. 

You can not hurt any of those you prefer by marking lower choices for others. The more choices you express, the surer you are to make your ballot count for one of them. But do not feel obliged to express choices which you do not really have. name.

Do not put the same figure opposite more than one If you spoil this ballot, tear it across once, return it to the election officer in charge of the ballots and get another from him.

The Directions to Voters" tell you the main facts about marking and counting the ballot. The names of the candidates are arranged in alphabetical order, but names are transferred from the top to the bottom of the list in different districts so that the candidates will all have an equal chance to have their names on the upper part of the ballot. voters of a city or state may sign a petition asking for a special election to be held at which the voters decide whether the person who is in office shall continue or be obliged to quit. Sometimes he is allowed to be a candidate at the special election along with others. Sometimes the recall is linked up with the initiative and referendum, and the three referred to as the

Is the recall in use in your state to any extent? 

If so, what are the provisions under which it may be employed? What arguments might be offered for or against the use of the recall? 

109. Some Proposed Changes in Election Methods. 

Many who are interested in good government think that in our zeal for democracy we have had the people elect more officers than they can choose intelligently. The principle known as the short ballot is much favored by advanced students of government - that is, only a few officers shall be chosen by popular vote, and those few shall then be held responsible for the management of public affairs. The supposition is that if the people vote

for just a few officers, it will be easier to learn the qualifications of candidates.

Our state and county governments have been open to criticism for having too many officers chosen directly by the people. Several states, notably New York, Illinois, Virginia, and Maine, have amended their constitutions within the last few years to reduce the number of state officials elected and allow the governor to appoint more of them.

Another fault that has been found with our systems of election is that often the winning party gets altogether too complete control of the government. To give the minority party more nearly its fair share of representation, various plans of proportional representation have been proposed. Occasionally the method of cumulative voting has been permitted. If there were three county commissioners or three members of a legislative body to be chosen in a certain district, the voters might be allowed to cast three votes and distribute them as they pleased, even giving all three to one candidate if they desired.

Do you think of any class of people who might not want proportional representation in electing officers? 

Some form of preferential voting is more commonly recommended today. Under such a system a voter marks his ballot for first, second, third, and maybe further choice, among the candidates for a certain office. Then the second and perhaps further choices may be counted in accordance with some understood arrangement. The idea of this method is to give every party or group a voice in the government in proportion to its actual strength among the voters in a community. 

This is the way one system of preferential voting works. Suppose at an election 100 ballots are cast, with four candidates to be elected and six candidates running. First a quota is determined which will be the number of votes necessary to elect a candidate. In this case the quota would be 21, because not more than four people could get 

21 first-choice votes out of 100 ballots. 

Now suppose John Smith receives 30 first-choice votes. Since he needs only 21 to elect him, nine votes which he doesn't need are turned over to the candidates who have been marked as second choice. If James Brown had 18 first-choice votes to begin with and three of these nine were marked for him as second choice, they would

be added to his 18, giving him 21 and electing him; the other six of Smith's surplus would be distributed among the other candidates. 

Then the lowest candidate among the first-choice votes would be ruled out, and his votes distributed among the other candidates in accordance with the way they were marked for second choice, or third choice, if necessary.

This process would go on until four candidates had received 21 votes each, counting their first choices plus the second or third choices that they got from some other candidates' ballots. 

If only one candidate was to be elected to an office, and no one person received a majority of first-choice votes, then the votes for the lowest candidate among the first-choice votes would be distributed among the other candidates in accordance with the way they were marked for second choice, and transfers would go on from the low candidates until some one received a majority of all the votes. 

It is important that the method by which voters elect public officers be simple and effective in representing the people's will. 

But if voters are ignorant and careless, any system of election will probably put poor officials into power. Every voter and everybody who is going to be a voter might make this resolution: "Whenever elections are held, I will vote regularly, honestly, thoughtfully, courageously." If every voter would do that, what a tremendous difference it would make in the quality of our government and in the success of Democracy!

In a government by the people political parties are usually active, the effects of parties are sometimes good and sometimes harmful, the good citizen should know how to use parties for the good of the whole community, candidates for office are selected most often by direct primaries, elections should be carried on so as to permit voters to express their preference freely, voters must meet prescribed requirements, office-holders to serve the people should possess proper qualifications, means are provided for removing unworthy officials, election methods may be always open to improvement.

CHAPTER XII OUR NATIONAL GOVERNMENT 

To inform the minds of the people and to follow their will is the chief duty of those placed at their head. - JEFFERSON

Public office is a public trust. CLEVELAND 

Every intelligent person knows something about our national government. Our local government touches us dozens of times where the national government touches us but once. 

But all of us, whether we live in Maine or California, Minnesota or Florida, have a common interest in the government of the United States. 

In spite of the vast scope of its work, its organization is simple and easy to grasp. So we will take up the organization and activities of the national government before we proceed to the states and the subdivisions of the states. 

When and under what conditions was our national Constitution made and adopted? What is its general organization?

What is the relation between the national and state governments? 

What powers does each have as compared with the other?

How are laws made in the national government, and what are the important facts about the organization of Congress? 

What officials in the national government are responsible for enforcing and administering the laws? 

What is the work and authority of the federal courts?

A. THE CONSTITUTION AND THE UNION 

110. The Background and Making of Our Constitution. 

The background of American government is English. The form of our national government was suggested either directly from the English government or from the experience of states which had made use of English customs in their own governments. Some of the phrases in the great Bill of Rights which King William III signed in 1689 appear in our own Constitution. But there came the break with the English King and Parliament; and in 1776 the 229

famous Declaration of Independence was adopted, which announced that "these united colonies are and of right ought to be free and independent states." Notice the plural number. They were free and independent states, not a free and independent nation. (What is the difference?) 

Official Photograph, U. S. Army Air Corps WHERE OUR NATIONAL LAWS ARE MADE 

This striking picture shows the western front of our Capitol and, in the background, the Library of Congress. The Senate and the House meet in the two wings of the Capitol. A squadron of airplanes is flying in formation above it. 

The wise members of Congress, however, knew the truth of Benjamin Franklin's words, "We must all hang together, or assuredly we shall all hang separately." They therefore appointed a committee which drew up a plan for a central government. This plan, the Articles of Confederation, went into effect in 1781. 

Now the United States had a constitution and some kind of national government.

The Articles of Confederation were better than nothing; that is about all we can truthfully say of them. They gave the central government practically no power to force the states or the people to do anything. The ablest Americans of that day- George

Washington, Alexander Hamilton, and Benjamin Franklin - were anxious to have a stronger and better government. Finally, under the urging of these men and others, twelve states sent delegates to a convention at Philadelphia in 1787 with instructions to remodel the Articles of Confederation so as to make them more suited to the needs of the country. 

When the convention met, it found that it had to draw up an entirely new constitution. A new and much stronger government was planned. It was provided that when nine states should accept this constitution, the new government should go into effect as far as those states were concerned. There was strong opposition in some states, but all did come under "the new roof,” Rhode Island last of all, in May, 1790. In April, 1789, the first 

Congress under the new Constitution assembled in New York; on the 30th day of that month George Washington took the oath of office as the new President. 

In view of the high regard that we have today for our Constitution, how could there have been any serious opposition to it in 1788? Probably nobody in the country was entirely satisfied with everything in the new Constitution. Perhaps that is so today. 

Be sure you are familiar with our national Constitution, which you will find in the appendix of your book and in many other places. You ought to study its organization carefully, so that if you want to find any particular statement in it, you will know in just what part of it to look. It is not worth while to learn the 

Constitution word for word, but every good citizen should be able to tell in his own words its important provisions. 

111. Changes in Our National Constitution. He would be a foolish constitution-maker who did not provide some way to make desirable changes in the constitution. But wisely the men of 

1787 set up a fairly difficult process of amendment, so that no change is likely to be made without careful thought and without the approval of a large majority of the people. 

There are two ways of proposing amendments: (1) Congress may propose them by a two-thirds vote of both houses. (2) A special convention is to be called by Congress to consider amendments if the legislatures of two-thirds of the states request it. 

Only the first of these methods has thus far been used.

After amendments have been proposed, they are submitted to the states to be accepted or rejected. No amendment becomes a part of the Constitution unless it is ratified, or approved, by three-fourths of the states. When Congress submits amendments to the states, it may provide that the state legislatures shall act on the amendments or that a special convention be called in each state for that purpose. In 1933 Congress, in proposing a new amendment to repeal the Eighteenth, for the first time required the states to hold conventions to vote on the change. 

What advantages does either method have over the other? Would it be wise to have the people of each state vote directly on amendments to the national Constitution? 

The chief facts about the twenty amendments which have been thus far made (May, 1933) may be seen from the following table:

Are there any amendments you would like to see adopted?

112. The Relation of the Union and the States. 

The Consti tution suggests plainly that our government is a federation and not a confederation. It declares that the national Constitution and all laws and treaties made in accordance with it "shall be the supreme law of the land, and the judges in every state shall be bound thereby." If the two authorities conflict, the state must recognize the higher authority of the nation. All public officers of the states as well as of the nation must take an oath to support the Constitution of the United States.

The national government also undertakes certain obligations toward the states. It must guarantee them a republican form of government. If in any state some person or group should try to establish a dictatorship or monarchy, or any other objectionable style of government, the national government would be bound to suppress it.

Further, the United States must protect every state from invasion by an enemy, though the state is expected to take its own part also. If a state cannot by its own efforts maintain order within its limits, its legislature, or its governor if the legislature is not in session, may appeal to federal authorities for help. 

113. The Distribution of Powers under Our National Con stitution. — The powers exercised by the state and national governments may be included under three general heads: 

1. Powers of the National Government. We often refer to these as delegated powers because they were definitely given by the states to the national government through the Constitution. We interpret these powers more broadly than some intended when the Constitution went into effect. These powers may be classified under six heads (Art. I, Sect. 8; Art. IV, Sect. 3; Art. V): 

(1) Financial a. To lay and collect all kinds of taxes except taxes on exports. (All taxes must be uniform throughout the United States.) b. To borrow money on the credit of the United States. (This is usually done through selling bonds just as any corporation might sell them.) c. To coin money and regulate its value. d. To provide for the punishment of counterfeiters.

(2) Military a. To provide and maintain an army and navy. (That this may be done as the people desire, Congress may not appropriate money for the purpose for a period longer than two years; in fact, appropriations are made year by year.) b. To provide for calling out the militia and to make rules for its organization. c. To declare war. d. To make rules in regard to captures in war. e. To grant letters of marque and reprisal (page 236). 

a. To make all necessary laws for the government of the territory belonging to the United States. b. To admit states into the Union. c. To govern the District of Columbia, the seat of the national government. d. To exercise authority over any place purchased by the national government and used for national purposes. (4) Commercial a. To regulate commerce between the states, with foreign countries, and with Indian tribes. b. To establish post offices and post roads. c. To enact patent and copyright laws. d. To fix the standard of weights and measures. e. To make laws governing bankruptcy. (5) Political a. To pass naturalization laws. b. To determine the punishment for treason. c. To regulate the punishment for piracy and other offenses committed on the sea and against international law. d. To organize courts below the Supreme Court. e. To propose amendments to the Constitution. (In this case a two-thirds vote is needed.) (6) General

To make all laws which shall be necessary and proper for carrying into execution any powers vested by the Constitution in any national officer. (This is sometimes called the "elastic clause.")

Some of the powers mentioned above, such as the fixing of the standard of weights and measures, Congress has never exercised very fully. It does not actually do many of these things itself, but it authorizes other people to do them. At first there was much argument as to the extent of the powers of Congress; but many measures are enacted today to which nobody objects, but

for which it would be hard to find definite authority in the Constitution.

Go over the laws passed by Congress at a recent session; try to discover what particular power mentioned in the Constitution justifies the passage of each law. Do not excuse too many of them by the elastic clause. 

An almost endless list of 

2. Powers Reserved to the States. powers comes within the group which the Tenth Amendment declares are "reserved to the states respectively or to the people.' 

Among these are the care of public health, the promotion of education, the regulation of local government, the care of the poor and insane, and the granting of the right to vote.

If the federal government does anything with reference to these matters, it is merely incidental to the exercise of some "delegated" power. Only the states have direct authority over such matters.

THE DISTRIBUTION OF POWERS 

The outside circle is intended to include all possible powers of government. The outer ring includes those which have been exercised by governments but are entirely forbidden in this country. The powers actually exercised are included in the heavy line circle and divided into those exercised 

3. Concurrent Powers. 

Concurrent powers are those which may be exercised by both national and state governments. Every government must have the power to obtain money. Both our state and national governments do this by borrowing or levying taxes.

Other matters, such as bankruptcy laws, acts regulating weights and measures, trying certain cases at law, and the enforcement of prohibition, are left within the reach of both federal and state governments. by the national government and those exercised by the state governments. The inside overlapping circle includes powers which may be exercised by both state and national governments. Substitute for the small figures and letters powers that belong in the appropriate sections. 

We should perhaps add a fourth group-"forbidden powers." 

These include powers which the states are forbidden to exercise and

a few which are denied the national government as well as the state governments. The list of powers denied the states is found in 

Article I, Section 10, of the Constitution. 

A state must not 

1. Enter into any treaty, alliance, or confederation. 

2. Grant letters of marque and reprisal.¹ 

3. Coin money. 

4. "Emit bills of credit" 

- issue paper money. 

5. Make anything but gold or silver legal tender in payment of debts.2 3 

6. Pass any bill of attainder ³ or ex post facto law.* 

7. Pass any law "impairing the obligation of contracts." 5 

8. Grant any title of nobility.

A state may not without the consent of Congress 

1. Lay any tax on imported or exported goods. 

2. Keep troops or ships of war in time of peace. 

3. Enter into any agreement with any other state or foreign power. 

4. Engage in war unless in actual danger.

Neither the states nor the national government may pass ex post facto laws or bills of attainder, or grant titles of nobility. 

The first nine amendments to our national Constitution are duplicated in the state constitutions, so that the rights of the people may be insured under both the national and state governments. 

The thirteenth and fifteenth amendments forbid both the national and state governments to permit slavery or to deny any one the right to vote because of "race, color, or previous condition of servitude." 

1 Letters of marque and reprisal are warrants issued by a government authorizing private citizens to equip ships and attack enemy vessels in war times. Civilized governments do not permit this sort of thing now. 

2 Legal tender is money which must be accepted when offered in payment of a debt. 

3 A bill of attainder is an act of a law-making body condemning a person to punishment without giving him a trial in court, and perhaps depriving his family also of some of their rights or privileges. 

4 An ex post facto law is one under which a person might be punished for doing an act which was not contrary to law at the time it was done. Of course, a law may be passed declaring an act a crime which had not been considered so before, but it can apply only to occasions after the law was passed. 

5 This means that no state may pass any law that would prevent or interfere with the carrying out of lawful agreements.

On some matters there is an honest difference of opinion as to which shall do the work Ination or state- 

or which has the right to do it. Often they coöperate directly, as in handling contagious diseases. Each sometimes makes use of the other's services. Our national government must realize that the union is made up of states, and the states must remember that there is a federal constitution and government binding them all into one. 

Would it be wiser to centralize more power in the federal government? What are the advantages in our present distribution of powers?

B. LAW-MAKING IN THE NATIONAL GOVERNMENT 

114. Some General Facts about Congress. Almost every outstanding government has a law-making body of two houses or two" chambers" - bicameral, to use a somewhat technical term. 

Why do we do so? The example was set by the houses of Parliament in the English government. The majority of the original states also had two houses in their legislatures. Moreover, there was a dispute among the delegates at the Constitutional Convention between the large states and the small states. To have two houses in Congress made it possible that in one house the states could be represented according to population, which would please the large states, and in the other the states could be represented equally, which would satisfy the small states. 

The two houses furnish an additional “ check and balance in the government by acting as a check on each other. Probably fewer laws are passed than if there were only one body, but it would be hard to prove whether we have fewer good laws or fewer bad laws. Having two houses enables one house to "pass the buck" to the other when a perplexing proposition is on hand. 

Regular elections for members of Congress are held in November of each even year, and their terms begin on the following January 3.1 The life of a single Congress is reckoned from one odd year until the next, since the Representatives are elected for two 1 If a representative dies or resigns before the end of his term, the governor of the state may call a special election to fill the vacancy. In the case of a vacancy in the 

Senate, the governor may appoint some one to fill the office until the time of the next regular election.

years only. The number of a Congress is reckoned by counting the years from 1789 until the year when the term of its representatives expires, and dividing by two. The Congress whose term began in 1933 was, therefore, the 73d: (1935-1789)÷ 2. 

Each Congress has two regular sessions during the two years of its life. The President may also call special sessions of the two houses together or of either one alone. The Twentieth Amendment to the national Constitution provides for an annual meeting of Congress beginning at noon on January 3 of each year unless Congress sees fit to change the date. Previously, regular sessions began in each year on the first Monday of December. If a regular session had to end March 4 following, it was called the short session; if it could run beyond that date, the long session.¹ 

Under the custom followed for many years, the Congress whose members were elected in November, 1932, and took office March 4, 1933, would not meet until December, 1933, unless the President should call them in special session. Why do you suppose such a practice ever became common? Find out whether special sessions are numerous. 2 The Constitution declares that members of Congress shall be paid a salary out of the national treasury. At first it was fixed by law at $6 a day, but has since been raised to $10,000 a year. The salary is not enormous, but it is more than some Congressmen could earn at anything else. It is supplemented by various extras, such as a liberal allowance for secretaries, mileage to and from Washington at the beginning and end of a session, free stationery, and the "franking" privilege of sending mail free which deals with public business. 

No member of Congress may hold any other office under the national government at the same time. He may resign from 

Congress and take another office, though not even this is permitted if the office was created or its salary increased during the time for which the person was elected to Congress. If this restriction did not exist, Congressmen might cause salaries to be raised or new 1 In the short sessions there were usually some members who had been defeated for reëlection in November, but whose term did not end until March 4. 

These were sometimes called "lame ducks." Since the Twentieth Amendment changed this situation, some refer to it as the "lame duck" amendment. 

2 As a measure of economy this was reduced in 1932.

offices established with the expectation of getting the benefit of the change themselves.

In order to prevent the bringing of private lawsuits or other legal obstacles to hinder a Congressman's attendance to his duties, the Constitution provides that a member shall be free from arrest while present at the sessions of Congress or while going to and from them, except for the commission of a crime. He has full freedom of speech while on the floor of Congress. 

Each house is the judge of the qualifications and conduct of its own members. If a person is a known law-breaker, or if his election was obtained dishonestly, he may be refused admission. 

Each house may reprimand or expel a member for improper conduct; a two-thirds vote is required to expel him, so as to make it unlikely that he will be expelled merely for party advantage. If two persons claim to have been elected to the same seat, the house which is concerned decides whom to admit. 

An account of the proceedings and speeches in Congress is published daily under the title of the Congressional Record. It looks like dry reading, but one who understands how things are done in Congress may find it surprisingly interesting. Sometimes Congressmen put into the Record speeches which they or some other person made somewhere else, with the idea that then they can get copies of these speeches printed at cost and sent out under their "frank" to impress the voters back home.

If a Congressman while speaking in the House accused the President's wife of being a thief, what, if anything, could be done about it? A Congressman in Washington once shot a Negro on a street 

Could anything have been done to him? car. 

115. The House of Representatives. The House of Repre sentatives is supposed to be somewhat closer to the people than the Senate. Its members have always been chosen directly by the voters, for two years, and they are all elected at the same time. 

They must be at least twenty-five years old, citizens of the United States for seven years, and must live in the state from which they are elected. They generally live in the districts they represent, but this is not necessary. 

The number of representatives from a state depends upon its population, except that each state must have one. New York

has the largest number 45. The total membership now is 435. 

After each census (1910, 1920, etc.) Congress is supposed to pass

Congress has given the President the right to make this apportionment if it fails to do it itself. The state legislature is supposed to divide the state into as many districts as it has representatives.¹

The fairest way to district a state is to make the districts nearly equal in population and composed of compact territory. But legislatures sometimes try to give a certain party control of more districts than it deserves by making them of queer shapes and uneven population. Gerrymander is the word applied to this practice. 

How many representatives does your state send to Congress? 

Name those who represent districts near you.

In England a district may elect to the House of Commons anybody from anywhere. Is 

¹ If a new apportionment changes the number of representatives from a state, and the legislature neglects to make new districts, certain members may be chosen by the voters of the whole state. A member so elected is a Congressman-at-large.

there any advantage in the English system over ours, or vice versa? Do you think women should have seats in Congress? Do you know of any who have been members?

In addition to the general duty of law-making, which both houses share almost equally, each house is given by the Constitution certain special powers. (1) In impeachment proceedings against a federal officer, the House of Representatives brings the formal charges. (2) If no candidate for President obtains a majority of the electoral votes (§ 119), the House chooses the 

President. (3) Only the House may introduce bills for raising revenue to carry on the government, on the theory that the people control the House more directly and that the people should determine for themselves how they should be taxed. 

In the House the regular presiding officer is the Speaker. He is elected from the House itself and is usually the leader of the majority party. The position is one of much importance, though its powers have been reduced in recent years. No member may speak unless recognized by the Speaker, who also makes rulings on points of order, on referring bills to committees, and the like. 

Is it better that the Speaker should be a mere presiding officer or that he should have extensive powers? Who is the present Speaker? 

Can you find out anything about him or his career? 

116. The Senate. In one sense it would be incorrect to speak of one house as being more powerful than the other, since each may and often does defeat bills that have been passed by the other. 

But the Senate always has a larger proportion of experienced politicians in its membership; it has a more permanent and complete organization; and its members, being fewer in number, have a better chance of becoming known individually to the whole country. Thus the Senate has acquired somewhat greater prestige than the House.

The voters in each state elect two Senators for terms of six years. 

Senators are grouped into three classes, and the terms of each class expire two years apart. So only one Senator from a state is elected at one time, except when a new state comes into the Union or when a vacancy occurs through death or resignation. At least two-thirds of the Senators will always have had two or more years' experience in office.

Each Senator must be at least thirty years old, a citizen of the United States for nine years, and a resident of the state he represents.

The special powers of the Senate are these: (1) The Senate tries federal officers who have been impeached. A two-thirds vote

117. How Laws Are Made in Congress. 

Congress, standing, or permanent, committees are organized in each house to deal with certain kinds of business, as Interstate and

Foreign Commerce, or Post Offices and Post Roads. After a bill is introduced, it is referred to the appropriate committee. The majority party controls all important committees. Usually that one of its members who has served longest on a committee is made chairman. In the House, to be chairman of the Ways and Means 

Committee, which considers all tax laws, is considered an honor second only to that of being Speaker. 

Per- Committees sometimes arrange for "hearings on certain bills, when interested persons may argue for or against a measure. sons who visit members of a law-making body and try to influence their votes on matters are usually called "lobbyists." A person may "lobby" for a good bill, but "lobbyists" are often considered a nuisance.

Find out the names of the chief standing committees of the two houses of Congress. Do any of the members from your state hold prominent positions on them? How many bills were introduced into the last Congress? Do you see why they cannot all be discussed by the whole membership In some state legislatures bills may not be offered during the last few days of a session. What do you think of such a rule?

The following is the ordinary process, omitting numerous details, which a bill goes through in order to become a law: 

It may be introduced by any member of either house, unless it is a revenue bill, which must come from the House of Representatives.¹ 

The presiding officer refers it to the proper committee. At this point the great majority of bills, good or bad, quietly meet a peaceful death.

The committee does nothing with them, and they are never heard of again.

If the committee thinks well of a measure, it will report the bill favorably to the house, perhaps offering some amendments. After the report, the bill is put upon the calendar to await its turn. 

When that time comes, it may be debated and perhaps amended. 

The final vote on a bill is almost always taken by a roll call. If a majority of the members voting are in favor of it, it will be passed, as far as that house is concerned.2 

1 Introducing a bill is a very simple matter. It usually consists of handing the bill to the Clerk or putting it in a basket on his desk. 

the number 2 A majority of the members must be present to make up a quorum of persons necessary to carry on business legally. If a quorum is not on hand, those who are present may have the absent members brought in. In the House, the presiding officer counts every member as present if he is in the hall, even though he does not vote.

CHANGES IN THE STATUS OF BILLS 

This list shows one day's changes. Some bills are just introduced. Others are reported back after being considered by committees. Others are passed by one house or the other. Try to find out what the various abbreviations mean. 

All this takes place in the house where the bill was introduced. 

Then it must go to the other house and run a similar gantlet there. 

If it is amended, it goes back to the first house for its agreement. 

If the first house does not agree to the changes, a special conference committee made up of members of both houses will try to adjust the differences between them.

Sometimes in the Senate, whose rules permit unlimited debate most of the time, members hold up proceedings by talking endlessly about nothing in particular, in order to tire out the friends of some measure

and induce them to drop it or change it. This practice is called filibustering.

When a bill has been agreed to by both houses in exactly the same form, it is sent to the President. He may treat it in three ways: (1) He may indicate his approval by signing it. (2) He may keep it ten days without taking any action on it, when it will become a law anyway, except as mentioned below. Such treatment would indicate that he did not like some features of it, but did not care to prevent its passage. (3) He may "veto" the bill, sending it back without his signature to the house where it was introduced, with a message telling why he disapproves it. Then the two houses by a two-thirds vote may pass it over his veto. This rarely happens. 

C. LAW ENFORCEMENT IN THE NATIONAL GOVERNMENT 

118. The President and His Importance. Few officials in all the world have quite the prominence at home or attract quite so much attention in other countries as the President of the United States. His thoughts and acts influence in great measure the thoroughness of the enforcement of laws, the spirit and motives of our policies as a nation, and the respect which is felt for this country by people of other lands. 

One would suppose that each party would try to pick out its ablest and wisest members as candidates for this great office. 

Frequently, however, a leader who has definite opinions about public questions may have made enemies. For this reason, several times parties have deliberately passed by their best-known members. Sometimes a candidate is chosen from a big state rather than a small one, for the electoral vote of the large states counts a great deal. The public at large does not. always know all the reasons that bring about the nomination of candidates for the presidency or any other office.

Our Constitution says that the President must be 35 years old, a native-born American citizen, and a resident of this country for 

14 years. Presidents are generally considerably older than 35. 

Theodore Roosevelt, our youngest President, was not quite 43 years old when he came to the office through McKinley's death. 

The President is elected for a term of four years, beginning, after 1 If a session of Congress comes to an end while bills are still in the President's possession, any which he fails to sign are considered dead. Putting an end to a bill in this way is known as a pocket veto.

1 1933, on January 20 after his election. His inauguration, or formal introduction to office, is witnessed by many thousands of people.

The Constitution does not say how many terms a President may have; but since Washington and Jefferson refused to take more than two, no other President has thus far had more. Perhaps

As a salary the President is paid $75,000 a year, which is small in comparison with what the heads of most governments receive.2

The suggestion has been made that the term be extended to six years and no reëlection allowed. Does this impress you as wise? 

Would a business firm choose a general manager on such a principle? 

119. How the President Is Chosen. No part of the Constitu tion has worked so little in the way its makers expected as the method provided for electing the President. They planned that a body of electors, chosen for their intelligence and good judgment, should make selections as they thought best for President and Vice President. Today, while the forms laid down in the Constitution are strictly observed, the electors vote only for persons who have been nominated beforehand by some political party. The process of selecting a President now includes the following steps: 

1. Nomination of candidates. - In the month of June or July in leap years each great political party holds a national convention in some large city. Delegates to this convention are chosen in each state by the members of the party in accordance with the laws of that state. Several candidates may be proposed at the convention by different delegates, and many ballots are sometimes needed before a nomination is made. The Republicans require only a majority of delegates to make the nomination; the Democrats require a two-thirds vote. A candidate for Vice President is also selected. A platform, or official statement of party principles, is adopted. 

2. Choice of electors. On the Tuesday after the first Monday in

November each state chooses presidential electors equal in number to the Senators and Representatives from that state; the total number now is 531. How many does your state choose? In every state, each party nominates a list of candidates for presidential electors. A voter who favors the election of the Republican candidates for President and Vice President will, therefore, vote for the list of electors nominated by that party. 

3. Voting by the electors. - The electors in each state meet at their state capital on the first Wednesday of the following January 1 and cast their votes for the candidates of the party which they repre- 1 Congress fixes the date for this step and the next one. Watch for changes.

sent. They make out six reports of their balloting and send one to the presiding officer of the Senate at Washington, two to the Secretary of State of the United States, and two to the Secretary of State in their state. The other is left with the United States district judge in whose district they meet. 

4. Counting the electoral votes. Later, both houses of Congress meet in the hall of the House of Representatives; and in their pres- 

A PRESIDENTIAL NOMINATING CONVENTION 

In 1932 both the Republican and the Democratic conventions were held in the great 

Auditorium in Chicago. ence the presiding officer of the Senate opens the returns, and they are formally counted. If it appears from this useless ceremony that some person has received a majority of the whole number of electoral votes for President or Vice President, he is declared elected. 

5. Special elections in Congress. If no candidate for President has a majority, the election is referred to the House of Representatives, which chooses one of the three highest candidates. In this case the vote is taken by states, the members from each state casting one vote for their state. This has not happened since 1825. If no one has a majority for Vice President, the Senate makes the choice, each member voting individually. This happened only in 1837. 

How much of this process of choosing a President could be safely done away with? Are there any possible improvements?

If the President dies, resigns, or is removed, the Vice President becomes President. He also acts as President if that officer is temporarily unable to serve, though thus far no Vice President has done so. The office of Vice President ought to be filled with almost as much care as is shown in choosing the President himself; but in practice the candidates for this office are selected to represent a certain state or section, or a disappointed faction of a party, more often than for any other reason. The vice presidency is not an attractive place for a man of active disposition, and on that account men of real ability have sometimes objected to being chosen Vice President. Even attending Cabinet meetings as he does now is not any great additional inducement. 

What Vice Presidents became "accidental" Presidents? Were any of them afterward given a regular election to the presidency? 

Can you think of any way of increasing the dignity of the office of Vice President? Suppose there is some question as to a President's "inability" to serve. How ought the matter to be decided? Have such questions arisen? (1881, 1919.) 

Congress has power to arrange for the filling of vacancies in the presidency when the vice presidency is vacant. The law now in force provides that the heads of the executive departments in the Cabinet shall form the line of succession, as far as those departments were in existence when the law was passed. The Secretary of State heads the list, followed by the Secretary of the Treasury, the Secretary of War, the Attorney-General, the Postmaster- 

General, the Secretary of the Navy, and the Secretary of the 

Interior. 

Why is it unlikely that any one further down the list than the 

Secretary of State will ever act as president? If the successful presidential candidate should die after the November election but before inauguration, who would be inaugurated? 

The President is, 120. The Varied Powers of the President. 

first of all, chief executive; but he has much influence in lawmaking and some powers of a judicial character. 

1. He must "take care that the laws be faithfully executed." 

He actually does very little of this work himself, but his attitude toward law-enforcement has much to do with the way other officers feel about it.

2. He is commander-in-chief of the army and navy and of the state militia when called into federal service. He may use these .. The . ..

Can the President declare war? Can he make war inevitable? Can he prevent war? Is there any way of restraining the President in the exercise of military powers? Could he take actual command of the army and navy in time of war? Would he be likely to want to do so? 

3. He appoints all officers of the United

States whose appointments are not otherwise provided for; in most cases the approval of the Senate is required. 

Any appointments made when the Senate is not in session hold good until the end of the next session of that body. He also signs the commissions of officers and has power to remove them from office. (This power of removal does not apply to federal judges.) 

4. He may make treaties with foreign countries. Here the approval of the Senate is required by a two-thirds vote. Usually the President directs some other official to do the actual work of treaty-making, though he has the right to do it himself. 

5. He decides whether to receive persons sent from other coun-

tries as ambassadors or ministers. Generally any foreign representative is accepted without question, but there might be times when the use of this power would affect our relations with other countries. 

6. The President may sign or veto bills or resolutions passed by Congress. This power gives him an effective voice in law-making. 

The number of bills vetoed by most Presidents is not very great, however. Sometimes the President will notify leaders in Congress in advance that he will veto a certain bill, and they may refrain from passing it. 

7. He prepares a message to Congress at the beginning of each session and at such other times as he thinks desirable. Usually the most important part of the message is the recommendations which the President makes on matters on which he thinks Congress ought to act.

For a long time the Presidents, beginning with Jefferson, who was not a good speaker, sent these messages in writing. But President 

Wilson adopted the method of speaking to Congress directly, believing that in this way a message would receive better attention. This plan was continued by President Harding, but abandoned by President Coolidge. 

8. He may require reports from the heads of the Cabinet departments on matters which concern them. 

9. He may call a special session of Congress when he thinks the needs of the country demand it. If the two houses disagree about the time of adjournment, the President may fix the date. 

10. He may grant reprieves and pardons to persons convicted of crimes against federal laws. A reprieve is a delay in carrying out a sentence. A pardon releases a person from whatever part of a sentence has not been carried out. He may also exercise the power of commutation — making a sentence less severe. This authority does not extend to cases where an official has been impeached; otherwise one of the checks which Congress has upon the other two departments would be made almost worthless. 

Is there any respect in which you think the power of the President should be altered? Mention occasions when the action of the 

President determined the course of our history. When President 

Wilson went to Paris to take part in the Peace Conference in 1919, considerable discussion was aroused; was there anything wrong in his going? Do you think there is any danger that the President will abuse his pardoning power? 

The Constitution 

121. The Cabinet and Its Work in General. 

nowhere states that there shall be a Cabinet. In Article II, Section 2, there are allusions to the heads of departments; but every-

thing else about the Cabinet is the outgrowth of custom, not of the Constitution. The ten executive departments were each established by act of Congress, but in no law is there any statement 

PRESIDENT WASHINGTON AND HIS CABINET 

Left to right: Henry Knox, Secretary of War; Thomas Jefferson, Secretary of 

State; Edmund Randolph, Attorney-General; Alexander Hamilton, Secretary of the Treasury; President Washington. that they shall act together. The President appoints the heads of departments for a term which is supposed to be four years; but he may remove them whenever he wishes. In George Washington's presidency four officials composed his list of advisers the Secretary of State, the Secretary of the Treasury, the Secretary of War, and the Attorney-General. Later additions came as follows: 

1798, Secretary of the Navy 

1829, Postmaster-General, whose office had existed for many years 

1849, Secretary of the Interior (at first called Home Secretary) 

1889, Secretary of Agriculture 1903, Secretary of Commerce (at first called Commerce and Labor) 

1913, Secretary of Labor

President Harding invited his Vice President to attend Cabinet meetings, and the practice was renewed under President Hoover. 

The Cabinet has two general functions: 

1. Each member is the head of an administrative department, and is responsible to the President for its management. 

2. The members, either individually or as a group, act as advisers to the President; but he is under no obligation to follow their 

THE STATE, WAR, AND NAVY BUILDING 

These three administrative departments of the national government have their headquarters in one building. advice. Meetings are held regularly twice a week, and special meetings occur when the President wishes them. Cabinet members do not always agree; a member, however, who repeatedly finds himself out of harmony with the President or his associates usually resigns.

Since these departments have grown up without any general plan, their work occasionally overlaps. Several times there have been proposals for completely reorganizing or even abolishing some of the departments; but it is difficult to work out any plan that will not hurt the feelings of somebody who is in office. A strong President is the one who will have to do it. 

After you have studied the work of the different departments, see whether you could propose any more business-like arrangement than that which now exists.

122. The Duties of the Administrative Departments. - The head of each department may have a number of executive assistants, and in some cases several thousand subordinate employees. 

The departments are organized in bureaus or divisions, each of which has some special function: 

1. Department of State. - If there is any distinction in rank among the departments in the Cabinet, the first mention goes to the Department of State. The Secretary of State is at its head, and 

OC. O. Buckingham Co.

THE ENTRANCE TO THE TREASURY BUILDING 

the Under Secretary is next in rank. This department, under the President's direction, has charge of our foreign relations. 

It frequently undertakes the making of treaties. 

The department also keeps the originals of all laws and treaties and has charge of the great seal of the United States, which must appear on the President's proclamations and on many other public documents. 

2. Department of the 

Treasury. This department oversees the financial affairs of the United States. 

The Secretary of the Treasury has general responsibility for the entire department. The 

Treasurer of the United States has actual charge of the government's money. The Comptroller of the Currency has special supervision of national banks. The Register of the Treasury keeps a record of all paper money, bonds, and the like, issued by the government. The 

Commissioner of Internal Revenue supervises the collection of domestic taxes levied by the national government. The Director of the 

Mint has charge of the coining of money. The Superintendent of the Bureau of Engraving and Printing has charge of the printing of paper money and various sorts of certificates. The Secret Service was originally established to detect counterfeiters, and the Public Health Service and the Coast Guard originally worked in connection with the collection of duties on imported goods. So they are still in this department, but now their main services are not connected with financial affairs.

A SECTION OF THE PANAMA CANAL 

These are the Miraflores Locks. At the right center are some of the little engines that help the boats to get through the locks. 

3. Department of War. This department has charge of the 

United States Army and of activities connected with the defence of the country, such as fortifying coasts and improving rivers and harbors. One of the greatest engineering works of all time, the Panama 

Canal, was constructed under the direction of Colonel George W. 

Goethals and his assistants from the Engineering Corps of the army. 

The General Staff is composed of a group of army officers of different rank who serve as a sort of connecting link between the War Department and the army itself. The head of it is the Chief of 

Staff, who is, while he holds this position, the highest officer of the army. The Military Academy at West Point and special training camps at various places in the country are also supervised by the War Department. The Bureau of Insular Affairs, which oversees the government of our island possessions, is also in this department. 

4. Department of Justice. - This department is headed by the 

Attorney-General, who is the legal adviser for the President and the administrative departments. The Solicitor-General also has important duties in this connection. Some official of the department has charge of every case in the federal courts that involves the authority of the United States or the breaking of federal laws. In this department are included the Bureau of Prisons and the Bureau of Prohibition. 

5. Post Office Department. The Post Office Department is headed by the Postmaster-General. Every postmaster, clerk, or mail carrier is an officer of the United States as truly as the President 

---

himself. Probably no other branch of the national government comes so closely in contact with the ordinary citizen as does the 

Post Office Department. 

6. Department of the Navy. This department has charge of the construction, equipment, and operation of our war vessels; it oversees the Naval War College and the Naval Academy at Annapolis. 

Courtesy Bureau of Construction and Repair U. S. S. TEXAS 

Here is one of our newer cruisers.

7. Department of the Interior. This department supervises the domestic activities of the federal government not handled elsewhere. 

Each of its bureaus or divisions is headed by a Commissioner or Director. The General Land Office has charge of the public lands of the United States. The Bureau of Indian Affairs looks after the 

Indian tribes still living on reservations. The Office of Education collects statistics in regard to educational conditions in the country and publishes bulletins for the information of the people. The 

Geological Survey studies the formation of the land and its natural resources. The Bureau of Reclamation has charge of irrigation dams and reservoirs constructed by the national government. 

8. Department of Agriculture. This department began in a very modest way to work for the benefit of the farmers of the country, but it now has a wide range of activities affecting our health and prosperity. The forecasts sent out by the Weather Bureau are of great value to farmers, aviators, and many others. The Bureau of Plant Industry, the Experiment Stations, and the Bureau of Chemistry and Soils study methods of improving crops and try to discover the kinds of crops best suited to certain neighborhoods. 

The Bureau of Forestry has charge of national forests. We have explained the work of the Food and Drug Administration and the 

Bureau of Animal Industry (§ 39).

THE BUILDINGS OF THE NATIONAL BUREAU OF STANDARDS 

This airplane view shows the main buildings of this important agency of the national government. There are twenty permanent buildings, each designed for a special kind of work. They are situated in the northwestern suburbs of Washington.. 

This department looks after 

9. Department of Commerce.

American commerce and trade. It contains the Bureaus of Foreign and Domestic Commerce, of Lighthouses, of Fisheries, of Navigation, of Standards, and the Coast and Geodetic Survey. The Census 

Bureau is one of the best known of all the branches of this department. Every ten years it makes a complete reckoning of the population of the United States, and at the same time collects other facts of importance. Between censuses it tabulates and publishes the returns which have been brought in and occasionally conducts special investigations in regard to manufacturing, mining, or agriculture. The Bureau of Patents has charge of the granting of patents to inventors. The Bureau of Mines aids mine owners and workers by making investigations in regard to safety and economical management of mines. 

10. Department of Labor. - This department gathers information about conditions of labor and industry and seeks to improve them where it can. It often tries to aid in settling serious labor disputes. 

The Bureau of Immigration receives and examines foreigners who seek to enter the country. The Bureau of Naturalization works. to Americanize foreigners who have been admitted. The Children's 

Bureau investigates anything that concerns the welfare of children. 

The Women's Bureau seeks to do a similar work for women, especially those engaged in industry.

123. Special Commissions and Institutions. 

Several impor- tant commissions are not connected with the Cabinet. They are appointed by the President and make reports to him or to 

Congress : 

1. The Interstate Commerce Commission, of eleven members, first organized by Congress in 1887, supervises railroads, telegraph and telephone companies, and other corporations engaged in interstate commerce. 

2. The Civil Service Commission, of three members, has charge of the examinations which must be taken by applicants for a great many government positions. 

3. The Federal Trade Commission, of five members, investigates the operations of large companies so as to prevent the use of unfair methods. 

4. The Tariff Commission is composed of six persons who must not all belong to the same party. It investigates business conditions in this country and other countries with the view of determining the proper duties on imported goods. 

5. The Federal Radio Commission, of five members, grants licenses to radio broadcasting stations and regulates the conditions under which they may operate. 

6. The Veterans' Administration has charge of all matters connected with soldiers who have taken part in our wars. It looks after the payment of pensions and the care of soldiers in official hospitals. 

7. The Federal Power Commission, of five members, is responsible for the enforcement of laws affecting the use of water power in navigable rivers and on public lands. 

The Federal Reserve Board and the Farm Credit Administration are other important bodies. The former has special duties connected with banking; the latter, with the government's dealings with farmers. 

Several institutions with headquarters in the city of Washington exist more or less independently of other branches of the government and are important on their own account. Four of these are worthy of special mention:

The Library of Congress is one of the largest and finest in the world. It has at present over 4,000,000 publications on its shelves. 

The granting of copyrights is done in connection with this library. 

The Smithsonian Institution and the National Museum were founded for the purpose of carrying on scientific studies and preserving a collection of objects of historical or scientific interest. James Smithson, an Englishman, bequeathed the money for the founding of the insti-

tution. It has assembled a marvelous collection of articles which could not possibly be duplicated.

The Government Printing Office, presided over by the Public Printer, is the largest in the world. It prints the Congressional 

Record and the reports of the various departments. Several of the departments, notably the Department of the Interior and the Department of Agriculture, issue a large number of pamphlets and bulletins containing a great variety of helpful information. These they either give away or sell at cost.

Some high school graduating classes take a trip to Washington instead of spending a large sum on elaborate graduation exercises. 

Do you think this is worth while?

D. OUR FEDERAL COURT SYSTEM 

124. The Importance of Our Federal Courts. In a federal government such as ours certain powers definitely belong to the nation, certain others to the states. Our national courts are limited, therefore, to questions that distinctly concern the Constitution of the United States, the laws of Congress, or treaties that are made by the national government. They have nothing to do with the enforcement of state laws. The seriousness of a crime has no bearing on the matter, for even so dreadful an offense as murder ordinarily comes under the jurisdiction of the state government. 

If, however, it is claimed that some decision in a state court trial is contrary to a law of Congress or the Constitution of the United States, then the case may be carried to the Supreme Court of the nation. The Supreme Court has the right to decide whether a state law conflicts with the higher authority of the nation. 

Disputes between states or between a state and a foreign country or crimes committed on the waters outside any state are not of a proper kind to be tried in a state court. These will, therefore, be tried in a national rather than a state court. Lawsuits between citizens of different states, if the amount involved is $3000 or more, may also be tried in a United States district court instead of a state court. Practically all kinds of cases not mentioned in this section will be tried in the courts of a state or territory or the District of Columbia.

In no other country do the courts enjoy the wide range of powers that they do in the United States. Nowhere else is the judicial department considered on the same plane of authority as the

executive and legislative branches. No other courts have the right to set aside the rules or acts of other departments. If the 

English Parliament passes a law, it is law. The English courts may not set it aside, but will put it into effect whenever cases are brought before them.

The opinion of our Supreme Court in all matters brought before it is final, and every other branch of the government is expected to abide by its decision. No matter how much Congress or the 

President may wish to see a law in force, if the Supreme Court says the law is unconstitutional, no citizen is bound to obey it. 

Is the Supreme Court in 1940 bound to adhere to the same principles in its decisions as the Court in 1800? Andrew Jackson, when 

President, is said to have remarked on one occasion, “John Marshall has made his decision. Now let him enforce it." What do you think of a remark of that kind? If the Supreme Court makes a decision which the majority of people believe is wrong, can anything be done about it? Theodore Roosevelt once proposed that if a court declared a law unconstitutional, the people ought to have the right to vote on accepting or rejecting that interpretation. What do you think of the idea? 

125. How Our Federal Courts Are Organized. -Our Supreme 

Court is mentioned in the Constitution, but Congress was given power to provide for courts of lower grade. It did so in the famous 

Judiciary Act of 1789, which was one of the first laws enacted under our present Constitution.

As the foundation of our federal court system we have the District Courts at least one in each state. In each district there is at least one judge. There is also a United States District Attorney, who prosecutes offenders against national laws; a United States. 

Marshal, who arrests persons accused of breaking those laws and performs such other duties as the courts may demand; and United 

States Commissioners, before whom persons are brought when they are arrested and who decide whether there is enough evidence to warrant a trial. In these courts are tried almost all the original cases brought into the federal courts. Original jurisdiction — the only kind the district court has is the right to try cases not previously heard in any other court.¹ 

Above the district courts we have the Circuit Courts of Appeals. 

The states are grouped into ten circuits, each one of which contains 1 The method of trying cases in the United States district courts is much the same as that described in Section 166.

JUDICIAL CIRCUITS OF THE UNITED STATES 

Congress has the right to determine how the states shall be arranged in circuits. Within recent years the number of circuits was changed from nine to ten. The heavy lines show the boundaries of the circuits. at least three states. From three to six circuit judges are appointed in each circuit. The purpose of these courts is to hear appeals from the district courts, when the defeated party in a case claims that he did not receive just treatment. The jurisdiction of the circuit courts is entirely appellate that is, they consider only those cases that have been tried in the district courts and from which appeals have been made. They do not hold formal trials. 

The Supreme Court is made up of a chief justice and eight associate justices. It meets only at the city of Washington. The opinion of a majority of its nine members is sufficient to settle any case. Lawyers for each side present their pleas directly to the court; it very seldom hears witnesses. Its principal duty is to give a final interpretation of the laws or the Constitution, or decide whether national or state laws are contrary to the Constitution. 

Thus its jurisdiction is appellate, except in two cases. It has original jurisdiction in cases concerning the representatives of foreign countries and cases in which a state is directly concerned. 

A few special courts have been created by Congress to deal with matters of peculiar character. The Court of Claims, made up of five judges meeting at Washington, hears money claims against the United States. (This does not include pensions.) If it decides in favor of a claimant, he may then go before Congress and ask for an appropriation to pay off the claim. The Court of Customs and Patent 

Appeals deals with cases that arise under our tariff and patent laws.

Harris & Ewing

THE UNITED STATES SUPREME COURT 

In the center of the picture is Chief Justice Hughes. At his right are Justices 

Van Devanter and Brandeis; at his left, Justices McReynolds and Sutherland. 

Standing, from left to right, are Justices Roberts, Butler, Stone, and Cardozo. 

The courts of the District of Columbia, by reason of their location, sometimes have to deal with cases that would not come under an ordinary state court. Each territory has its own courts, too. 

In what court would the following cases be tried?

A member of an ambassador's family was accused of selling liquor. 

John Smith made pennies to give away at a Sunday school celebration.

The state of Vermont and the state of New Hampshire disagreed as to the location of the boundary line between them. 

Al Capone, after being convicted, asserted that improper evidence was admitted during his trial. 

A rum runner shot a prohibition officer who was trying to arrest him.

William Brown, who was compelled by the law of Wisconsin to pay an income tax to the state, claimed that this was in conflict with the 14th Amendment to the United States Constitution. 

A passenger on the Leviathan coming to New York stole a pearl necklace from a stateroom.

Henry Johnson of Illinois sued James Williams of Kentucky for $2500 damages for getting him to buy worthless bonds.

126. Federal Judges. - All federal judges are appointed by the 

President with the approval of the Senate. Their term is for "good behavior," which generally means for life. They can be removed only by impeachment.

The supreme justices receive $20,000 a year; the chief justice receives an extra $500. Judges in the Circuit Court and the 

Court of Claims receive $12,500; in the District Courts $10,000. 

A judge may resign or retire at the age of seventy with his salary continued at the same rate, if he has served ten years; but many judges prefer to keep on with all or a part of their work. 

The life term does not apply to district attorneys and marshals or to territorial judges. They are also appointed by the President, but their term is generally four years. 

Is the life term for judges undesirable? Why do you suppose it was adopted? Does their salary seem large?

A lawyer able enough to deserve appointment as a federal judge could make more by practicing law than he would get in salary as a judge. But the dignity attending the position and the assurance that a judge can hold his place or his salary virtually as long as he lives are some compensation for any money sacrifice that a man might make in accepting the appointment.

Our national government goes to England for its background, rests upon the Constitution made at Philadelphia in 1787, is subject to such changes as amendments may provide, has a fairly clear distribution of powers between itself and the states. 

National law-making is done by a Congress of two houses 

a House of Representatives elected by the voters in the states, a Senate composed of two elected members from each state, is accomplished by a process in which committees in Congress, as well as the President, have a vital part. 

Enforcement of national laws centers in the important office of President, chosen by a special process for a four-year term, possessing varied and extensive powers, is assisted by a Cabinet of ten members,

is aided further by several independent commissions and institutions.

The judicial branch of the national government is more powerful than in any corresponding government, is organized in three grades of regular courts with supplementary special courts, is directed by judges whose positions are made exceptionally secure and who usually possess experience and high ability.

CHAPTER XIII STATES, TERRITORIES, AND POSSESSIONS
