- STANTON 

Even before we studied the topics taken up in the last chapter, we probably knew more about our national government than about the government of our states. A good citizen must also be well informed about his own state if he is to perform his citizenship duties successfully. Most of the states passed through a preliminary stage in which they were known as territories. We still have a few territories, as well as other districts which have been given special governments to meet their particular needs. 

How did our states originate? 

How may new states be brought into the Union?

What are the relations of the states to one another? 

What general features are more or less common to all state governments?

What special facts are important with reference to your state size, population, industries, constitutions, officials?

What special types of government outside the states are subject to national control? 

127. The Making of Our States. You will remember from your study of history that the thirteen original states had governments worked out under British rule. Every colony had a governor and some sort of legislature and court. The three departments of government as we have become acquainted with them were already laid out, you see. In the four years from 1776 to 1780 all the states, beginning with New Hampshire and in- 268

States, Territories, and Possessions 269 cluding Vermont, made state constitutions, except Connecticut and Rhode Island, which used their colonial charters as state constitutions for many years. In transforming themselves into states, the colonies often made little change in the general plan of their government.

If your state was one of the original thirteen, find out the kind of government which it had before 1776. If your state was not one of those thirteen, select one in which you may have some especial interest. 

THE NEW CAPITOL AT JACKSON, MISSISSIPPI 

The admission of a new state to the Union today is entirely in the hands of Congress with the exception of one point: if the new state is to be made from land wholly or partly within the limits of states already in the Union, the legislatures of any states concerned must give their consent. Maine and West Virginia are the only states which have been so formed. A new state does not have to have any particular population or area. 

When a new state is to be brought in, Congress usually passes an enabling act giving the voters of the prospective state the right to elect a convention to draw up a constitution. Then if the voters of the region accept it, it is sent to Congress and the President; and on their consent, the state is admitted. Congress has the

right to insist on certain conditions for admission; but after the state is once in the Union, it may change its constitution as often as it wishes so long as it does not go contrary to the national Constitution. The custom is to add a new star to the flag on the fourth of July after the state is admitted. 

When a state has once been admitted, it has no way of getting out, and Congress has no way of putting it out. In the words of former Chief Justice Chase of the Supreme Court, this is "an indestructible Union composed of indestructible states." Congress has admitted some states earlier than they should have been, to gain more electoral votes or members of the Senate for a political party. Nevada, for example, had a population of only 20,000 when it was admitted; even today you could put the people of seven 

Nevadas in the one city of Pittsburgh.

When was your state admitted? Was there any complication or trouble about it? How did it rank in population then, comparing it with the other states? How does it rank today? How many states do we have now? Are there likely to be any more added? 

Why or why not? 

A new state 

128. The Relation of the States to One Another. 

finds itself in all respects, except population and experience, on the same footing as the states already in. Indeed, the Constitution says that "no state without its consent shall be deprived of its equal suffrage in the Senate."

The Constitution tells us that "full faith and credit shall be given in each state to the public acts, records, and judicial proceedings of every other state." This means that an act legally done in one state must be respected as a legal act by the other states, even though their own laws might differ on the point. 

A deed to property, recorded in a lawful manner in one state, must be recognized by the courts of another state. If a New Jersey court says that my property in that state is subject to a tax, I cannot escape the tax, though I live in Pennsylvania and am not taxed on similar property there. 

Would it have been surprising if the original thirteen states had considered themselves superior to later ones?

A citizen of one state is entitled to all the "privileges and immunities" of citizens of another state while he is within the

States, Territories, and Possessions 271 borders of that state 

but no more. A citizen of New York staying in Pennsylvania may claim all the privileges of a citizen in Pennsylvania, but he has no right to violate a Pennsylvania law, even if his act would not be a crime in New York. It is our duty to learn the laws of the place where we are and not offer our ignorance as an excuse for repeated law-breaking. The really important principles and customs are similar in most of the states. 

A person moving from one state to another has no serious trouble adjusting himself to his new surroundings, so far as government is concerned. 

en-

DIXON LINE PENNA STATE HIGHWAY DEPT A STATE BOUNDARY MARKER 

There is absolute freedom of trade between the states. Intrastate commerce commerce tirely within a state may be regulated as that state sees fit. 

But any time a journey or an exchange of goods or messages extends across the boundaries of a state, it becomes interstate commerce and is subject to the control of Congress. No state is allowed to tax goods brought from another state, except for the purpose of inspecting them. The need for anything of this kind is rare. 

This was set up at one point on the famous Mason and Dixon Line between Pennsylvania and Maryland. 

To look at the marker from another angle would show the name of Maryland. Near by stands an old post set up for a similar purpose years ago.

Prove that a very large part of present-day business is subject to national regulation.

The laws of a state are not in force beyond its own limits, but this fact is not intended to aid a criminal to escape punishment by fleeing from the state. Suppose, for example, a person who was accused of murder in Cincinnati went to Chicago after the crime was committed. The authorities in Cincinnati might lay a statement of the facts before the governor of Ohio. He would then send to the governor of Illinois a "requisition" asking that the

accused person be returned to Cincinnati, and in ordinary cases the governor of Illinois would instruct the Chicago police authorities to do this. The accused person may agree to go without making this proceeding necessary, but he has the right to insist upon it. 

Occasionally a governor refuses to honor a requisition. No punishment is provided for such a refusal; and in the rare cases 

THE LOUISIANA STATE CAPITOL 

This is the Louisiana State Capitol, at 

Baton Rouge. It is strikingly different in construction from most buildings used for that purpose. when this happens the accused person is safe in the state where he has taken refuge, at least while that particular governor remains in office. 

If a criminal escapes to a foreign country, the process of getting him back is taken up by our State Department; states may not deal directly with a foreign country. With most of the countries of the world we have special "extradition" treaties covering cases of this kind. 

If you ever traveled in other states than the one in which you now live, did you notice any difference in laws or customs? 

129. State Constitutions. 

One Constitution with comparatively few amendments has served the national government from 1789 to the present day. 

Not so with the states. Massachusetts is the only state that has not had two or more distinct or separate constitutions. 

Some of the state constitutions, especially the later ones, are very long. Those of Oklahoma and Louisiana have over 40,000 or 

50,000 words. How small in contrast with these was New Hampshire's constitution in 1776 of about 600 words! The early constitutions were often made by the state legislature; now a constitution is usually drawn up by a special convention called for this purpose alone.

States, Territories, and Possessions 273 of In the later state constitutions we find a much wider range subjects than in the early ones. They contain the usual provisions - a bill of rights, an explanation of the form and powers of government, and the method of amendment, which is usually fairly easy; but a good many provisions are put in which really ought to be left for laws. 

In broad, general outline all state governments seem somewhat alike. In each of them we find the separation of departments legislative, executive, and judicialas in the national government.

Get acquainted with the constitution of your state, noticing its main divisions. Explain the process by which it may be amended. If your state publishes a legislative handbook or some similar manual of information, try to keep one or more of the most recent issues always on hand.

States. In every state there is a law-making body of two houses.¹ Sometimes it is called the Legislature, LAWS OF NEW YORK.- By Authority CHAPTER 363

every other year, and in Alabama only once in four years. The process of law-making and the management of the business of the two houses are very much like those of Congress. 

It has often been said that the members of the average legislature are usually of a rather poor quality, and sometimes this really seems to be the case. But who is to blame? The legislators are

chosen by the people and come from the people themselves. Generally the legislators will be just what the voters wish them to be. 

131. State Governors and Administrators. In every state the governor is the head of the executive department. He is elected for a term of two, three, or four years. In most states he has the veto power and many other powers corresponding to those of the President. In fact, some states give the governor a right which the President does not enjoy that of vetoing single items in bills appropriating money, without having to veto the whole bill. The governor appoints a large number of executive officers, usually with the approval of the state senate. 

---

States, Territories, and Possessions 275 Three-fourths of the states have a lieutenant-governor, who succeeds to the office of governor if a vacancy occurs there. He generally presides over the state senate. 

There are many other officers who have a share in carrying on the state's business; but they do not, as a rule, form a cabinet as in the federal government. In many of our states very unbusinesslike methods of handling public affairs have prevailed. In several states, including Illinois, Ohio, Pennsylvania, Virginia, Maine, and New York, notable reforms have been undertaken. Their purpose is usually to give the governor more real authority by allowing him to appoint most of the state executive officials and making him directly responsible for the administration of the state's affairs.

Among the most common administrative officials appear the 

The Secretary of State has charge of the official records and papers of the governor and legislature, and has numerous other duties which vary considerably in different states. In Massachusetts, Virginia, and Pennsylvania this officer is known as the Secretary of the Commonwealth.

The Auditor, Auditor-General, or Comptroller, must see that no money is spent from the state treasury unless it has been authorized by law, and sometimes has other duties connected with state finances. 

Every state has a Treasurer, who is responsible for the actual care and expenditure of money paid into the state treasury. 

Almost all the states have an Attorney-General, who is the legal adviser of the state officials. He and his assistants also represent the state in legal cases in which the state is directly concerned. 

Most of the states have also an Adjutant-General, with responsibility in the state's National Guard; a Superintendent of Education, Superintendent of Public Instruction, or Commissioner of Education; 

and a Commissioner of Insurance. Many of them have a Commissioner of Agriculture and a number of other officers whose names suggest their duties.

What advantage, if any, results from giving the governor the power to veto certain items in appropriation bills? Do you believe in having a strong governor? Is anything gained by having a large number of state officers chosen by popular vote? Is the "short ballot" principle observed in electing your state officers? Can you explain why people put up with inefficient management of state affairs?

132. State Courts. There is more difference in the judicial systems of the states than in any other features of their government. The terms of their judges range from two years in Vermont, where they are elected by the legislature, to twenty-one years for supreme court justices in Pennsylvania, where they are chosen by popular vote. Yet in general the powers of the courts are about the same.

Every state has a Supreme Court, in some states known as the Court of Appeals or by some other special title. Every state has also a system of lower courts. In many cases the courts in the counties are really the lowest grade of state courts. Sometimes several counties are combined to form a judicial district or circuit. In several states there is a middle grade of courts between the county or district courts and the highest court. Into the district courts are brought first most of the cases tried in the courts of our land, both criminal and civil. 

133. Our Own State. Since no one of the forty-eight states is governed just like any other, we shall have to make a special study of our own state, using information from encyclopedias, state handbooks, or other sources. We should try to keep up to date with any changes of officials or form of government. It would be well to observe carefully any points of difference between the general facts given about state governments in the last three sections and the main features of our own state government. 134. Special Types of Government under National Control. — 

There have always been regions under our flag which were not allowed to have entire responsibility for their own government and which have been governed directly under the laws of Congress. 

In these cases our thought has been mainly to prepare the people of the region for self-government, although in some instances we have thought it right to consider our own interests as well. (See the Constitution, Art. IV, Sec. 3, Par. 2.) We have sometimes given the people of such regions all and sometimes only a part of the rights of citizens of the United States. We have had different tariff laws applying to some of these regions. 

We have generally used the word territory to apply to a region preparing to become a state. The government of territories has been based quite largely on the famous Northwest Territory Act of 1787, which was passed by Congress while the Articles of Confederation were still in effect. It applied to the region north of the 

Ohio and east of the Mississippi, but was so successfully drawn up that it could readily serve as a model for territorial government

States, Territories, and Possessions 279 long afterward. Only six states of the Union besides the original thirteen became states without having previously been territories - Vermont, Kentucky, Maine, Texas, California, and West Virginia.

From your knowledge of United States history, can you explain why the six states mentioned never had a territorial government? 

If your state did have one, how long was it under such a government? Were there any especially notable events during that time? 

Courtesy Alaska Railroad

RESURRECTION BAY, ALASKA 

The town of Seward, which is visible in the picture, is the terminus of the Alaska Railroad, which goes through the interior of the territory as far as Fairbanks. 

Alaska has much scenery of interest to tourists. 

A territory has a governor, judges, and other officers, who are appointed by the President for a term usually of four years. The territory elects a legislature of two houses, but its laws are subject to the veto of the governor of the territory and the approval of Congress. The voters of the territory elect a delegate who has a seat in the national House of Representatives and may take part in debates, though he has no vote. At present, Hawaii, Alaska, and Puerto Rico have territorial governments. 

Do you think any of our three present territories should be admitted as states? Why or why not?

The city of Washington and the District of Columbia are now one and the same thing. The whole nation feels an interest in the capital of the country, and the capital is in a sense the property of the entire nation. The affairs of the District of Columbia are managed by a body of three commissioners appointed by the President. Congress itself makes the laws for the District. The cost of its government is paid in part by the national treasury and part by taxes on private property in the District. The inhabitants do not vote for President, Congress, or even local officers. Officials who do not live there permanently, such as the President and Congressmen, have the right to go back home at election time and cast their votes there.

Is the government of the District of Columbia an example of "taxation without representation," or 'government without the consent of the governed"? Is such a government justifiable? If you had to move to Washington to live there permanently, do you think you would feel the loss of any of your rights as a citizen?

Other lands owned and governed by the United States are called possessions, dependencies, or colonies. The chief difference between our territories and our possessions is that we have tried to give our possessions a government suited to their particular needs without reference to their ever becoming states. The Virgin Islands in the 

West Indies have a governor appointed by the President and some voice in their own affairs. The Panama Canal Zone is under the direct authority of our War Department. Guam and American 

Samoa, two of our island possessions in the Pacific Ocean, have been governed entirely by officers of the navy, but may be put under civil rule sometime soon. 

Perhaps none of our possessions have brought us so many problems of government as the Philippine Islands. They are the farthest away and are inhabited by people of many races and of different states of development. We have tried to give them gradually more and more responsibility for their own government. 

They have a governor-general and a vice governor, who are appointed by the President and must be Americans, and other similarly appointed executive officials who are Filipinos. They have a legislature composed of a Senate and a House of Representatives. The laws passed by the legislature are subject to the

States, Territories, and Possessions 281 veto of the governor; and if a bill is passed over his veto, the 

President of the United States has a still further veto power. 

Congress may set aside any act of the legislature, although it has never done so. The Islands elect two commissioners who attend our national House of Representatives. 

Courtesy Panama Pacific Line A STREET IN CRISTOBAL IN THE CANAL ZONE 

You see all kinds of conveyances here. Notice the types of building construction common in hot climates.

Ever since we acquired the Philippines, a much debated subject has been whether we should continue to control them or grant their independence. In 1916 Congress passed an act in which the promise was made that they would be given independence when a stable government had been established there. As time went on, producers of sugar and tobacco in this country began to favor the independence of the Philippines so that their products would be treated like those of any foreign country. In 1933 Congress passed over President Hoover's veto a bill authorizing the drawing up of a constitution for an independent Philippine republic. If this is accepted by the people of the Islands, the authority of the United States will be gradually withdrawn and the independence of the Islands formally proclaimed on the tenth Fourth of July thereafter.

Why do you think the leaders of the Filipinos are so anxious to gain independence? Would there be any advantages from the point of view of the United States if they were independent? Is it right to consider our own interests as well as the interests of the Filipinos?

CHAPTER XIV OUR LOCAL GOVERNMENTS 

Have ye seen her, O my brothers, The New City, where each hour 

Is a poet's revelation, or a hero's perfect power,

Or an artist's new creation, or a laborer's new strength, Where a world of aspiration clings God by the feet, at length? 

Have ye seen her, The New City, in her glory? 

- WILKINSON 

Coming down the list of governments, we reach the government of the county and its subdivisions. A city or town governs only those people who live, own property, or visit within its limits, but it touches almost every phase of their lives. Because the government of local communities varies so much from one state to another, it is even harder to make general statements about local government than it is about state government; and it is often difficult to get correct information about our own local government in print. Yet the influence of a dishonest or incompetent local government may reach far beyond the boundaries of the community. A good citizen ought to have a clear understanding of the importance of local government and of the way the affairs of his county and local community are conducted. 

Why are local governments needed, and what forms have developed in this country?

What place does the county have in the government of our state? 

How are the subdivisions of counties formed, and what authority do their officers have? 

How is our own community governed? 

What forms of city government are most common? 

What special problems do American cities face today?

135. The Needs and Forms of Local Government. 

like to have a hand in the way they are governed in connection with those matters vitally concerning their everyday lives their roads, fire and police protection, schools, and the like. 

Besides, no state government, not even that of little Rhode Island, 286

could from a central office at the state capital attend to all the peculiar needs of each local community. Even the enforcement of state laws must largely be done by local officers. The trying of cases at law, the holding of elections, the protection of public 

Courtesy Cleveland Chamber of Commerce

PART OF CLEVELAND'S CIVIC CENTER 

This is the public square in Cleveland. Street car lines from all parts of the city make a turn at this point. How many activities of local government are suggested by this picture? health, the construction and repair of streets, are all matters which have to be done in the local communities, even if we should find it possible to get along without local officers to handle them. 

State constitutions or state laws, however, prescribe the forms that local governments take in their respective states. The state is not a federal government as our Union is. All the powers of government are centralized in the state, not "delegated" to it by the counties which compose it. 

Every state is divided into counties, or, as they are called in Louisiana, parishes. The counties themselves are usually subdivided into towns or townships, boroughs or villages, and cities.

In some states, such as those in the South where the subdivisions of the county have little to do, they are known as precincts or hundreds, and may not be of much use except for holding elections. 

Since we were so careful in making our national Constitution provide for a division of powers between the national government and the states, why do you suppose the same principle was not followed between the states and their subdivisions? Would there be any gain in forming state governments after the federal principle? 

136. Types of Local Government. — The main thing you can be sure of in regard to local government is that if you have to move from one state to another, you may have to learn the details of it all over again. We can distinguish three general types of local government the town system of New England, the county system of the South, and the mixed system of the middle Atlantic colonies.

Your study of history has told you why the early settlers of New England kept rather close together in towns or villages. 

In Connecticut, Rhode Island, and Vermont, the colony or state was formed by a combination of towns, instead of the colony being formed first and towns being carved from it. 

In the South, however, the plantation, not the village, was the center of business and life. The town government was unknown in the South; county governments looked after all local wants. 

As we might expect, the middle Atlantic colonies found their needs both like and unlike those of New England and the South. 

Here was produced a mixed system which had good use for both town and county governments. New York, being near New 

England, had towns much like those of New England. Pennsylvania gave more importance to her counties than to the subdivisions of the counties which were called " townships." 

These three systems, the town system, the county system, and the mixed or county-township system, are all in use today. When 

New England people went West, they took their ideas of government along with them; and so we find in states like Michigan or 

Wisconsin a form of local government with many features adopted from New England or New York. Pennsylvania's county-township system has been used as a pattern by such states to the west as Ohio, Indiana, and Kansas.

The pioneers who crossed the Alleghenies from Virginia and the Carolinas were used to the county system, and established it in the regions where they settled. The states which use the county system have smaller counties and more of them. Sometimes a state like Illinois, which was settled by people from both the northern and southern Atlantic states, allowed a county to choose for itself whether it would be subdivided into townships. 

Which of these types of government is most nearly like that in your state? 

137. Counties and Their 

Officers. In a large majority of the states the county is the most important feature of local government. Counties are often irregular in shape and may differ considerably in the same state in area and population. Usually the state legislature has power to make new counties, but the constitutions of some states require that a new county have a certain area and contain a certain number of people. 

Sometimes the question of making a new county is submitted to popular vote.

A COUNTY COURTHOUSE 

Almost everywhere the holding of courts is based largely on the county. Each county builds a courthouse, and almost every county has a jail. As the county courthouse is the "seat of justice" for the county, the place where it is located has come to be called the county seat.

In many states county commissioners are the most important county officials. They oversee the county business, fix the rate for the county tax, and in many cases are responsible for keeping up the highways. In New York, Michigan, and other states, a county board of supervisors, composed of one member from each township and one from each ward of a city, performs these duties. The sheriff's chief duty is to keep order in the county. The treasurer

handles the county money. The auditors inspect the accounts of other county officers.

The clerk of courts and perhaps other officers keep the records of court proceedings. Still other officers keep records of the deeds, wills, or other legal papers that concern the ownership of property. The district attorney, or state's attorney, prosecutes persons accused of crime. Most counties have a superintendent of schools, and in many states there are directors of the poor in each county. 

The administration of county affairs is often very poor. Sometimes the people elect so many county officers that they cannot know the candidates well enough to pick the right men for all positions. In some states- Maryland and North Carolina, for instance the laws either permit or encourage counties to have a county manager who looks after the county's business just as if he were manager of a large business corporation. Sometimes no new officer is chosen, but one of the existing officers, such as the county clerk or the county engineer, is given additional powers. 

Some states do not require all counties to have the same form of government. In California, for instance, a county gets a charter from the state government just as a city would and is allowed a good deal of freedom in deciding what officers it shall have and how its business shall be carried on. This principle is often referred to as home rule. 

When and why was your county seat selected? How are new counties formed in your state? What was the most recent county? 

Are the counties in your state large or small? Is there any reason for the condition that exists? (In general, consider a county large if its area is over 750 square miles.) 

You will readily see why this book cannot be expected to give you definite information about the particular county in which you live. 

Perhaps your class can prepare a description of the government and other important facts about your county which will be helpful. 

Such an account should include at least the following items: 

1. The chief features of the history of your county. 

2. Its present area and population. 

3. The main facts about agriculture, industries, natural resources, and the like, in the county. 

4. A map of the county showing all the important cities and other subdivisions, with the main highways and railroads.

5. The chief executive officials of your county. These can be tabulated as suggested here.

138. Laying Out Towns and Townships. — In the New England colonies towns came into being in various ways. Today, in some states, a new town may be formed by the state legislature. 

In others the state legislature passes a general law, and when the people of a neighborhood wish to form a new township, they simply carry out the provisions of this law.

Meridian 

Base Line

Prime Ranges

SURVEYING LAND 

Towns

In the states of the Middle West and the Far West, townships were in many cases based on a survey of public land.

A fairly definite plan for making such surveys was worked out by Congress at an early date. Townships were made six miles square, each one containing thirty-six sections.

When settlers moved to this land, these" Congressional " townships often became the basis of local government.

This diagram shows the way surveys Congres- have been made marking out sional townships" in our western states. 

One of these townships is shown as subdivided into its thirty-six sections. The black section is the one reserved under national law for the support of schools. 

In mapping out new land, surveyors have to take some imaginary lines as starting points. You remember from your study of geography that the imaginary lines running north and south are called meridians, and those running east and west and indicating the

distance from the equator are called parallels. The surveyors, in laying out a new state such as Ohio or North Dakota, selected certain meridians as the ones from which they would measure, and 

TOWN LIKE WINHALL PERU A Town LINE BOUNDARY POST 

On the line between towns in the New England states you will often see a marker like this. This one happened to be on a main highway going over the Green Mountains. called them "principal meridians." In the same way certain parallels of latitude were called "base lines." The eastern and western boundaries of townships were laid along the principal meridians or at a regular distance from them. 

The northern and southern boundaries were laid along the base lines or at a fixed distance from them. Since the circumference of the earth grows less as we move north from the equator, the meridian lines come closer together. 

This fact made it necessary to take new base lines at certain intervals and start surveys anew from these lines. 

Even in states that have level surfaces, some irregularity of boundaries could not be avoided, but the shape of townships laid out in this way is much more regular than if a ridge of hills or a river formed a township boundary. That is why New England town boundaries have so many crooks and angles in comparison with the regular lines that mark townships in our middle western states. 

How did the towns or townships in your state originate? Are they irregular or regular, equal or unequal? 

139. Governing New England Towns. -The great distinguish ing feature of the New England system of government is the town meeting. This is regularly held in the spring, though special meetings may be held at other times. In the typical New England town all the voters have the right to attend town meetings, elect town officers, and take part in discussions of town affairs. 

The town meetings act on almost every conceivable thing, from fixing the tax rate or putting up a new school building, to selling a worn-out road scraper or appropriating money for band concerts. 

Nothing exactly like this exists in any other part of the world, and no better training in democracy was ever offered. Large

communities cannot easily permit this freedom, but town government is so simple and inexpensive that people who have it hesitate about giving it up. Brookline, a suburb of Boston, is one of the world's richest communities in proportion to population, but is still a "town," though it has nearly 50,000 people. 

When the population becomes large, a real town meeting is no longer possible. The great mass of voters cannot possibly get into any hall in town and think what would happen if several hundred wanted to discuss the same question! Some of the larger towns get around the difficulty by having the voters choose a number of persons who will then come together in a "representative" town meeting to consider the business of the town. 

In Vermont and Connecticut, each town, regardless of its size, sends a representative to the state legislature. If a man lives long enough in one of the little towns, he is pretty sure of getting a term in the legislature, and one citizen may have to hold several offices at once in order to have them filled by somebody. 

Town officers are numerous, but are chosen for short terms and are not paid high salaries. The most important are the selectmen. 

There are usually three. During the interval between town meetings, they represent the town and act in its name whenever necessary. The assessors or listers put a valuation on the taxable property in the town. The constables are the police officers. The justices of the peace perform various legal ceremonies and hold court in small cases.

If you are living in a New England town, hold a town meeting. 

If you are living under another form of local government, you might be interested to hold a town meeting and see how you are impressed as to the desirability of that form of government. If you live in a "town," be familiar with the names of its important officers and follow year by year any changes that may take place among them. 

140. Towns and Townships Outside New England. Most towns or townships outside the New England states do not hold town meetings. The voters of a township elect its officers but have no direct part in the conducting of township business. In states which have the mixed system, township officers are usually chosen for a longer term than in New England. 

The New York town has a supervisor whose duties are similar

to those of New England selectmen. A Pennsylvania township has supervisors whose chief interest is the care of roads. In some states the township elects one or three trustees. Almost everywhere we find a treasurer, an assessor, auditors, justices of the peace, constables, overseers of the poor, a collector of taxes, and school directors. 

In a town that has no town meeting the people can still find ways to act together in matters of local importance. Community councils, village improvement societies, social clubs, coöperative organizations of various kinds, all may be active in promoting the welfare of the people. If the township or other local government enjoys the privilege of the initiative and referendum (§ 96), it has almost as much opportunity for solving its own problems directly as if it had formal town meetings. 

What form of town or township government do you like best?

Do you think the New England system would work well in other states? If you live under a township government, be familiar with your local government and the officers who are responsible for conducting it. 

141. Boroughs and Villages. It often happens that a community of some size grows up within a township. The people of this community may want better schools, paved streets, improved street lighting, or fire protection, which would be expensive if extended throughout the township. At the same time they may not have enough people to form a city government or care to assume the expense that often goes with it. 

Some of the states have made special provision for this class of community by organizing boroughs or incorporated villages. The method of formation varies with each state, but the approval of the majority of voters or property owners is commonly required. 

In Pennsylvania and Minnesota a borough becomes wholly independent of the township of which it may have formed a part. 

In some other states the borough or village may be organized to meet only special needs, such as street lighting or fire protection, and for other purposes may continue to be simply a part of the township. This is the plan in such states as Connecticut and Vermont.

The borough or village elects a small law-making body known as the council or board of trustees. The Pennsylvania borough has also a chief executive known as the chief burgess. The other officers are

about the same as in the township. New York State has a great many villages with a village mayor, a village board, and other officers. 

The formation of a borough or village is sometimes the first step toward city government. In some boroughs and villages a "manager" is employed to take charge of administering public affairs, like the "manager" in some county governments (§ 137) or in one of the new forms of city government (§ 144). 

If you live in a borough or village, be sure you understand the relation of its government to any other local government in the state. Know who its chief officials are. 

(6 

142. How Cities Are Formed. The word city may be applied to very different kinds of places. In the political sense, any place may be called a city that is governed under a charter which gives it certain definite but extensive powers for the government of its local affairs. The charter is really a constitution for the city. From another viewpoint a city is like a business corporation and is known as a municipal corporation. 

How Cities Are Classified in Two States CLASS PENNSYLVANIA CLASS WISCONSIN 

1st 

Over 1,000,000 1st 

150,000 and over 2nd 500,000-1,000,000 2nd 39,000-150,000 2nd A 3rd 10,000-39,000 3rd 4th 

Under 10,000 135,000-500,000 10,000-135,000

Does this classification give you any hint as to the kinds of communities one finds in each of these states? Would you suppose that cities in these two states need much different forms of government? 

Many states arrange their cities in classes in accordance with population, and then provide one form of government for one class and another for a different class. In some states a place must have 10,000 people before it can become a city. In others the laws permit places that are really no more than good-sized villages to be called cities. The authority which creates and controls the city is that of the state exercised through its legislature. Unless the state constitution limits its power, the legislature may provide for a city any form of government whatever.

In most states a city is a part of county just as a town or township is. St. Louis, Baltimore, and the large cities of Virginia are separate, however. Philadelphia city and county cover the same territory. New York City includes five counties. 

There is honest difference of opinion among friends of good government as to the best way to secure it in cities. Some believe that a large city should be subject to the control of the state, because the interests of all the people of the state may be so bound up in its largest city that it may not be safe to let the city do entirely as it pleases. Others urge "home rule," believing that each city should have the right to decide upon the kind of government it wishes and to work out a charter to suit its needs. In some states - New York, for example - the legislature authorizes several types of city government and permits each city to select the one it prefers. 

What is the law of your state concerning the formation of cities? 

Is home rule permitted? If you live in a city, when did it get its charter? 

143. The Most Common Form of City Government. Many of the early city governments were patterned after those of the states, instead of having a plan suited to their special needs. Perhaps this is one reason why city governments on the whole worked so poorly. Almost every city has had at some time or other one chief executive, whose place is somewhat like that of the governor of a state, and a bicameral law-making body. mayor. 66 The chief executive in the city is most frequently called the We may read about him most often when he welcomes some notable visitor or gives a key of the city" to some actress, but he really has some important duties. It is his general duty to see that city ordinances are enforced. He usually has the right to appoint many city officials, subject to the approval of the council, and to sign or veto all ordinances passed by the law-making branch. His salary varies from almost nothing in the smaller cities to $25,000 in New York. The mayor of New York City gets as large a salary as the governor of New York State. 

The name applied to the law-making branch is usually the council. When it is bicameral, the two bodies may be called the select council and the common council, or the board of aldermen and

the council. The term of office of the mayor and council varies widely. In some places a mayor is elected every year. BILL NO.

CONDITTER REMARKS 

Many administrative officials are needed in a large city, and these are commonly grouped into a few great departments. Every city must have, under some name or other, a department of public works, to look after highways, sewers, water supply, and the like; a department of public safety, controlling policemen, firemen, building inspectors, and similar officers; a department of health; and perhaps a department of charities or welfare and other departments to meet the peculiar needs of the city. It also needs a treasurer, a controller, and a city solicitor or attorney. Every city has a school board or board of education, which in many states is allowed to manage its affairs independently of the other administrative departments.

A DAY'S BUSINESS FOR THE CITY COUNCIL 

When the Council meets in this city, each member finds before him a typed list of matters which are to be considered. Notice the kinds of items they have to talk about or act upon.

In many states the judicial branch of city government is relatively less important than the others, for most crimes and lawsuits are taken up by the county courts or the lowest grade of state courts. But there are always police judges or magistrates who have authority over small cases, especially violations of city ordinances, and who give hearings to persons accused of graver crimes, just as a justice of the peace does in the township. 

Why is the position of police magistrate sometimes filled by unfit men? Find out if your father knew all the men for whom he voted in the last local election. What were the leading issues in that election? 

144. Plans for Improving City Government. The checks and balances of the national government and state governments often

do not work satisfactorily in cities. The power of government may be so divided between the mayor on one side and the council on the other that nobody can be sure who is responsible for anything. In recent years there has been a tendency to give the mayor greater power and to have one small house in the council instead of two, so that the people of the city will be able to keep better watch over their officials. Many reformers believe that સ્વસહાસ

THE SEA WALL AT GALVESTON 

Galveston had to build this great, substantial wall to protect the city from high water which storms might drive up over it. we need in government more of the methods which a great business concern employs. Such plans throw away all the old theories of checks and balances and substitute a commission or manager form of government.

In 1900, after the city of Galveston suffered from a tidal wave, its government was completely reorganized in a fashion now followed by many other places and known as the commission form of government. A small body of persons, more often five than any other number, is elected by the voters to have general charge of the city's business. This commission passes the ordinances needed

for city government. Each member of the commission is the head of an executive department. One of the number is sometimes called the mayor; but his power is little greater, if any, than that of the other commissioners. Under this plan there should be no conflict of authority between the branches of the government, except that which might result from differences of opinion among any group of five men who think for themselves. 

The city manager plan, which centralizes city government still more closely, has made tremendous progress in the last few years. 

It is very much like the system in vogue in many well-governed European cities. Under this plan, the voters elect a small number of commissioners or councilmen who pass ordinances as in the commission form of government, but who elect one person as city manager and tell him to appoint the heads of administrative departments of the city and handle the business so as to get the best results.

The first large city to adopt the city manager plan was Dayton, Ohio. In that place and in others it has worked excellently. 

Even such large cities as Cleveland and Cincinnati have tried it, though Cleveland dropped the plan after a few years. Sometimes friction has developed between the commissioners and the manager whom they elected, but in the great majority of cases the city manager plan has brought economy in government and has given satisfaction to the people. 

145. Our Own City. Whether you live in a city or not, you ought to understand how cities are governed and the problems they face. You should therefore study thoroughly your own city or the city nearest you. Develop this study along the following lines, omitting such items as do not concern you. 

1. What are the most important facts in the history of the city? 

2. What is its present area and population? 

3. What is its importance in the United States or in your section of the country? 

4. Be thoroughly familiar with the map of the city. Understand its division into wards. (Of what use are wards in your city?) 

5. If you have the mayor-council form of government, be able to answer these questions: What are the duties of the mayor?

How is he chosen and for how long? What are the chief facts in the career of the present mayor? How are your councilmen chosen

and for how long? Who are the present councilmen, and in what part of the city does each live? How is the work of the council carried on? 

6. If your city has the commission form of government, explain the method of election and present membership of the commission. 

How extensive are its powers? 

7. If you have the city manager form of government, how is the manager chosen? Who holds the position at present, and what has been his experience in public life? How is the body chosen which selects the manager? 

8. Under what administrative departments is the work of your city carried on? Who are the present heads of those departments? 

What in general are their duties, and what important bureaus or divisions are included in each department? How are these administrative officers chosen and for how long? 

9. What officials in your city have judicial power, and how much authority do they have? How are they chosen and for how long? 

10. What salaries are paid the most important officials in your city government? Do you have any trouble getting capable people to accept positions under your city government? If you have the commission or city manager form of government, ask some intelligent citizens how the government compares with that which prevailed before the present form was adopted. (The answers you get may not be the same.) 

11. If you do not have the commission or city manager form of government, has either plan ever been seriously considered? Do you think it would be an improvement over your present government? 

12. How many of your city officials are you personally acquainted with or do you know by sight? 

146. Special Problems of Local Government. Some of the most serious problems in the field of government are connected with the government of American cities. We have given too little attention to city government, but were absorbed in national problems and the development of business. 

The wonderful growth of cities has often taken place so suddenly and quietly that men have been surprised and shocked when some great problem seemed to burst upon them all at once. The crowding into a small area of hundreds of thousands of people and millions of dollars of wealth would produce many problems, especially since among these people were millions of foreigners.

The Englishman, James Bryce, whose keen observation enabled him to tell Americans things they did not know about their own government, declared, "The government of cities is the one conspic- XXXL uous failure of the United States."

Some cities have gone millions of dollars into debt for public works constructed by contractors who stood in with the politicians who ran the city. Graft has been altogether too common. Even organized gangs of criminals have been able to prey upon the honest people of the city and collect tribute from business men.

Only if the best citizens are willing to sacrifice some of their time and energy to support honest and competent officials can we hope for better things.

In these days of good roads and easy transportation there is no longer the need for so many petty local officers to care for roads, to assess property, and to carry on other public business, as once there may have been. To pay the salaries of all these officials means an extra tax on the people. Reformers often recommend that townships and separate school districts be done away with and their work handled through a county-wide organization. Thus they believe we may save considerable money, have a better chance of getting experts to take charge of public affairs, and probably have much more efficient local government.

CHAPTER XV HOW OUR GOVERNMENTS ARE FINANCED 

Taxes are the sinews of government. CICERO 

The country now understands the issue, a low tax widely distributed or high taxes narrowly distributed. - WALSH 

Millions for defense, but not one cent for tribute! - PINCKNEY

Since everybody in some way gets the benefit of the service that governments render, the matter of paying the cost of this protection and service is a fundamental one. One of the very first acts of Congress under the United States Constitution was a bill for levying revenue it had to be. Ours is a rich country; there is plenty of wealth in it somewhere. But how to get the necessary money to carry on the work of government has from the earliest days of organized society been a serious problem, and a great many people have not been satisfied with the answers given to that problem. 

What are some services rendered by our governments that require expense?

By what means do our governments get money or property for public use?

What characteristics or forms of taxes are commonly considered desirable?

Who decides how public money shall be spent and how such expenses may be handled intelligently?

If governments go into debt, how may their debts be paid off? 

Have any proposals for improvement been made in our methods of taxation? 

147. Why Our Governments Need Money. It would be fine if a government could wave a magic wand and obtain money from nowhere to carry on its work, but our officials have never discovered how to perform such a trick. Anything that is done costs somebody something. A government may occasionally get things done without cost, but most of the things it buys and the labor of the people who work for it have to be paid for. 306

The more we ask the government to do for us, the more it has to spend for us. Some things governments must perform if they are to be done properly, such as protection from foreign enemies and the preservation of individual rights. There is also a long list of things which the people feel the government can do better than private citizens can, such as maintaining highways and operating post offices. This group of activities has become far more extensive in recent years than formerly. 

Take some particular building or piece of public property like your courthouse, state capitol, school building, public park, and list the ways in which it causes expense. 

148. How the Government Gets Money or Property for Public 

Use. 

1. Taxes. The only way we can depend upon governments having all the money they need is to compel people to pay taxes. 

We have learned by experience that people will not voluntarily contribute much to the support of their government. A tax is really the taking of private wealth for public use. This wealth is usually in the form of money. 

2. Special payments and assessments. Some income is derived from fines collected from law-breakers. If a license is granted permitting a person to engage in a certain business, a fee is usually charged. In many cases, if a new street is constructed, the owners of the property which fronts on this street will have to pay a special assessment toward the cost of construction—sometimes one-third, sometimes one-half, and sometimes the entire cost, depending on the law and custom of the state and community. 

3. Eminent domain. - Governments also have the right of eminent domain the power to take private property for public use. Usually the government will pay the owner of the land or building a fair price. The exercise of this right, therefore, does not bring the government any money, but does sometimes acquire land or buildings which the owners would prefer to keep in their own hands. 

4. Sale of public land or other property. There was a time when our government received considerable revenue from the sale of public land. Now our fertile uncultivated land is about all gone.

We still have on some of our public land coal, oil, or other

1 valuable resources; we may let private citizens make use of these resources by paying the government a royalty. Some governments secure a monopoly on the sale of some product like salt, matches, or tobacco, and get some revenue that way. 

Courtesy Caterpillar Tractor Company

LAYING A WATER MAIN 

This picture shows some of the construction work and machinery used in making this kind of improvement after a street has already been paved. This street is in one of the most attractive sections of Baltimore. Is this work likely to be done at the expense of the whole community or of the property owners along this street? 

5. Gift. Occasionally some generous citizen gives cash for a certain purpose, or builds a library, lays out a park, or provides some other facility for the comfort or advantage of the people. 

Such a source of income, however, is not at all dependable, and for most governments does not amount to much. 

6. Loans. Any government which is not hopelessly bankrupt can usually borrow money. Since borrowing carries with it the idea of paying back, this source of revenue is only a temporary one; but it is very often used - much too often, in fact. 

To what extent do you find any evidence of the use of any of these sources of income in your local, state, or national government?

In the construction of your school building and grounds, how many sources of income were used? 

149. Taxes Good and Otherwise. "Nothing is certain but death and taxes." In the minds of most people nothing is less welcome than these same two things. Some people, who would otherwise call themselves good citizens, always try to avoid paying anything to support the government. There are at least three considerations that should be kept in mind in levying taxes. 

1. We usually adopt the policy that ability to pay shall be the basis for taxation. A person with great wealth can pay a considerable tax without missing it. The government must look to those people who have wealth in order to know where to go for financial support. Besides, those who have the most property need the most protection at the hands of the government. 

Why would it be undesirable to levy taxes on the basis of the benefits one receives from the services of the government? 

2. A good tax- there are good taxes is used for the benefit of the people as a whole and not for special classes. Sometimes a community does not realize the benefits it is getting from a school tax. Parents and their children profit most directly, but the community as a whole benefits by having all its citizens educated and can afford to give this education to those who ofttimes could not pay for it. 

3. Convenience as to the time and manner of paying taxes should also receive careful consideration. direct and

All taxes may be classed under two great heads indirect. Direct taxes are those which are collected from the person or persons who are supposed to bear the burden of them a tax on incomes, for example. Indirect taxes are those whose burden the tax-payer may pass on to some one else. A tax on tobacco, for instance, is paid to the government by the manufacturer or dealer; but he adds the amount of the tax to the price he would otherwise charge the purchaser. Indirect taxes are usually more popular than the others, for people pay them without realizing that they are doing it. Real Real estate taxes are those levied on land or buildings. estate bears the biggest share of the burden of almost all local

taxes; its fixed nature makes it the easiest and surest thing to tax. Personal property is that part of our wealth which is movable and can be carried from place to place, such as furniture, clothing, and stocks and bonds. Since it is comparatively easy for a dishonest citizen to conceal some of his personal property, some states do not use it as a source of revenue. 

Taxes on one's income are now an important source of revenue for the national government and for some states. Many believe this is a particularly fair tax, because it reaches all kinds of people, regardless of their source of income. The national income tax is paid on the graduated basis. That is, the rate of tax increases in

What are the rates of tax provided by the present federal income tax law? Are taxes on the house one owns direct or indirect? 

Inheritance taxes are another important source of revenue for some states and also for the federal government, although the amount collected may vary considerably from community made it pos- year to year. It is argued that since the sible for a person to store up wealth, the community has a right to claim some of that fortune when the person cannot use it any longer. 

The federal government has a source of revenue which the states may not use imported goods. For such taxes, we use the words customs, duties, imposts, or tariff.

In contrast with such taxes are the excise taxes, collected on goods produced within a country, as tobacco, playing cards, and the like. 

During times of war or bad financial conditions such taxes are increased, and levies are made upon amusements, automobiles, bank checks, and numerous other articles. Sometimes these are referred to as "nuisance taxes," because they are more or less of an inconvenience to those who have to pay them. Income and inheritance taxes, and excise taxes, are often referred to as internal revenue.

Still another tax collected in some states is the poll, or capitation, tax. This is a tax of a uniform amount collected from every one so much "a head." Since people's wealth differs so much, this kind of tax does not agree with the principle of ability to pay; but taxes of this kind are seldom made high enough to be a very great burden on anybody.

How many of these various kinds of taxes are used in your local government? How many of them are used in your state? 

150. Levying and Collecting Taxes. Levying taxes - determining what shall be taxed and to what extent is one of the duties of the law-making branch of state, national, and local governments. When the rate is expressed as so many dollars or cents on the yard, pound, or other unit of measure, it is called a specific tax. If it is put in percentage form, it is called ad valorem - in proportion to the value.

In fixing the amount of state and local taxes, a common way to express them is so many mills on a dollar. To find what the rate will be, the officials who levy taxes must know how much they want to get from this source and what is the value of the property that is to be taxed. Then it is just a question of arithmetic to find what percentage the amount to be collected is of the value of the taxable property. If the real estate of Smithville is estimated to be worth $400,000 and the local school board desires to spend $6000 to support schools in that community, the "millage" will be $6000 divided by $400,000 and that equals what? 66 

Valuations of real estate are commonly made by officers known as assessors. For some reason it is the practice in certain communities to assess real estate at much below its real value. 

You can see that the rate of tax necessary there to get a certain amount of money will seem much higher than in communities where the property is reckoned at its true value. One who has to pay an income tax is expected to report his income himself. If there is any suspicion that the taxpayer has not told the truth, the authorities do not hesitate to look into the matter; if they find that he is guilty, he may be fined or imprisoned. 

Taxes are levied with the understanding that they are to be paid within a certain time. Frequently a discount is offered if they are paid sooner, and a penalty is added if they are not paid by a certain later date. Taxes not paid when due are referred to as delinquent.

A government may even take property on which taxes have been unpaid and sell it in order to get the amount due the government. 

Find out exactly how property is assessed and taxes are levied and collected in your community. Study this with reference to the various governments to which citizens of your community have to pay taxes - local, state, and federal. 

Taxes may be used for other purposes than just to get revenue. 

Our protective tariff is so arranged as to make certain imported goods cost as much as home goods of the same quality, or even more. 

Its purpose is to encourage the production of those goods in this country and to reduce the importation of goods produced in other countries. Sometimes a tax is purposely made so high as to prevent the use of the thing that is taxed. When the national government wanted to stop state banks from issuing paper money, it put a tax so high on such paper money that state banks found it unprofitable to issue it. The words of Chief Justice Marshall, 'The power to tax involves the power to destroy," bring out this point which we sometimes do not think about. (6 

151. Spending Public Money. - Spending public money must be authorized by the same law-makers who decide how to get the money. Bills for this purpose are commonly referred to as appropriation bills. A president, governor, mayor, or superintendent of schools, however, may recommend how money shall be spent. 

When money is to be paid out, usually a controller, auditor, or some similar officer has to approve the payment before the treasurer has the right to make it. In smaller governments, this checking of expenses is sometimes done once a year by auditors who determine whether money was paid out that was not authorized by law. An officer who is to buy goods for public use may be required by law to advertise for bids and let the lowest bidder have the contract.

If you were hired to mow the lawn on your school ground, what "red tape" might you have to go through in order to get your pay?

If your county jail were ordering canned goods to feed its inmates, what process of buying and paying for the goods might be required? 

In every well-managed government a budget system is in operation. A budget is a carefully planned arrangement for spending money for a given period, perhaps a year. Large business organi-

zations have careful budget systems; and the government is, after all, a business organization operated in the interest of the people." Balancing the budget "-making sure that revenues will virtually meet the expenses of the government is sometimes difficult, but is necessary to any well-ordered government. 

John Lodwick News Service ONE OF ST. PETERSBURG'S ATTRACTIONS 

This Florida city has thousands of visitors in the winter, many of whom come year after year.

In the foreground of this picture is the Municipal Recreation Pier, which contains an amusement hall, convention hall, restaurant, radio station, and other features. Do you consider this kind of public work proper for the community to undertake, or should it be handled as a private enterprise? 

There is in the national government a Bureau of the Budget, headed by the Director of the Budget. He receives from each of the various departments and commissions of the federal government their estimate as to the amounts of money they want to spend for the next year. He presents them to the President with his own recommendation as to what should be allowed. The President then turns the budget over to Congress. There is no way of com-

pelling Congress to accept exactly the recommendation of the Director of the Budget or the President; but Congressmen would have considerable explaining to do if they deliberately ignored it. 

There is a Comptroller-General, who must inspect all bills against the federal government before they can be paid, to make sure that they have been authorized by Congress. He has been known to hold up the traveling expenses of public officials, for example, because he thought they were going beyond their reasonable limits.

In a state the governor or some budget officer whom he may appoint may draw up a budget to be submitted to the legislature. 

There may be also an Auditor-General with the same relative place in looking after expenses as the Comptroller-General in the national government. In our city and county governments there is also a comptroller or auditor to supervise such expenditures. A definite budget officer is not so common in these local governments, although a mayor or city manager might be expected to draw up a budget in the city. The law-making body adopts the budget as a whole or part by part just as any law would be passed. 

Do you have in any of the local governments with which you may be closely connected a really good budget system? If so, what officers are connected with it? If you do not have a regular budget system, by what officers is that work handled? Do you see any difficulties in connection with budget-making? Might there be any opposition to this plan of handling a government's finances? If so, who would object? 

There are 

152. Public Debts and What to Do About Them. 

those who say it is a good thing for a government to be in debt that under such circumstances officials will be more careful about spending money. Others say that the possibility of borrowing money is an encouragement to extravagance. People are inclined to spend money if they can make other people pay it back. A public debt, some would say, is a means of making those who come after us pay our bills.

In general, there are perhaps only two good reasons which justify a government's borrowing money. (1) If public improvements are planned, such as bridges, highways, and the like, they should last for years and will be used by many who are not tax-payers today;

it may be justifiable to borrow for the construction of those improvements and let those who are to use them later help pay for them. (2) Serious needs sometimes arise for which there seemed to be no cause at the time a budget was planned and adopted. 

Then it may be right to borrow. A flood may ravage a considerable part of a whole state, and cause millions of outlay to 

Courtesy Denver Tourist Bureau

DENVER'S NEW MUNICIPAL BUILDING 

This building cost $5,000,000. It ought to serve Denver's needs for years to come, even if the city continues to grow rapidly. For expenses of this kind, it is sometimes entirely proper to borrow money. repair roads and bridges, besides immense loss to private property. 

So with the enormous expenses of a war. It is out of the question to expect a nation to put all the burden on the people who have to carry it on. At least two or three generations have to pay the bill for a war of any size.

Governments most often borrow through the sale of bonds or treasury notes. The notes may be issued for a period of ninety days or some other short time, but bonds will run anywhere from ten years or less to thirty years or more.

A strong and well-established government can usually borrow money at a low rate of interest; yet if a bond pays 4% interest

and runs twenty-five years, it will have cost the government in principal and interest twice the original expense for which the issue was incurred. A government should, therefore, be sure that any advantage in stretching out the payment for some public work over a long period would compensate for the greatly increased total cost of the work if paid for in that way. 

How is money obtained to pay off bonds? One plan is to issue the bonds in series so that a few will become due every year and pay them out of regular taxes. Still another way is to set apart

When the time comes to pay off the bonds, sufficient interest will have piled up to meet the full amount that is due. 

If governments or corporations get into difficulties and cannot pay off their bonds when they become due, the value of their bonds drops tremendously. Occasionally a government will declare a moratorium, or postponement of the time when certain payments are to be made. When such a thing happens, people wonder whether it is really just a postponement, or whether they should say good-by to the money they have invested in that government's bonds.
