153. Special Problems of Finance.- Much of our national debt has been caused by what we refer to as national defense. It has been estimated that 85% of all expenses of our national government 

Courtesy U. S. Children's Bureau THIS IS NOT A RIVER 

It is one of the main roads in a certain part of Texas after a rain. Do you wonder that the demands for highway improvement have been vigorous in recent years? is the result of wars that have occurred in the past or that are feared in the future. Other nations are staggering under even worse burdens.

Our national expenses have also been marked by considerable waste in public improvements. The annual bill for improving rivers and harbors was a standing joke and a scandal. This bill and bills appropriating money for public buildings were often referred to as the "pork barrel," out of which a Congressman expected to get a share of "pork" for his own district. Our budget system has reduced this extravagance. 

State governments have their financial problems too. Building highways has been the principal temptation for the states to spend

money in the last few years. Campaigns for the approval of a $60,000,000 bond issue with such arguments as, "Pull Missouri out of the mud," made an appeal to people who would not have been reached by ordinary proposals for spending money. To meet the cost of constructing and repairing highways, all the states collect taxes on gasoline, ranging from one cent to seven cents a gallon. The support of schools is another state problem, especially in the South. It is generally believed that the whole state should bear much of the burden of uplifting the educational standards of those districts which could not unaided pay for high-grade schools. 

The total expenses of the local governments in the United States are greater than those of the national government. Here, too, are serious problems. In the thinly settled farming community there are many more miles of road in proportion to population than in the large city and roads cost money! The large cities, on the other hand, are particular sufferers from bad government and graft and big salaries paid to politicians for doing nothing. 

To prevent national, state, and local governments from all drawing too much on the same source of revenue is a problem not yet solved satisfactorily. Inheritances are taxed by the national government and by all the states except Florida, though the national government exempts inheritances under $50,000 from federal taxation. A corporation may have to pay a tax where it gets its charter, to any state where it has a factory or branch office or other property, and a federal corporation tax besides. How to get rid of some of these features of "double taxation " is one of the questions which our law-makers should try to work out. 

Why do you suppose Florida amended its constitution so as to forbid inheritance taxes? Study the report of the comptroller or corresponding officer in your local government and find the principal items for which it spends money. Discover what sources of revenue it draws upon chiefly. From the World Almanac or some similar reference book find out how the chief cities of the country rank in amount of debt per capita. 

154. Proposed Tax Reforms. Let us mention a few ideas on taxation which have not yet become popular enough to satisfy everybody. One suggestion is that a sales tax be levied on either retail or wholesale transactions. Such a tax would not have to be

very high, but because of the enormous amount of sales, large and small, it might bring in a considerable amount of revenue. 

Another proposal that has been advocated with great fervor is the single tax. It means that a tax on the value of land would take the place of all other forms of taxation. It is urged that land is the gift of Nature and really belongs to the people as a whole. If private individuals or corporations take for their own benefit this land, they should pay to the community what that land is worth as a source of profit in their private business. Henry George, who was the first to bring this proposal before the people, believed that it is wrong for people to hold land for speculation and not permit it to be used, while the growth of the community is constantly adding to the value of the land. This, he said, is one of the chief reasons for city slums and other evils from which cities suffer. 

No city in the United States has been willing to accept the entire single tax idea. A number of comparatively new towns in western Canada, however, have tried it out in part. Some of our own cities have agreed that land should be taxed at a higher rate than buildings. Opponents of the single tax believe it is unfair to put all the tax burden upon land when the buildings that are erected require much more protection from fire, burglary, or other dangers. Besides, they do not think it is safe to depend entirely upon one source of revenue, even so fundamental a thing as land. 

Has there been in your community any agitation for the adoption of the single tax or the reduction of the tax on buildings as compared with land? (Be sure you understand that the single tax would be levied not upon the area of land but upon the value of land. A square foot of land in the heart of a city under a single tax or any other system would be reckoned as being worth perhaps thousands of times what the same area would be in a farming district.) Why do you think land owners are very seldom in favor of the single tax idea?

Some believe that rich people do not pay nearly their share of taxes. They urge that the income tax rates should be high on yearly incomes of $100,000 or more, for instance. Others, however, argue that if very high taxes were levied on large fortunes, there would be no inducement to carry on big business enterprises, and much harm to the industrial welfare of the country would result. The people who propose new taxes are usually the ones

who will not have to pay them if they are enacted. Perhaps such selfishness is natural; but it shows how complicated the whole business of taxation is, and how hard it is to work out a system with which a considerable number of people would not find fault. 

What would you think of a law which would require every citizen with an income over $10,000 a year to pay the excess over that sum as a tax? Do you think it would be well to require everybody to pay a tax on his income, whatever its size? (This question would assume that taxes on small incomes would be of a lower percentage than on larger ones.) What is your opinion of the single tax? Is there any evidence that land speculation has been harmful to the proper development of your own local community? Should one's income from government bonds be taxed? 

Public revenues are necessary to meet the running expenses of government, are derived from various sources, are levied in various forms, depend upon taxes of many different kinds, assume that a good tax requires honest expenditure of money, assessment on the basis of ability to pay, use for the benefit of all the people, reasonable convenience in payment. 

Public expenditures are made through appropriation bills passed by law-making bodies, should be wisely planned through intelligent budget systems, may permit public debts for exceptionally important purposes, have been at times a source of extravagance and dishonesty, have brought new problems as new demands have been made by the public.

Our sources of taxation in this country have been criticized for overlapping and lack of fair distribution, have led to the proposal of numerous changes, such as the sales tax, the single tax, and increased taxation upon wealth.

CHAPTER XVI OUR RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES 

Ah! When shall all man's good

Be each man's rule, and universal Peace 

Lie like a shaft of light across the land 

And like a line of beams athwart the sea? 

- TENNYSON 

Today no nation that amounts to anything can keep by itself. Our own country has a neighbor on the north whose territory stretches three thousand miles or more side by side with ours; and another on the south with a boundary line more than one thousand miles in length. It is a matter of only a few days to cross the ocean that separates us from Europe; and the time may come when not only 

Lindberghs, but ordinary business men, may cross the Atlantic every day. Every important country is tied up in many ways with every other country.

To what extent should we think of the world as a community? 

How do governments carry on their dealings with one another?

By what means have nations settled disputes with one another? 

What world-wide organizations have been formed to promote peace among nations?

What further efforts are still in progress to reduce the chances of war?

In how many ways is the United States related to the other nations of the world?

What are our policies in dealing with other countries, and how are those policies carried out? is " 155. The World as a Community. — We said that a community a group of people who occupy a definite area and are bound together by common interests and common customs or laws." 

The world is a pretty big thing to be referred to as a community. 

Yet it is a definite area. Are the people of the world bound together by common interests and common customs or laws? 323

There are many things that all classes, ages, and kinds of people need or wish for their existence or comfort. Progressive nations are all interested in trade. They send messages to one another with the speed of lightning. They are interested in education, religion, pleasure, and politics, though not always exactly the same kind. 

The Universal Postal Union brings postal departments into coöperation so well that for five cents you can send an ordinary

ONE PHASE OF THE WORK OF THE COAST GUARD 

A Coast Guard cutter is standing near this iceberg off the coast of Newfoundland. 

It sends out radio messages several times a day notifying ships to be on their guard. 

Do other countries benefit by this service of our Coast Guard? letter almost anywhere in the world. International organizations and conferences to promote health, encourage scientific enterprises, or improve business relations have been planned by private citizens and aided by governments. 

Trade makes nations know about one another. Travel enables people of one nation to meet face to face those of even distant lands. Science and industry are fast coming to be alike in every quarter of the globe. There is enough to justify us in thinking of the world as a great community, made up of nations, or "states," and of people who are much alike everywhere. 

What conditions or qualities do you think of as making a nation great? Mention several respects in which all nations are alike. 

Mention several respects in which nations differ. Make a guess as to the number of nations in the world, and then count them as listed in your geography or some good reference book, and see how nearly right you were.

156. The Relations of Governments with One Another. 

Nations must have some means of dealing with one another, and what can that means be except through their governments? 

Surely we could not permit any group of persons to go from one nation to another and say, "We are the people of our country." 

When we say that "England," or "Germany," or " France," or the "United States" is doing this or that, it is the government that happens to be in power that is following that particular policy. 

Many of the people of the country may not agree with the policy, but for the time being they must accept it as their country's. 

A formal agreement among two or more nations is usually referred to as a treaty. Treaties are made either by regular officers of the government or by special representatives. In the United 

States, the President has power to make treaties, but he usually asks some one else to do the actual work. Sometimes the Secretary of State is intrusted with special responsibility for negotiations of this kind.

Our Senate's part in foreign relations is of two kinds: (1) It must give its consent by a two-thirds vote to any treaties before they can go into effect. (2) It may approve or reject the President's appointments of ambassadors or other foreign agents of the government. Though the Senate cannot do anything alone in this field, it has a very important power of restraint upon the policies of the President or his Secretary of State. A President does well to get the Senate's good will before attempting anything of great importance in foreign affairs. 

The provision that two-thirds of the Senate must ratify treaties seems in some respects unreasonable, for as few as thirty-three Senators out of ninety-six can block a policy which the other sixtythree and the President may feel is right. Most other countries do not tie the hands of their leaders in the same way, though many nations are coming to have the law-making body give its approval to treaties after they are made.

Make a list of eight treaties you consider especially important, and be able to tell what they dealt with. It has been said, "A majority can make war, but it takes two-thirds to make peace." 

Just what does that statement refer to? Would it be better to have treaties approved by a majority of both houses of Congress, rather than by two-thirds of the Senate alone?

Under ordinary circumstances would it be wiser for the President

or the Secretary of State to do the actual work of treaty making? 

Make a list of six famous Secretaries of State, indicating at least one important achievement connected with each. What particular problems has our present Secretary of State had to deal with? 

157. Peace-Time Relations among Nations. 

All governments regularly send persons to represent them in other countries, and have persons in many important cities who will look out for their 11 

Courtesy Foreign Service Buildings Office, Department of State UNITED STATES GOVERNMENT BUILDING, OTTAWA, CANADA 

This building contains the residence and offices of our ambassador to Canada.

interests there. Agents who represent nations in other countries are of two general classes diplomatic representatives and consuls. Diplomatic representatives are those who come into direct contact with the chief officials of other governments. To the leading nations of the world we send men with the title of ambassador, and receive from them representatives with that title. In the case of smaller countries we send and receive persons known as envoys or ministers.

Find the names and, if possible, something about the lives of six of our ambassadors to other countries and of six ambassadors of other nations to the United States.

Consuls make up the other class of foreign representatives of a nation. They are stationed in important cities all over the globe. 

Their chief duties are to assist fellow citizens in their neighborhood, look out for business conditions, and make reports to their home government. 

Courtesy Foreign Service Buildings Office, Department of State THE RESIDENCE OF OUR MINISTER TO ALBANIA 

These are the buildings of the American legation in the capital of one of the smaller countries.

Not so long ago positions in the foreign service of our country were practically all given as political jobs. Now, however, all except the highest offices are filled by appointment after civil service examinations to test the fitness of candidates for such positions. Now it is possible for one to be a career man" in our foreign service and prepare definitely to spend his life in that kind of work.

It costs our ambassadors so much to live in proper style abroad that only rich men have been able to accept some of the highest positions. Do you think that is wise? We have now begun to buy or build embassies in the leading capitals of the world where our ambassador or minister may live when abroad. What do you think of the policy?

Ambassadors and ministers have the privilege of exterritoriality - that is, wherever they go they are at home, so to speak- and of diplomatic immunity — that is, they are not subject to the laws of the country where they stay. If, however, an ambassador should act so as to offend the government to which he is sent, he is considered persona non grata - an unacceptable person. In such a case his country would usually send some one else. If private citizens travel outside the borders of their own country, they may be asked to have a passport from their own government which will prove their citizenship. Some countries, however, do not insist upon passports. 

How would you define "persona grata"? Can you think of any reasons why in time of peace any government should object to receiving a particular person as an ambassador? Who decides whether a certain person is acceptable as ambassador to this country? Do you think the practice of diplomatic immunity is good?

The chauffeur of an ambassador to the United States was once arrested for speeding; would that case have any point under international law? Mention at least two notable cases in our history when the President of the United States demanded the recall of the minister or ambassador from another country. The United States does not exchange ambassadors with Russia (May, 1933). Why?

There is one thing our “world community" does not yet have to the extent we should like. We do have what we call international law a collection of rules and customs which nations commonly observe in their dealings with one another. It can be enforced, however, only by the moral sentiment of the world, for even the World Court cannot compel nations to obey it. It ought to be developed further. 

Some customs are pretty generally accepted. All persons within the borders of a "state" are subject to its laws, except foreign rulers or their representatives. A "state" has authority over all the land and water within its boundaries, and over the waters for three miles off the coast sometimes more, if special treaties are made. Furthermore, each "state" has a right to decide what form of government it shall have and to manage its own domestic affairs in whatever way it thinks best. 

158. Settling International Disputes. If you read the history of nations, you may think that it is just a story of one war after

another and that what was called peace was simply taking breath to fight again. There are people called pacifists, who declare that war is never justifiable. War settles nothing except which party is the stronger or luckier, but sometimes it seems as if nations have 11 

US NAVY THE MACON, NEWEST AND LARGEST OF OUR NAVY'S AIRSHIPS 

to choose between submitting to wrong or fighting to defend the right. Today there is a general feeling that war is a calamity and by every honorable means ought to be prevented. 

Denmark has practically done away with anything like an army or navy. Is Denmark in a position to do this to any better advantage than our own country?

The World War cost the lives of probably twelve million men of different nations. It wrecked the health and happiness of many 

It left some of the nations in debt to an amount equal to half their estimated wealth. Our own country had a war debt in 

1920 about twenty times as large as it had ever had before. more.

If there should be another war, what new terrors might one have to fear in addition to those of the World War? Is it possible for great nations to keep out of a war which they do not start? 

The United States has frequently made use of arbitration in settling disputes with other countries. (Be sure you realize arbitration means the calling in of one or more disinterested parties

to help settle the trouble, and that plenty of disputes have been settled peacefully by other means.) It cost Great Britain $15,500,000 to pay this country what we were awarded by arbitration in the settlement of the famous Alabama Claims; but how many times that sum it would have cost Great Britain to have a war over them, whether she won or lost!

Make a list of disputes with any country which the United States has settled through arbitration or direct negotiation. Were any of these worth having a war about?

Most of the world was both surprised and pleased in 1898, when the Czar of Russia proposed that delegates from all the nations come together at The Hague to consider how they might reduce their expenses for armies and navies. The anniversary of that day, when the first Hague Conference assembled, May 18, 1899, is observed by lovers of peace in many nations, as World Peace Day, or Good Will Day. The conference did not get very far with plans for cutting down armies and navies, but it did provide for the establishment of the Hague Court of Arbitration. 

This is composed of a list of men selected by the governments of different nations. When a dispute arises between two nations, they may make selections from this list of names and ask these persons to serve as a Board of Arbitration, to settle the question in dispute.

Why do you suppose the Czar of Russia was sufficiently interested in international peace to call this conference? What is the attitude of the Russian government today toward international peace? 

What did Andrew Carnegie do for The Hague and World Peace? 

159. World Organizations to Promote Peace. The World 

War did bring about some good for the world, for it led to the first great organization of the nations for the promotion of peace and coöperation. Something like a League of Nations had been suggested by thinking men as far back as William Penn, but it was Woodrow Wilson who made the League of Nations a reality. 

He insisted that the League Covenant (which is practically a constitution) be a part of every peace treaty made after the war. 

He thought that if a world organization should be formed of which all well-disposed nations would become members, it would tend to make war impossible. So the members of the League bind them-

selves to refrain from war. The League is even given power to declare a boycott of any nation which violates its pledges. 

This League of Nations came into being in January, 1920. 

At first, only the Allied nations and those which had been neutral in the World War were allowed to join, but now all the Central powers are members. To Wilson's great disappointment, the 

THE COUNCIL OF THE LEAGUE OF NATIONS 

At the thirteenth annual meeting of the League of Nations in the electoral hall at Geneva, Eamonn de Valera (center), President of the Irish Free State, sat as president of the Council. 

United States Senate could not be induced to give a two-thirds vote in favor of the Peace Treaty, and we are therefore on the outside.

The League has been of great service in many ways. It relieved much of the distress that existed in several countries after the World War. It has tried to establish some restraint on the traffic in opium and other harmful drugs. It has engaged in activities to promote health and to reduce the danger of epidemics. Some say that these activities have been even more effective than those connected with preventing war. 

The League's organization is very simple. There is a Council which now has fourteen members. Great Britain, France, Germany, Italy, and Japan are permanent members of the Council; and our

own country would be if it should join the League. Other members of the Council are elected from time to time by the Assembly of the League. The Council meets at least four times a year and oftener if occasions arise. It is the executive body of the League. 

The Assembly meets at least once a year — regularly in the month of September. It gives the representatives of all the nations of the world who belong to the League a chance to express their opinions about international affairs. Fifty-seven nations are now (May, 1933) members of the League. Mexico, Turkey, and Iraq were the last to be admitted. Of the important powers of the world only the 

United States, Russia, and Brazil are not members. The headquarters of the League are in Geneva. A permanent office known as the Secretariat keeps records of its business. There is also an 

International Labor Office which studies labor conditions all over the world.

Follow closely the meetings of the League Assembly and Council during the current year. If Japan should insist on its withdrawal, with what conditions is it supposed to comply?

The League Covenant called for the establishment of a Permanent Court of International Justice, which was realized in 1922. 

It is composed of fifteen judges elected for nine years by the League Council and League Assembly voting separately. The Court meets at The Hague. Its judges have to give all of their time, if necessary, to the work of the Court. That the League has tried to make this a real world court is proved by the fact that it has from the beginning elected a prominent man from the United States to be one of its judges, though the United States has not yet contributed to its support. A considerable number of nations, including such leading powers as Great Britain and Italy, have agreed to refer to the Court all international disputes in which they are concerned, although each nation is free to choose for itself whether it shall accept such an obligation. 

Is there any reason why this country or any other should hesitate to support the World Court? Do you think the judges of the 

World Court would have any difficulty in being impartial if a case came before them in which their own country was concerned? (Judges have already voted against the claims of their own governments in cases that have come before the Court.) 

1 Do not confuse this World Court, as it is commonly called, with the Hague Court of Arbitration. The old Hague Court was really not a court but simply a list of names from which selections could be made for handling particular cases.

160. Other Efforts to Pro mote Peace. Although the opposition of certain Senators kept the United States out of the League, we have been active in a number of movements to curb the god of war. President Harding and Secretary of State 

Hughes brought together in

Washington in the fall and winter of 1921-1922 the representatives of the nine nations that were most concerned in problems of disarmament and that had interests in lands washed by the Pacific Ocean. Several agreements were entered into with the of purpose saving China from being dismembered. The five great naval powers agreed to declare a ten-year "holiday" on the construction of big battleships.

In 1928 our Secretary of 

State Kellogg and Foreign 

Secretary Briand, of France, worked out the famous 

Briand-Kellogg treaty, or

Pact of Paris, in which the signers agreed to "renounce war as an instrument of national policy," and to settle all their disputes by peaceful means. Practically all the important nations of the General Pact for the

Article 2 The High Contracting Parties agree that the settlement or solution of all disputes or conflicts of whatever nature or of whatever origin they may be, which may arise among them, shall never be sought except by pacific means.

Then in 1932 there was assembled at Geneva at the call of the League of Nations a special conference attended by most of the powers which were not members of the League, as well as those which were. This was the first world conference ever held for the definite purpose of trying to agree on some means of reducing armies and navies. 

Why do many nations still insist upon having armies and navies of considerable size as compared with their own wealth and population? Why is it difficult to bring about the reduction of armaments? 

Be sure you keep in touch with any developments toward disarmament or reduction of armaments. The United States government has insisted in some of the more recent conferences that it could not reduce its own army below its present size. If that is true, does our government have the right to oppose the size of armies maintained by other countries? 

161. The Place of the United States among Nations. The relation of the United States to the other countries of the New World has always been a problem. Under the Monroe Doctrine, our government insisted that European powers should not interfere with the governments of Central and South America. Some republics of the New World have even wondered whether the United States wished European countries to keep out so that it might dominate the New World in its own interest. Instead of a big brother we might be a greedy uncle!

In an effort to promote pleasanter feelings between this country and the other New World republics, some of our Secretaries of State helped to plan international conventions in the capitals of various countries, such as Mexico, Rio de Janeiro, Santiago, and Havana, and even attended some of these themselves. There has also been established an organization known as the Pan-American Union, with headquarters in Washington in a beautiful building given by Andrew Carnegie. Its purpose is to help the various countries of the New World understand one another and be able to act together in international matters. 

Several republics of Central America and the West Indies have often been upset by revolutions and have not paid their debts

properly. Over these little republics and also over Cuba we have assumed that sort of authority that is often called a protectorate. 

By this we mean a relationship in which a strong nation has some responsibility for the welfare of a weaker nation, without actually becoming the owner of that nation's territory. A somewhat 

THE COURT OF THE PAN-AMERICAN BUILDING 

The tropical plants and birds one sees in this patio of the building of the Pan- American Union, at Washington, make it one of the most attractive places in Washington for a tourist to visit. similar relationship was set up by the League of Nations Covenant under the title of mandate, when some big powers were given the responsibility of looking after weak or backward peoples. An arrangement of this kind can do much good for a weaker power, if the stronger one goes at the matter unselfishly. 

What impression was made upon some South American countries by the way we acquired the Panama Canal Zone? 

We used to think we were so favored by nature that we could get along by ourselves, regardless of what anybody else in the world would do. If we were willing to stay entirely by ourselves, perhaps that might be true; yet when we think of the millions of

dollars' worth of rubber, tea, coffee, tin, and other things that we import every year, we see how differently we should have to live and work if we tried to avoid dealings with other countries. 

Now that every little while man is achieving some new stunt in

THE PEACE BRIDGE AT BUFFALO 

The name of this bridge helps to commemorate the long, unbroken peace between this country and its northern neighbor. the air, our boasted protection by sea on the east and on the west may not mean much. Our people themselves have come from all parts of the world. 

When Washington urged this country to avoid " permanent alliances with any portion of the foreign world," he meant simply that we should not line up with one European nation against another on any permanent basis - not that we should have no association with other countries. Neither did the Monroe Doctrine imply anything of that kind. 

Ask five people to tell you what the Monroe Doctrine means; then compare the five statements with the summary of the Doctrine which you find in any good textbook on United States history. 

Make a list of ten effects upon our industries and social life that

would result from our being suddenly cut off from the rest of the world.

When the League of Nations began its work, our government ignored its existence. During the administrations of Presidents 

Coolidge and Hoover the United States sent delegates to take part in a number of conferences called by the League; and representatives of our government served with delegates of other nations on committees created by the League. We have registered treaties with the League's Secretariat, just as League members do. Some believe that this is the proper policy for this country to follow to work with the League when occasions make it desirable, but not to accept membership in it. Others feel that this is a selfish and unwise policy that it is not creditable to us to get the benefit of the League's activities when we do not contribute to its support — and that we ought to join. 

We should be willing to take the lead among nations in cultivating friendliness and understanding. Selfishness does not pay 

We may be the wealthiest and most self-sufficient of all the nations; but just as a rich man may be the loneliest person in town, so a nation which tried to keep apart from the rest of the world would ultimately be the most hated of all. It is to our own interest to show our good will and help in keeping the world at peace.

American tourists travel in other countries to a much greater extent than do the people of any other nation. Do you think their attitude and conduct have any effect on what the other countries think of us? Can a person be both a patriotic American and a sincere believer in the League of Nations? Is it right for citizens of the United States to talk about themselves as Americans? When we use the word America, just what do we, or should we, mean by it? What can we, as individuals, do to promote peace and good will among the nations of the world? 

Our relations with other countries rest upon the thought of the world as a community, are carried on through the making of treaties and the exchange of representatives between our government and others, are conducted by the President and Secretary of State, but subject in some important cases to the approval of the Senate, are dictated in part by the understandings known as international law,

have been in many cases marked by peaceful settlements of international difficulties, have been characterized by coöperation in numerous international matters, but not thus far by membership in the 

League of Nations, are so closely intertwined with the problems of other nations as to force us into a place of great responsibility.

CHAPTER XVII OUR RIGHTS AND DUTIES AS GOOD CITIZENS 

We will never bring disgrace to this city by any act of dishonesty or cowardice, nor ever desert our suffering comrades in the ranks. 

We will fight for the ideals and sacred things of the city, both singly and together. 

We will revere and obey the city's laws, and do our best to incite a like respect and reverence in those above us who are prone to annul or set them at naught. 

We will strive unceasingly to quicken the public sense of civic duty. Thus in all these ways we will transmit this city not only not less, but greater, better, and more beautiful than it was transmitted to us. 

PLEDGE OF THE ATHENIAN YOUTHS 

Now that we are finishing that part of our study which deals particularly with our government and the way it serves us, let us sum up the relations of the citizen toward his government. As we go on further and study about the ways we earn a living and the principles that affect our industrial life, we shall find that they have a great part in the life of a citizen. But since we most often think of citizenship in relation to government, let us make sure that we understand our rights and duties as good citizens in that connection. 

How and where may one learn the rights and duties of a citizen?

What are the groups or classes of rights to which American citizens are entitled?

What means do our governments provide for the protection of our rights ? 

What duties belong to good citizens in exchange for the rights which they enjoy?

What questions and problems may come up in practicing the duties of good citizenship?

What responsibilities do young citizens have in playing their part in the life of their community and nation? 

162. What Are Our Rights and Duties? We have already found that almost all our constitutions contain a bill of rights. 

Our people were so anxious to have these rights set down in black and white that right at the start ten amendments were added to meet what they thought was a deficiency in our national Constitu- 341

tion. Since state constitutions have this same kind of guarantee, citizens of the United States are protected in two ways by their national Constitution against unreasonable acts on the part of the national government, and by their state constitutions against tyranny or unfairness on the part of state officials. 

Read Amendments I-X of the national Constitution, if you have not already done so. Find a similar list in your state constitution. 

Are any rights or privileges mentioned in either list that are not mentioned in the other? If so, what are they? Are they important? Would our rights be any less if they were not set down on paper?

The numerous duties laid down in the laws of our Congress or state legislature must not conflict with these established rights. 

At the same time, we must never overlook our duties. We must try to know the laws so that we shall not break them through ignorance.

The rights listed in our constitutions can all be included under three general types - personal liberty, personal security, and private property. 

163. Guarantees of Liberty. Quite likely Americans talk more about liberty than anything else that concerns the acts of government. Sometimes it seems as if they overdo it and care too much about being free to do things they should not do. Perhaps we need a better definition of "liberty" or "freedom." 

Some one has said that freedom is the opportunity to develop in the fullest and best way one's faculties and talents. Then is one not free unless he does as he pleases under all conditions? Far from it that would be anarchy, when no good citizen would be free from interference on the part of evil and dishonest men. 

True freedom for one person must carry with it freedom for every one else. 

Does a person have complete freedom of thought if he is uneducated? Is it wise for a government to try to limit freedom of thought?

In Amendment I of our national Constitution, the first form of liberty that is guaranteed is liberty of worship. There is no established church in this country; people may belong to any church, or even to no church at all. Further, citizens are assured

Our Rights and Duties as Good Citizens 343 

the right of free speech, of a free press, of peaceable assembly, and of petitioning the government. 

Of course there are limits to the exercise of these "freedoms." 

If you tell somebody that I am a thief, when it is not true, you are abusing your privilege of free speech; and I have the right to call you to account for circulating slander about me. If you put such an untrue statement in a letter and circulate it, or have it printed, the offense is called libel. A group of people have the right to hold public meetings so long as they do not raise a disturbance or cause a riot. On the other hand, a government is not denying this right if it insists that a permit be obtained for the meeting, providing it does not discriminate between one group and another in granting such permits.

In some of our cities and states there are officers or bureaus whose duty it is to censor books, plays, or pictures that is, to pass judgment on their fitness to be presented to the public. 

Sometimes the writers or producers of these things pretend to be very much stirred up if they are denied the right to publish or show their product freely. But surely a community has the right to protect itself from nastiness, in print or otherwise, even though there are people who are willing to pay money to read or see objectionable things. One's liberty in free press or free speech must end when it begins to put temptation or harm in the way of weaker members of the community. 

Liberty has not always meant just the same thing. See Magruder, American Government, 1932 edition, page 665, for an interesting comparison on this point.

How far does a newspaper's right extend to attack the motives or policies of a prominent person? Public officials, accused of bad government, sometimes say that we are knocking our city if we tell about their administration of affairs. Is that true? 

164. Guarantees of Security. As we use the word security here, we are thinking of the right of every citizen to enjoy life, health, and a good reputation. No one may by any unjust act deprive a citizen of such rights. If any limitation on his rights is attempted, he may ask the state to protect him. Our constitutions even give considerable attention to the rights of persons accused of crime.

Remember that not everybody accused of crime is guilty. 

Sometimes there are suspicious circumstances which cause the arrest of an innocent person. Besides, even a criminal should not be punished for something he did not do. An anarchist may be

THE RIGHT TO BEAR ARMS 

For the safety of the community, a person may be required to obtain a permit like this, even though this right is guaranteed in the Constitution. accused of murder; but however much we may dislike an anarchist, we have no right to convict him of murder unless he killed somebody.

When actually seen committing a crime, a person may be arrested at once; but a warrant must be issued by some judge or similar officer to authorize a policeman, sheriff, or 66 constable to enter a house and seize persons or things there. If 

Howard Smith is suspected of having in his home goods smuggled into the country contrary to our tariff law, before Smith can be arrested or the goods seized some official has to swear out a warrant specifying the goods and accusing Smith of having them. 

Now suppose the arrest has been made. A person is not yet legally guilty, unless he admits it. While waiting for trial, the accused may be let out on bail.¹ (In murder cases that privilege is not usually granted.) When the trial is held, the accused person has the right to know just what the charge is, to call witnesses in his behalf and compel them to testify, and is not required to testify against himself. If the accused person is too poor to hire a lawyer, the court will appoint somebody to help him. If he is found guilty, no fines or punishments beyond those corresponding to the offense committed may be imposed. 

It would be unreasonable, for instance, to give as severe a sentence to a person who stole a loaf of bread as to one who held up a bank. 

1 Bail is a pledge of money by persons who promise that if the accused does not appear for trial when he is supposed to, the money will be forfeited to the government. The amount varies with the seriousness of the crime of which a person is accused.

Our Rights and Duties as Good Citizens 345 

Do you ask why we are so careful to give an accused person all these means of protection? Long ago when these features of the 

Bill of Rights were being developed in England, the king himself was sometimes wicked; if the king was not, his officers were not always what they should be. So all these guarantees were necessary dare we say it? - perhaps they still are for the same reason. Some of these rights were won only after bitter struggles. 

Some cheap and tricky lawyers will help their clients to take advantage of provisions meant only to protect the innocent, and thus sometimes prevent a guilty person from receiving what he deserves. Nevertheless, if somebody stole a diamond ring and put it in your desk, where it was later found, you might be very glad that all these guarantees for the benefit of persons accused of crime are written in our constitutions. 

How many of the rights of accused persons mentioned above are likely to be abused? Are there any which are no longer necessary y? 

As a further means of security to a person accused of crime, we have the writ of habeas corpus. This also has come down to us from England. If a person is kept in jail and not given a prompt trial, his friends or his lawyer may go to court and obtain a "writ " which will command the jailer to present the accused person to the court so that he may be tried or released on bail. In time of war or other serious disturbance, it is sometimes necessary to keep men under guard who are suspected of disloyalty or other major offenses, even though the government does not have positive proof against them. Under such conditions the writ of habeas. corpus may be suspended for a while. 

165. Guarantees of Property. A third guarantee which we expect from our governments concerns private property. Any citizen has the right to own and use property of any kind as long as his use of it does not injure other people or the community. 

Not even the government may take private property for its own use without paying the owner its value. 

Perhaps some might say that when a person is compelled to pay taxes, the government is really taking some of his property. But since the government gives protection to the property a citizen owns, and makes it possible for him to enjoy comforts and con-

veniences, it has the right to demand that the citizen pay for this service. The government is not taking one's property by taxing, but simply collecting a payment for services rendered. 

By property we mean any material thing that one owns. Can a government take away any of one's other possessions such as education, religion, friendship, ideals? Are these in your opinion more or less important than property?

So then, personal liberty, personal security, private property all are assured by the government to citizens who use them in the right way. These privileges should be used with full respect for the rights of others whose privileges are just as complete as our own. This very limit helps to make any community a decent place. 

If you carry a revolver on the street, you may be arrested. Would such an arrest be a denial of your rights as a citizen? Have you known of any cases when people were arrested for holding a meeting or taking part in one? Would there seem to be any interference with their liberty in those cases? Does the citizen exist for the benefit of the government, or the government for the benefit of the citizen? Is a person a good citizen who insists on the full exercise of his rights, when he knows that such exercise is going to embarrass his government? Our constitutions forbid cruel and unusual punishments. What do you think of electrocution in this connection?

Point out occasions when absolute freedom of speech, press, or assemblage would be distinctly harmful. Point out occasions when certain people might wish to restrict unfairly the exercise of those freedoms. 

166. How Our Courts Protect Our Rights. The cases brought into a court may be grouped into two classes criminal and civil. 

Criminal cases in which persons are accused of breaking laws may run all the way from stealing a loaf of bread to murdering a man. Civil suits in which one person or corporation claims to have been wrongly treated by another are usually a claim for money damages." In criminal cases the courts must decide whether the accused person has committed the crime with which he is charged, and if he has, whether he shall be given the extreme penalty provided by the law, or some lesser sentence. In civil suits the person who brings the charge is known as the plaintiff, and the one who is sued is the defendant. The jury must decide 66

Our Rights and Duties as Good Citizens 347 

which of the two parties is in the right; and if it decides in favor of the plaintiff, it determines how much the defendant must pay him by way of damages.

Our criminal courts protect the whole community from dangerous citizens or selfish law-breakers. Our civil courts make it possible for persons who have been unfairly treated to have that unfairness corrected. To understand the work of our courts, let us follow, step by step, the process connected with the trial of ordinary cases at law. 

CRIMINAL CASE 

1. Arrest. An arrest is made by an officer (1) who sees a person committing a crime, or finds him under suspicious circumstances, or (2) who possesses a warrant calling for the arrest. In the latter case some person must have made a complaint telling why the accused should be arrested. 

2. Hearing. The accused person is brought before a magistrate or alderman the or justice of peace. This official hears a statement of the charges against the accused, and if the case seems to warrant it, holds him for trial in the proper court. 

If the accused has friends who will go bail for him, he may be released until time for his trial.

AN INDICTMENT 

This is the form in which a grand jury brings in its indictment of a person accused of crime. 

The name of the accused person in this indictment is fictitious. twenty-three persons, is summoned whenever a session of criminal court is to be held. The district attorney lays before it the principal features of the evidence against the accused. If twelve of the jury

think the accused ought to be brought to trial, they bring in a true bill and draw up an indictment, or formal charge, against him. If they think there is no possibility of his guilt, the accused is set free. 

4. Trial. A petit jury of twelve men is drawn for each case. 

Witnesses are presented by the prosecuting officer in behalf of the State or Commonwealth and by the attorney for the defendant. 

Ewing Galloway

A SCENE IN SUPERIOR COURT 

This happens to be in San Francisco, but many other court rooms are similarly arranged. Notice the place of the judge, the witness, the clerk, the court reporter, and the jury. Both men and women serve on juries in California and in a number of other states. At the long table in front are the lawyers and their clients. 

When the

These witnesses tell what they know about the case. testimony is finished, each attorney delivers a closing speech for his side. The presiding judge then charges the jury, telling them the laws that apply to the case and the facts they ought to keep in mind in reaching their conclusions. The judge does not as a rule tell them his own opinion about the guilt of the accused. To determine whether he is guilty or innocent is particularly the work of the jury. 

5. Verdict. The jury retires to a separate room to talk over the case. If the twelve agree that the accused is guilty or not guilty, they so report to the court. If, after discussion, they conclude that they cannot possibly come to an agreement, they report that fact also. When they have finished their work on the case, they are discharged. Then they either go home or wait until they may be drawn to hear some other case.

Our Rights and Duties as Good Citizens 349 

6. Sentence. If the jury says "guilty," the judge imposes sentence. Whether it is a fine or imprisonment or both, depends on the nature of the case and the opinion of the judge as to what is warranted in this particular instance. Sometimes the law specifies definitely what the sentence shall be. In other cases, the judge is allowed considerable discretion concerning the amount of fine or the length of the term in prison. It becomes the duty of the sheriff to see that the sentence is carried out. (If the jury says "not guilty," the accused person is discharged and cannot be tried again for the same offense. If the jury disagrees, the case may be tried over later with a different jury.) 

7. Appeal. If the convicted person thinks that something was done improperly in the course of the trial, or the judge was mistaken in some ruling, or the law was incorrectly applied in some way, he may appeal. If the higher court decides against him, he must serve his sentence.

CIVIL SUIT 1. Complaint. The plaintiff's lawyer files a statement with an officer of the county court, setting forth the reasons why he thinks the defendant has done wrong and ought to pay money damages because of this wrong. The court official then notifies the defendant. 

2. Answer. The defendant's attorney files with the court a statement either admitting the truth of the charges or denying them. 

In the rare cases when the defendant admits the charges, judgment is entered against him at once. 

3. Docket. The case is placed on the court's docket, or list of cases to be heard when it meets. 

4. This step is much like the corresponding step in the trial of a criminal case, except that the district attorney has no connection with it. Private citizens and not the state or commonwealth are the prosecutors in a civil suit. In some states the jury trying such a case is called a traverse jury. 

5. Finding. The jury decides in favor of the plaintiff or the defendant. If it finds in favor of the plaintiff, it will also decide how much damages the defendant shall pay. Sometimes, when it thinks that no great wrong has been committed, it will simply require one side or the other to pay the costs of the case, or may even divide the costs between the two sides. 

6. Judgment. If the defendant has been ordered to pay damages, the judgment will be entered on the records of the court and the authority of the court will be used, if necessary, to make certain that the judgment is carried out. 

7. Appeal. Appeal may be taken in civil suits the same as in criminal cases.

Quite likely some of the details of the processes described here may not be exactly like those followed in your state. Find out just what the proceedings are in your own state.

Courts in this country have been criticized even by such famous jurists as Chief Justice Taft for their slowness and uncertainty in carrying on their work. Months and even years sometimes pass

This is a building in which the people of New Orleans take great pride. It bears this inscription: "The impartial administration of justice is the foundation of law." before cases are brought to trial. In that time important witnesses may disappear, and all the good effect that might come from a speedy trial be lost. Our best lawyers are trying to rectify this discreditable reputation. 

Give some specific examples of rights under each of the three general heads. Did you mention any which might not have been thought of when the first ten amendments were attached to our national Constitution? 

167. Duties of Good Citizenship. 

It has been said that every right carries with it a duty. If every citizen realized the twosided nature of citizenship, we should be much better off than we are today. Too many people talk too much about their rights and forget the corresponding duties.

Our Rights and Duties as Good Citizens 351 

For each right in the list of examples which you have just given mention a corresponding duty. Why do you suppose our constitutions do not include lists of duties as well as rights? 

Two of our most fundamental duties are obedience to lawful authority and service to our government and fellow citizens. 

If some citizens refuse to respect the laws which they dislike, why couldn't other citizens do the same? If we make such allowances for our likes and dislikes, you can be assured that very few laws will be generally respected. If some one is willing to inconvenience himself in being kind to you, you have a right to feel that he thinks something of you. So if one has a proper regard for his government, he will expect to render service willingly though at times it may be inconvenient.

Is it ever a citizen's right or duty to take the law into his own hands? Should a public official enforce some laws, and neglect to enforce others? 

One of the fondest 

168. Duties of the Good Citizen in War. hopes that any American might have for his country should be that it would never have to engage in another war. Yet when we think how suddenly the World War broke upon the peoples of the earth, we have to admit that there may still be nations who will break pledges and use violent means to get what they want. We must recognize the obligations that might arise if war should again come upon us.

Every male citizen from the age of eighteen to forty-five may be called upon for military training, if necessary. This period we sometimes speak of as the militia age. In a short war, a nation usually depends upon its regular army and those who are willing to volunteer for service. But in a war as great and serious as our 

Civil War or the World War, even our own government drafts men and compels them to fight.

In wartime everybody is called upon for financial sacrifice. 

Ordinary taxes are increased, and some others are added. Perhaps we should not think of such things as simply occasions for sacrifice; during the World War, when our people were constantly being urged to buy war savings stamps and bonds, some people were induced to save who never would have done so otherwise. Women and girls not only engaged in Red Cross work and the like, but

went into some occupations which usually are left for men and boys.

In time of war it is perhaps easier than at any other time to display the virtue of loyalty to be thrilled by war songs and appeals to one's patriotism. We are loyal to something we love 

Courtesy Vermont Marble Company THE TOMB OF THE UNKNOWN SOLDIER 

Here is buried one who gave his life for his country, though the world will never know his name. Visitors to Arlington National Cemetery look upon this place with reverence, just as visitors do who find similar memorials in Westminster Abbey and under the Arch of Triumph. or honor, and surely we Yet ought to have such feelings toward our country. loyalty may call for selfdenial, and to those who go into the camps and trenches it may mean the greatest sacrifice.

Such 

Unfortunately some are disloyal even in war. people we call traitors.

Our Constitution has limited rigidly the number of offenses that can be termed treason. Only making war upon the United States and giving assistance to the enemies of the country can be so considered. Moreover, it is also required that a person shall not be convicted of this great crime unless he admits it in court, or two persons have witnessed the treasonable act. Congress may determine the punishment for treason - and it usually is death. But our Constitution provides that no penalty inflicted on a traitor shall affect the rights of any one else. 

It is sometimes difficult to determine whether a person's unwillingness to fight in time of war is due to disloyalty, cowardice, or conscience. We should respect the feelings of a man or woman who honestly believes that all war is wrong, but certainly a refusal to support one's country in war should rest on only the soundest and most honest convictions.

A MEMORIAL DAY PARADE 

The fast-thinning ranks of G. A. R. veterans march in honor of their comrades in the annual Memorial Day parade. zenship because they would not take an oath to bear arms for the United States. Does such a requirement appear justifiable? Some thoroughly sincere citizens have urged that real lovers of peace should make a vow never to take part in any war. Do you believe such a vow would be patriotic or wise? We have recognized the necessity of drafting people for military service in time of war. Why do we not do it in time of peace, so that we may have trained soldiers always ready, as some countries do? 

169. Duties of a Good Citizen in Peace. Loyalty in peace is just as necessary as in war, for peace is, or ought to be, the natural condition for every country. There are many forms of loyalty in peace. Paying taxes, for instance, never carries with it any pleasant thoughts; yet it must be done if the government is to endure. Very few people are asked to pay any more in taxes than they receive in benefits from the government. 

Another point at which some of our people fail to measure up is the matter of jury service. When we look at some of the juries that try cases involving many dollars' worth of property and the liberty and perhaps the life of some citizens, we wonder whether anybody should be asked to trust his life and property to the

decision of such a group. 

Yet if juries are sometimes not intelligent or honest, it may be because honest and intelligent people so often try to get out of jury service. As long as we use juries to reach decisions in our courts, they ought to be made up of our best citizens rather than those who are less fit. 

How are persons chosen for jury service in your state or county?

Are there any classes of people who are excused from jury service? 

If so, what may be the reasons for excusing them? Is there any reason why women should be exempted from service on juries? (The custom of states differs somewhat on this point.) Can you think of any fairer way of getting decisions in court than through juries? Why do you suppose unanimous decisions are required of juries in criminal cases? Is this requirement ever abused?

Besides, one can aid the authorities when they are trying to enforce a law. A person is not a tell-tale if he reports to the authorities occasions when laws are repeatedly broken. He is helping to make his government count for more and cause its authority to be respected by everybody. 

Will anybody deny that voting is another citizenship duty?

Indeed, it is both a duty and a privilege, for the state does not give this privilege to every one. It may be inconvenient to go to the polling place two or three times a year to register or vote; but bad citizens will not usually overlook this matter, nor will the politician who uses bad citizens to serve his own ends. Good citizens must be even more vigilant, or else put up with the kind of government of which they may be constantly complaining. 

If more of our good citizens, too, were willing to serve on registration boards and election boards, we might have more intelligent service in the management of our elections. 

Is it the duty of a good citizen to hold office if he is asked to do so? Should a person who offers himself as a candidate for office be looked upon as a good citizen or as a self-seeker? A lawyer of high ability, who was earning $20,000 a year at his practice, was asked to accept an appointment as judge, at $10,000 a year. He said he could not afford to accept the appointment. What do you think of his decision from the standpoint of citizenship?

There are other duties of a good citizen which we shall not discuss here because we are considering chiefly the citizen's rela-

Our Rights and Duties as Good Citizens 355 

tion to his government. At least these three are extremely important, however: 

1. Practice the Golden Rule in all our associations with our fellow citizens. 

2. Do our best to earn an honest living for ourselves and those who are dependent upon us. 

3. Make the best use of our talents and opportunities. 

Make a list of fifteen Americans who had no part in any war which our country has fought, but who have nevertheless shown themselves worthy of high regard as good citizens. 

170. The Importance of Coöperation in Good Citizenship. 

We must get teamwork, even if we have to force people to coöperate, though it is far better if coöperation comes willingly. Some are too ignorant to appreciate the advantage in a new way of doing things, and some are narrow-minded or suspicious. Since such people are often sincere, they may need to be educated, though it may take time.

Some are afraid their selfish interests are going to be affected in some way. Others have no use for any proposition if it is put forward by somebody of a different party or religious denomination. Often we cannot tell why we are Republicans or Democrats, or why we belong to one church rather than another; but just let somebody of a different belief or creed propose something, and we are immediately "agin it." President Coolidge was said to have remarked about a certain Senator, on being told that he did not believe in the Bible, "I guess he did not write it." Then there are others who hopelessly assert, "It can't be done." 

To deal with these varied objectors takes tact and judgment. 

It usually does no good for one to lose his temper and call names. 

One should avoid antagonizing people when there is no gain in doing so. At the same time, one who is really in earnest in an undertaking must not be surprised at opposition. Grover Cleveland's friends once proclaimed, "We love him for the enemies he has made." Sometimes we may be proud to be opposed by certain people and certain influences. 

If you wish to secure a vacant lot near your ground, what sort of opposition may you meet? school for a play- 

What if you propose to have a new school building erected? Is coöperation any easier now than in pioneer days?

Coöperation may sometimes require yielding a part of one's rights or privileges in order to accomplish something for the good of the whole neighborhood. Again, it may consist in going along with a movement that does not exactly suit us rather than quitting the cause and letting the whole movement fail. Alexander 

Courtesy Cleveland Chamber of Commerce

CLEVELAND'S TERMINAL TOWER GROUP 

The railway passenger terminal underneath this structure is said to be one of the most modern in the world. Several other notable buildings are included in the picture. In how many ways may coöperation have been needed before these buildings could go up?

Hamilton was not satisfied with the Constitution of the United States as the convention drew it up, but he felt that it was the best that could be secured at that time and gave it his hearty support. Without his help it probably would have been defeated. 

Coöperation may also consist in offering helpful suggestions at the right time, if we are sure that something can be done in a better way than has been proposed. Some of the most disgusting

Our Rights and Duties as Good Citizens 357 

people are those who enjoy saying, "I told you so," but who did not say anything at the time their advice would have been helpful. 

Make a list of ten occasions when you have observed coöperation. 

What caused it, in each case? When was it good and when bad?

Illustrate coöperation, or the lack of it, from your own school life. 

If you fail to prepare your assignment for class or fail to do something else, is any harm done, providing that everybody else did what he ought? 

171. Patriotism and Good Citizenship. - Patriotism has been defined as loyalty to one's country. Do we prove our patriotism by applauding when we see the flag in a movie or even by learning the Constitution? One can pass a high examination on the contents of a civics textbook, but has he those ideals and purposes in his heart which will cause him to be honest, courageous, and faithful in private life or public service?

How do we observe our national holidays - Memorial Day, the 

Fourth of July, Armistice Day? Are they to the average person anything but one more holiday? a war. 

Some think they are patriotic when they brag about the United States. It is the richest of all nations, they say it never lost 

Are such things evidence of true greatness on the part of a nation? Should we not be more proud of our opportunities to use our wealth and strength for the good of all mankind? 

That would be real patriotism!

A famous editor, Mr. Oakes, was greatly distressed by what he called "the bad citizenship of good citizens." Perhaps they fail to vote; perhaps they refuse to keep informed about important public questions. Patriotism means everyday service; it means keeping out of our minds motives and temptations to be selfish or disregard the authority of our country. True patriotism will display itself in sacrifice when there is call for it. It will always try to face the facts and know the truth. 

Can one practice patriotism in school life? How can a business man be patriotic in time of war without serving in an army? How can an editor of a newspaper show his patriotism? Mention any act that you have read or heard about recently which you look upon as an example of patriotism. 

172. The Responsibilities of a Young Citizen. Have you been thinking that you are not yet twenty-one and cannot vote or that

you are not even eighteen and would not be allowed to render military service? Have you asked yourself, "What does all this have to do with me? Is there anything that young citizens can do to show their loyalty to their government? 

Certainly there are many ways for young citizens to prove their loyalty. School is both a big part of your actual life today and a preparation for future usefulness. Our community and state spend on the average between $1100 and $1200 for each of us who follows through a twelve-year course in the public schools. If your parents send you to private school, it will cost them much more than that. Is that money wasted? Will your community have reason to regret spending so much on you? You can be faithful to the calls that are made upon you for learning and for service. If you do not take advantage of these opportunities now, the chances are that when you grow up and are called upon for bigger and better service, you will not be ready.

Does the rendering of a daily salute to the flag help you to be patriotic? In how many ways may we show respect for the "Stars and Stripes"? 99 In old Athens a young man's "coming of age was made a notable ceremony. Young men all took the "Ephebic Oath," which is quoted at the beginning of this chapter. It applies just as truly in these days as it did then. You will have opportunities to participate in many movements for the improvement of your community. You can form the habit of being interested in what is going on in your community, in your state, in Washington, or all over the world. 66 Let us, every one, boys and girls alike, resolve that the United States of America is never going to lack good citizens. As the 

Father of His Country " once said, "Let us raise a standard to which the wise and honest may repair." We may be surprised to find the number who will be willing to join us in an unending good-citizenship campaign. Let us be faithful and true patriots from day to day, so we may be ready to serve our country in whatever way she may need us.

Are the rights, benefits, and obligations of citizenship any different in the case of girls from what they are in the case of boys? Quote the Boy Scout oath as a list of qualities a good citizen should possess.

Our Rights and Duties as Good Citizens 359 

Do you think of any other qualities that should be added to that list? Read The Makers of the Flag, by Franklin K. Lane, as an illustration of patriotic service which we sometimes do not recognize. 

Make a list of fifteen people of prominence whom you consider good citizens and be able to tell why you consider them so. Make a list of fifteen qualities which seem to you particularly important for a good citizen to possess. Be able to give examples of ways in which one may display those qualities. 

Good American citizens find their rights set down in bills of rights and in constitutions and in national and state laws, are guaranteed liberty, limited only by the liberty of others and the general welfare, are guaranteed security from tyranny and wrong, even against their government, are guaranteed the right to use property in any way not harmful to the public good, have the benefit of definite procedure in court to determine questions of guilt or innocence, right or wrong, have duties corresponding to all their rights, loyalty to their country in war, service to their country in peace, coöperation with other good citizens in accomplishing worthy ends, patriotism in the truest sense, have the opportunity, whatever their age, to show their loyalty and good will.

INTERESTING PROJECTS YOU MAY LIKE TO UNDERTAKE 

1. Draw up a list of rules for the proper use of the United States flag. 

Supplement it with drawings showing the flag in proper position. 

2. Cartoons illustrating good and bad citizenship in connection with voting, tax-paying, or some other duty. 

3. Send a representative committee of the class to visit a trial in court and report on its observations. Try to have them obtain full information about every stage of the proceedings followed in your own local courts. 

4. Hold a mock trial, conducting it as nearly as possible as it would be carried on in a real court. Try to avoid making the case merely funny. 

Change the form of oath given to witnesses, if necessary, so that no person will be asked to tell "the whole truth" unless he is actually going to do so.
